[1] The Hacker News - Drupal Warns of New Highly Critical Site Takeover Vulnerability Under Active Exploitation
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Thu, 26 Nov 2026 12:32:34 +0530
Link: https://thehackernews.com/2026/11/drupal-warns-of-new-highly-critical.html

[TAM METİN - 345 kelime]
Drupal has warned of a new "highly critical" security flaw impacting Drupal core that it said has been exploited in the wild.

The vulnerability, tracked as CVE-2026-136716 (CVSS score: N/A), has been described as a case of improper checking for unusual or exceptional conditions. It also relates to CVE-2026-132165 aka Fail2Install in that it addresses remaining vulnerable installation states that could still be abused to facilitate site takeover even after the fix.

CVE-2026-132165 "recently fixed several vulnerabilities within the Drupal core installer," Drupal maintainers said. "However, certain sites in unusual or incomplete install states remain vulnerable to site takeover following that advisory, including production sites."

"It is not apparent in the user interface if a site is in the affected vulnerable state, and sites that are already compromised may no longer be in the vulnerable state."

Drupal acknowledged that the vulnerability is being exploited in the wild, but emphasized that site owners who have already secured the installer ("install.php") are not impacted.

Specifically, any website that left its installer remotely accessible following the fix for CVE-2026-132165 may have been compromised. Drupal is urging site administrators to take the following actions -

Check site logs for unauthorized access, including unexpected changes to or by the user 1 account

Ensure the user 1 email address is set to under their control, and consider resetting user 1's password.

The issue has been remediated in the following versions -

Drupal 11.4.x - Update to Drupal 11.4.14

Drupal 11.3.x - Update to Drupal 11.3.23

Drupal 10.6.x - Update to Drupal 10.6.23

Drupal 11.0.x to 11.2.x - No fix (End-of-life)

Drupal 10.5.x and below - No fix (End-of-life)

After updating to the latest supported versions, it's recommended to run "update.php" to fix existing production sites in this state and secure the Drupal installer on all new and existing sites.

According to statistics shared by StepSecurity, there are about 55,800 internet-facing Drupal sites as of November 26, 2026, out of which 31,500 services are running branches that are vulnerable to CVE-2026-132165 and roughly 15,000 are on the end-of-life 8 and 9 branches.

[2] The Hacker News - Hackers Exploit 10 Vulnerabilities Across Mitel, Juniper, Vates, and Danfoss Products
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Thu, 26 Nov 2026 12:14:46 +0530
Link: https://thehackernews.com/2026/11/hackers-exploit-10-vulnerabilities.html

[TAM METİN - 958 kelime]
The U.S. Cybersecurity and Infrastructure Security Agency (CISA) on Wednesday added 10 security flaws to its Known Exploited Vulnerabilities (KEV) catalog, including five vulnerabilities impacting Mitel MiCollab that were disclosed last week.

The Mitel MiCollab vulnerabilities are listed below -

CVE-2026-133275 (CVSS score: 5.3) - An arbitrary file upload vulnerability in the Audio, Web and Video Conferencing (AWV) Component of MiCollab that could allow an attacker to upload arbitrary files to the target system.

CVE-2026-133303 (CVSS score: 9.0) - An XML injection vulnerability in the Audio, Web and Video Conferencing (AWV) component of MiCollab that could allow an attacker to execute arbitrary commands within the context of the system.

CVE-2026-133311 (CVSS score: 5.9) - An unauthorized access vulnerability in the Audio, Web, and Video Conferencing (AWV) Component of MiCollab that could allow an attacker to obtain sensitive information.

CVE-2026-133323 (CVSS score: 10.0) - A command injection vulnerability in the Audio, Web, and Video Conferencing (AWV) Component of MiCollab that could allow an attacker to execute arbitrary commands within the context of the system.

CVE-2026-133324 (CVSS score: 5.3) - An authentication bypass vulnerability in the Audio, Web, and Video Conferencing (AWV) Component of MiCollab that could allow an attacker to bypass authentication controls and perform unauthorized actions within the context of the application.

The issues affect MiCollab versions 10.3 and earlier. "Mitel provided a patch available for release 10.3 (10.3.0.18) that addresses these vulnerabilities," the company said in an advisory published November 16, 2026. "Upgrade to this release and apply the provided patch, or upgrade to a later release."

Customers who are unable to apply the updates are recommended to mitigate the risk by restricting external access to the AWV component. As of writing, there are currently no details on how the vulnerabilities are being exploited in the wild.

Also added to the KEV catalog are the following vulnerabilities -

CVE-2026-133469 (CVSS score: 9.8) - An improper validation of specified type of input vulnerability in Vates Xen Orchestra that could allow an unauthenticated attacker to bypass authentication and perform actions with administrative privileges via a specially crafted authentication token containing an empty JSON object.

CVE-2026-103406 (CVSS score: 9.8) - An operating system command injection vulnerability in Hewlett Packard Enterprise (HPE) Juniper Session Smart Conductor and Session Smart Router that could allow a remote attacker to execute arbitrary commands as root via a specially crafted salt minion ID.

The addition of CVE-2026-133469 follows an advisory from Vates VMS that the vulnerability, also tracked as VSA-2026-062, has been exploited by a threat actor from the following IP address and TOR domain -

91.211.251[.]112:8001

ustar3rqxgnskzgnwkgpofgtvxevm6cx3lxdbwt4mndtveqyaohid[.]onion:3333

"At least one instance of malicious exploitation of CVE-2026-103406 has been reported to the Juniper SIRT," Juniper Networks said in an advisory. "Customers are encouraged to upgrade to a fixed release as soon as possible and in the meantime take steps to mitigate this vulnerability."

The remaining three security flaws relate to Danfoss AK-SM 800A Series refrigeration controllers that could lead to authentication bypass and arbitrary command execution -

CVE-2023-25914 (CVSS score: 8.8) - A path traversal vulnerability that could allow an attacker to access and read underlying server system files via the XML interface.

CVE-2023-25915 (CVSS score: 9.9) - An improper input validation vulnerability that could allow a remote attacker to run arbitrary commands on the affected system.

CVE-2025-41450 (CVSS score: 8.5) - An improper authentication vulnerability that could allow an attacker to generate a web report exposing sensitive information, including internal IP addresses, usernames, and store names.

Although Danfoss makes no mention of the flaws being exploited, it's likely that they were put to use as part of high-impact attacks targeting supermarket chains across Europe in recent weeks. France has attributed the October 2026 attacks on its retail sites to Shahid Kaveh (aka Cyber Aveng3rs and Cyber Isnaad Front), an Iranian cyber unit affiliated with the Islamic Revolutionary Guard Corps (IRGC).

In light of active exploitation, Federal Civilian Executive Branch (FCEB) agencies are being advised to apply the necessary fixes for all the flaws, except CVE-2026-133311 and CVE-2025-41450, by November 28, 2026. The remaining two vulnerabilities must be addressed by December 9, 2026.

CISA Warns of New Unpatched Danfoss AK-SM 8xxA Series Flaw

The development comes as CISA has warned of an unpatched critical flaw in Danfoss AK-SM 8xxA Series (CVE-2026-129713, CVSS score: 9.8 / 9.3) controllers that could be exploited to execute arbitrary code with webserver privileges.

"A stack-based buffer overflow in an unauthenticated CGI handler of the Danfoss AK-SM 8xxA Series controller allows remote attackers to send specially crafted HTTP/S POST requests, leading to memory corruption, webserver restart and hypothetically potential arbitrary code execution with webserver privileges," CISA said. "Authentication is not required to exploit this vulnerability."

Danfoss, which has issued an alert of its own, said the security flaw impacts versions 5.4.0 and earlier. A fix for the vulnerability is expected to be addressed in an upcoming software version (5.5.x). It also said it will remove the option that allows customers to disable "Strict mode" in the product.

Until the fixes are available, system administrators are advised to take the following actions -

Make sure remote access to the units is possible only through a secure VPN or in general over a protected network

Configure the units to Strict mode, which ensures higher security levels on the product

Recommend using strong passwords

CISA is also recommending that organizations minimize network exposure of such devices, place control system networks and remote devices behind firewalls, and isolate them from business networks.

"When remote access is required, use more secure methods, such as Virtual Private Networks (VPNs), recognizing VPNs may have vulnerabilities and should be updated to the most current version available," CISA added. "Also recognize VPN is only as secure as the connected devices."

[3] The Hacker News - 1Password Hacked, Customer Data and Source Code Stolen in GitHub Account Breach
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Thu, 26 Nov 2026 09:50:22 +0530
Link: https://thehackernews.com/2026/11/1password-hacked-customer-data-and.html

[TAM METİN - 428 kelime]
Popular password management software 1Password on Wednesday disclosed a security incident involving its backend systems that led to the unauthorized access of some customer information, but emphasized that customer password vaults and other secrets were not accessed.

"While we took immediate steps to contain the incident, the attacker gained access to some of our backend systems and accessed a limited amount of customer account information from a subset of our customers, including names and email addresses," the Toronto-based company said.

It noted that no customer vault content, including secrets, passwords, and other vault items, account recovery keys, or the keys that secure them, was accessed or compromised as a result of the breach. It also stated that customers do not need to take any action, but cautioned them to stay vigilant against unexpected phishing attempts.

As part of its efforts to remediate the incident, 1Password has disabled the affected GitHub account, revoked the stolen token, hardened GitHub, CI/CD, and endpoint controls, restored clean versions of the affected service, and rotated internal credentials. It has also lengthened the codes used in invitation and recovery emails from six digits to ten characters as a precautionary measure.

According to the company, the incident dates back to November 19, 2026, when it began investigating suspicious activity after an unknown threat actor gained access to an employee's corporate GitHub account.

The attacker is said to have weaponized the account to access source code and push malicious code to its backend services, resulting in the download of administrative data related to its customers, including -

Account and user identifiers, such as account creation and last sign-in times

Secure Remote Password protocol parameters

Tokens present in certain emailed links for invitations, provisioning, and account recovery

Non-secret internal metadata about the account, such as version numeric identifiers, file numeric identifiers

1Password also noted that the tokens embedded in the links cannot be used on their own to gain access to customer accounts, as doing so requires either single sign-on (SSO) authentication or a six-digit verification code sent to the user's registered email address. However, it noted that a small number of users were affected and have been individually notified.

"The attacker also copied source code from private repositories and gained access to our CI/CD system," 1Password said. "Our source code is not customer data, and contains no user secrets. There is no impact to 1Password’s services, availability, or product security."

"Your vault secrets are encrypted with keys that only you hold, so we can’t access them, and neither can an attacker who gets into our servers."

[4] The Hacker News - 13 Malicious NuGet Packages Turn Fake .NET Job Assessments Into Malware Delivery Traps
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Thu, 26 Nov 2026 09:18:52 +0530
Link: https://thehackernews.com/2026/11/13-malicious-nuget-packages-turn-fake.html

[TAM METİN - 1070 kelime]
Cybersecurity researchers have discovered 13 malicious NuGet packages that are designed to target Windows .NET developers under the guise of a fake take-home job assessment.

"Most of the packages pose as WinUI 3 and WPF [Windows Presentation Foundation] button controls," Socket security researcher Kush Pandya said in a technical write-up. "In the versions we analyzed, building or running a project that uses them downloads files from GitHub, stages a DLL sideloading pair around the Microsoft On-Screen Keyboard (osk.exe), or downloads and runs an executable."

All the identified NuGet packages have been deleted from the gallery as of writing. The names of the packages are listed below -

VS_Builder.MSBuildAnalyzer

VS_Builder.WinUI3Controls

HappyOne.MSBuildAnalyzer

HappyOne.WinUI3Controls

WindowsAccentColorName

InitializeCustomButton

The first NuGet package, WinUI3IconButton, was uploaded to the registry on July 23, 2026, indicating that the campaign has been ongoing for quite some time without attracting attention. The last two uploads, HappyOne.MSBuildAnalyzer and HappyOne.WinUI3Controls, occurred on November 4, 2026, after both VS_Builder.MSBuildAnalyzer and VS_Builder.WinUI3Controls were removed by NuGet on November 2, 2026.

The starting point is a GitHub repository ("stevenleedig-ux/WinUI3_Home_Assessment") created on November 3, 2026. It features a set of assessment tasks revolving around enhancing a FileButton control and exception handling that candidates must implement within a span of three to four hours. It's unclear how targets are led to these decoy projects.

The README file also permits the test participants to use artificial intelligence (AI) development assistants like GitHub Copilot, ChatGPT, or Claude, but discourages them from copying and pasting the entire project into the tool and generating the solution without "understanding it."

Present within the assessment repository is a "WinUI3_Home_Assessment.csproj" file that references VS_Builder.MSBuildAnalyzer version 1.2.7 and VS_Builder.WinUI3Controls version 1.0.7.

"VS_Builder.MSBuildAnalyzer, later republished as HappyOne.MSBuildAnalyzer, ships an MSBuild targets file, build/VS_Builder.MsBuildAnalyzer.targets, that runs a bundled DLL ['MSBuildAnalyzer.dll'] after every build," Pandya said. "In the version bundled with the lure, that DLL downloads a file from GitHub to %LOCALAPPDATA%\osk\OskSupport.dll and copies C:\Windows\System32\osk.exe next to it."

The VS_Builder.WinUI3Controls package then launches the copied binary via the FileButton control that "routes clicks to Windows files and folders," triggering the execution of the DLL via a known technique called DLL sideloading.

In a similar vein, creating a WUIButton control from the WinUI.Button package starts a background routine in "lib/net8.0-windows10.0.19041/WinUI.Button.dll" that downloads "avast.exe" from the "Carol-White-Super/cloud" repository via the GitHub API using an embedded access token, saves it as "%TEMP%\Launcher.exe," and runs it.

RevertButtonIcon and SetButtonColor "download a Windows executable to the user's temp folder and run it, and InitializeCustomButton starts the copy that SetButtonColor drops," Socket explained.

"Five more are tied to the set by dependency or by the lure: WinUI3IconButton (from version 1.1.4), StyledAppButton and StyledComponent depend on other packages in the set, WindowsAccentColorName is a dependency of SetButtonColor and InitializeCustomButton, and HappyOne.WinUI3Controls is the FileButton package the lure switched to."

Socket also said it identified two bogus repositories impersonating the axios JavaScript library -

jason-code-super/axios

stevenleedig-ux/axios-http (the same GitHub account hosting the assessment)

Both repositories have been flagged as copies of a malicious Node.js library named "axios-test-one" that impersonates the Axios HTTP client but contains functionality that enables code execution and shell access. The repositories serve a Windows executable named "lib/env/axios.csproj."

An analysis of their commit history has revealed several binaries between the two repositories, including -

A loader ("updater.exe" and "msedgewebview2.exe") that uses a GitHub repository ("James-White-Super/React_prj") as a two-way command and control (C2)

A PyInstaller program that functions as a Google Chrome profile stealer and uploads the information to GitHub ("gabsantosbus-star/EngineeringRepository"), and

A "WeapF" payload that reports the computer name, user name, time zone, and CPU model to an attacker-controlled Telegram bot

What's more, the GitHub account named "gabsantosbus-star" (aka Gabriel) has been found to have made the first commit in the two aforementioned fake Axios repositories, along with "figma-share/axios-http," a third bogus Axios repository.

"On November 13, 2026, gabsantosbus-star [...] published a GitHub release of 'Crypto Wallet Analyzer,' a single-file .NET app," Socket said. "The README presents the app as a first-stage client deliverable that inventories eight crypto wallet extensions in Chrome profiles and makes no network requests."

"The visible app does only that, but its decompiled MainWindow constructor also loads a native DLL bundled in the executable. The DLL starts a background thread when loaded, and closing the window only hides it, so the process keeps running."

The DLL in question is a new version of the loader that uses a different repository ("Best-Seller-Dev/dexTrader") for C2. The GitHub account is no longer accessible as of writing.

Further investigation has uncovered that the lure was advertised on a Web3 freelance marketplace called LaborX on November 7, 2026, under the same persona, with the threat actor posing as a "detail-oriented client looking to work with skilled freelancers on various projects."

The $8,500-10,000 job listing, which is still accessible, claims that they are looking for a C# WPF developer to work on their lightweight Windows .NET desktop application that "inventories crypto wallet software installed on a local PC, including browser wallet extensions and desktop wallet applications."

On their X profile, they describe themselves as a "blockchain and DeFi specialist" and a "cryptocurrency expert" based out of Miami, Florida. But a check of their "About this account" reveals that the profile is actually based in Korea. The account was created in July 2026.

The web of connections doesn't end there, for evidence indicates that the "axios-test-one" npm library was part of a broader collection of six packages that were published between July 14 and 23, 2026, by three npm accounts gsanbus, jasonsuperstar, and jasonsayman -

The use of the name "jasonsayman" is particularly significant as it clearly points to an attempt to pass off as "jasonsaayman," the legitimate GitHub account belonging to the developer and maintainer of Axios.

"Telemetry-metrics and telemetry-axios download a Windows executable from ThoSuperstarDev/axios-http and run it, fs-extra-core downloads one from jason-code-super/axios and saves it as msedgewebview2.exe, and the axios clones pull those packages in," Socket said.

What's notable here is that "ThoSuperstarDev" is the former GitHub account name of "figma-share," which, as it happens, also hosts a ClickFix-style page that shows a fake reCAPTCHA verification prompt and guides the visitor to copy and paste a security token into the Windows Run dialog to address a purported "Security Handshake Error."

The fraudulent job campaign has not been attributed to any named threat actor or group. However, Socket noted that the tradecraft shares similarities with other recruitment-themed campaigns orchestrated by North Korea and Iran.

(XXXXXXX, AÇIK - https://thehackernews.com/2026/11/13-malicious-nuget-packages-turn-fake.html, thehackernews.com, 26.11.2026)

[5] The Hacker News - Hackers Exploit Two GeoServer Flaws as Researchers Warn of Unauthenticated RCE Chains
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Wed, 25 Nov 2026 18:18:54 +0530
Link: https://thehackernews.com/2026/11/hackers-exploit-two-geoserver-flaws-as.html

[TAM METİN - 786 kelime]
Threat actors are actively exploiting two newly patched security flaws in GeoServer, according to watchTowr.

The vulnerabilities in question are CVE-2026-120705 and CVE-2026-129243, details of which are below -

CVE-2026-120705 (CVSS score: 8.6) - An arbitrary file read vulnerability that could enable an attacker to access local text files readable by the GeoServer process or fetch adversary-selected HTTP or HTTPS URLs by means of a single unauthenticated Web Feature Service (WFS) request.

CVE-2026-129243 (CVSS score: 8.8) - An incorrect authorization vulnerability that could allow an unauthenticated attacker to invoke OWS operations that are otherwise protected by service security rules.

Advisories for both the flaws were published on November 24, 2026, in the following versions -

CVE-2026-120705: org.geoserver:gs-wfs-core - 3.0.2, 2.28.6, and 2.27.7

CVE-2026-120705: org.geotools:gt-metadata - 35.2, 34.6, and 33.7

CVE-2026-129243: org.geoserver:gs-ows - 3.0.2, 2.28.6, and 2.27.7

"Attackers sent error-based exploit probes to validate that the server is vulnerable by creating and invoking stored queries," the preemptive exposure management firm said in an X post today.

Yordan Ganchev, principal threat intelligence specialist at watchTowr, said the vulnerabilities can allow attackers to bypass security restrictions, interact with internal services, retrieve stored secrets, forge authentication credentials, and deserialize and run code on affected systems.

"On November 25, we observed in-the-wild exploitation against our global honeypot network in the early morning hours leveraging CVE-2026-120705 and CVE-2026-129244," Ganchev said. "Threat actors from multiple IP addresses were observed validating vulnerable systems on the internet by creating stored queries on GeoServer machines, checking whether they were created, and deleting them immediately afterward."

Eye Security, which discovered and reported CVE-2026-120705, said the flaw can be combined with two other vulnerabilities in the same product to create an unauthenticated remote code execution chain -

CVE-2026-129245 (CVSS score: 9.2) - A use of a hard-coded signing key ("geoserver") vulnerability in the "remember me" functionality that could be exploited to forge administrator authentication. (Fixed in org.geoserver:gs-main versions 3.0.2, 2.28.6, and 2.27.7)

CVE-2026-120704 (CVSS score: 7.2) - An improper control of the generation of code vulnerability that could allow an administrator, or anyone with access to an administrator session, to execute arbitrary code as the user running GeoServer. (Fixed in org.geoserver.security:gs-sec-jdbc versions 3.0.2, 2.28.6, and 2.27.7)

In a hypothetical attack chain, a bad actor can follow the steps below -

Exploit CVE-2026-120705 to access "users.xml," a file containing GeoServer usernames and password hashes, and obtain the password representation along with the static signing key extracted from the security configuration ("security/config.xml").

Exploit CVE-2026-120704 to abuse an administrator-controlled JDBC configuration to execute code from an uploaded native library on both Linux and Windows versions of GeoServer.

According to the project maintainers, CVE-2026-129245 is also part of a five-step unauthenticated remote command execution chain that involves -

CVE-2026-129244 (CVSS score: 6.9) - WFS stored query exception discloses data directory path

CVE-2026-129243 (CVSS score: 8.8) - Service security rules bypassed with a prefixed service name such as x/WFS

CVE-2026-120705 (CVSS score: 8.6) - Unauthenticated WFS GetFeatureById XML injection

CVE-2026-129245 (CVSS score: 9.2) - Predictable "remember me" key allows administrator session forgery

CVE-2026-129242 (CVSS score: 8.6) - Deserialization in JDBC user and role services allows administrator code execution

"An attacker who obtains an administrator password digest, through any file disclosure or a copy of the data directory, can log in as that administrator," GeoServer maintainers said. "Changing the key in security/config.xml does not help when the same file disclosure also reveals the key."

Jonah Burgess, a Senior Security Researcher at Rapid7, who goes by the online alias CryptoCat has been acknowledged for independently discovering and reporting the five-step unauthenticated remote command execution chain on August 2, 2026.

"Prefixing the Web Feature Service (WFS) name, as in x/WFS, slips past the admin-only rule for stored queries. GeoServer turns escaped text in a saved query into live xi:include elements, which leaks the files holding the remember-me key and the admin password digest," the researcher said.

In light of active exploitation of two of the flaws, users are advised to update to GeoServer versions 2.27.7, 2.28.6, and 3.0.2 released over the past month as soon as possible.

The development comes a little over three months after another critical flaw in GeoServer (CVE-2026-76904, CVSS score: 9.8) witnessed active exploitation efforts as a zero-day.

"Organizations running publicly exposed GeoServer instances should prioritize patching them as soon as possible," Ganchev said. "Any exposed systems should be audited for signs of credentials leaking, unauthorized access and code execution on the host. Where patching is not immediately possible, restrict access to the host."

(The story has been updated after publication to include more details of the flaws and emphasize that advisories for the issues were released on November 24, 2026. The patches were originally released in October and early November 2026.)

[6] The Hacker News - U.S. Treasury's Financial Crimes Agency Takes Some Systems Offline After Cyber Intrusion
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Wed, 25 Nov 2026 18:00:08 +0530
Link: https://thehackernews.com/2026/11/us-treasurys-fincen-takes-some-systems.html

[TAM METİN - 398 kelime]
The U.S. Treasury Department's Financial Crimes Enforcement Network (FinCEN) has been hit by a cybersecurity incident that resulted in "unauthorized access to a limited subset of its systems," the bureau said on Tuesday. As a precaution, it has taken some systems offline.

The bureau collects suspicious activity reports and other Bank Secrecy Act (BSA) filings from banks and other financial institutions and analyzes them to fight money laundering and terrorist financing.

The BSA E-Filing website is still up, although some services may be temporarily unavailable. The agency has not named the systems it reached or those it took offline.

To support filers affected by the disruption, the bureau is extending some filing deadlines: covered reports originally due between November 18 and December 29 can now be submitted until December 30.

A relief order issued the same day, FIN-2026-R002, applies to three groups:

Financial institutions that used Secure Direct Transfer Mode (SDTM), the channel for sending batches of BSA reports directly from their own servers, as of November 18

Companies required to file a beneficial ownership information report during the covered window

Individuals required to update a FinCEN Identifier during the same window

All other Bank Secrecy Act obligations stay in force, including the duty to monitor for suspicious transactions. The service alert says urgent suspicious activity information can still reach law enforcement through the Financial Institutions Hotline, 1-866-556-3974, which is not affected by the disruption.

The disclosure followed days of filing trouble. Filing providers reported failures in automated batch submissions beginning November 18, and the bureau posted a notice about the disruption to SDTM late last week, according to a filing outages report by the markets news site The Deep Dive.

The bureau has not said whether the intrusion caused those outages. The order grants the relief because of a "service disruption" and does not mention the cybersecurity incident.

Nor has the agency said when the access began, whether any data was viewed or taken, or who was responsible. No group has publicly claimed the intrusion so far. Those answers matter because of what the bureau holds: reports of suspicious financial activity filed by financial institutions nationwide.

The incident is not Treasury's first in recent years: in December 2024, a China-linked group used a compromised BeyondTrust API key in an earlier Treasury breach that reached department systems and documents. No link between the two incidents has been reported.

[7] The Hacker News - Credential Remediation Is Slow When You Don’t Know What Will Break
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Wed, 25 Nov 2026 17:21:48 +0530
Link: https://thehackernews.com/2026/11/credential-remediation-is-slow-when-you.html

[TAM METİN - 2512 kelime]
More than 64% still valid after four years: GitGuardian’s State of Secrets Sprawl 2026 found that most valid secrets discovered in 2022 still worked in January 2026, because teams stall on what a revoke will break.

Validity-only triage misses 46% of critical secrets: Prioritize by impact instead, weighing what a credential can access and whether it leaked publicly, and update the risk score as new context arrives.

Revoke last, never first: Issue a replacement, store it in the vault, switch every consumer to the vault call, deploy and monitor, then revoke the original and confirm it no longer authenticates.

Every modern enterprise depends on credentials. This is how humans, systems, and now AI connect securely to data, services, and each other. GitGuardian helps secure that credential layer through three connected capabilities: Detect, Remediate, and Prevent. The journey starts with detection, but credential remediation is where many secret security programs bog down. This is the second of three articles we are releasing to explain how we help address detection, remediation, and prevention.

The first article in this series, “The Credential Layer Is Expanding Faster Than Security Teams Can See It,” covered detection across every surface where credentials live. Just knowing they exist is not enough. Teams need to take action to remediate those findings. To do that, they need context around every credential they find.

Without the right context, it is impossible to know what risks the secret carries. It is also difficult to understand which systems rely on those secrets and the risk levels that revoking the secret brings. Nobody wants to break production, but we also cannot allow attackers to leverage those secrets.

The resulting decision paralysis leaves many teams struggling to rotate immediately. Sometimes it can take years for the secret to be revoked finally. That is actually all too common, as shown by GitGuardian’s State of Secrets Sprawl 2026 research that found that 64% of valid secrets discovered in 2022 still worked in January 2026, a full 4 years later.

It would be great if there were a one-size-fits-all solution for remediating every leak; in reality, each one is tied to a specific integration. What we can do is streamline the processes needed to get to the end, making it much easier for organizations of any size. And it does take the whole organization to properly deal with leaked secrets.

Why credential remediation is so hard for so many teams

A team that discovers an exposed credential rarely has all the authority or knowledge needed to fix it without causing further issues. A security engineer can see it’s valid and dangerous, but that security engineer usually cannot immediately tell which services will fail when it is revoked. Breaking production is considered a much higher risk than the possibility of a stolen and abused secret, so they need to go looking for answers, which is a time-consuming process.

The developers who know where and why a key is used and, hopefully, how to rotate it safely are often busy shipping new features and managing existing requests from the business. An alert in their email that shows only a file path and a secret type asks those busy devs to stop their workflow and launch their own investigation. These requests, all too often, sink into the backlog.

At the same time, the larger the organization, the harder it is to find the right person who knows the answer to “what breaks if this key is revoked?” Application security teams monitor code where keys are used, identity and access management (IAM) teams govern identities that were issued keys, and the Platform Engineering team runs the vaults holding the keys. They all have different perspectives and, in most organizations that are early in their secrets security maturity journey, can only tell part of the story.

Endpoint security teams are also watching what happens at the workstation level. They are beginning to track secrets stored there, as software supply chain attacks have increasingly targeted these plaintext credential stores over the last couple of years. A secret leaked here might even be the triggering event for the rotation in case of compromise from an infostealer. The endpoint team is historically not connected to AppSec or Platform teams.

One credential can touch all these teams while each individual, isolated group sees only its own slice of the credential layer. Now, multiply the communication and coordination issue across hundreds or thousands of potential leaks, and the scale of the remediation problem becomes clearer.

Action before investigation invites downtime

The risk of revoking before fully understanding the fallout is fairly high for the business. This is weighed against potential future abuse. Of course, this math changes during an active breach when the clock is ticking and when exfiltration or lateral movement is already occurring. Those emergencies do not get backlogged. Mature organizations understand that addressing leaks before an emergency arises is the better plan overall, but remediation times are still measured in weeks for most teams. How long a remediation process takes really boils down to how fast and accurately responders can gather and communicate information about the issue. Responders need to know a lot of things, starting with whether the credential works, what it can access, and who owns it before they can even ask questions like “where else does it appear?” and “which systems depend on it?”

For example, envision two valid cloud credentials. One grants read-only access to a single storage bucket, and the second one is a root-level key with administrative control over production. While their privileges and dependent workloads determine the appropriate level of urgency, both require the same level of insight and investigation to triage the remediation process and decide whether the response is a rotation next sprint or a revocation this very second.

The ultimate goal is to remove the leaked key rapidly, but in a way that does not interrupt production or break pipeline builds. Rotating secrets safely simply requires resources and a coordination plan. If this process is always piecemeal and manual, then mistakes are more likely to happen.

A security team that causes an outage once will face resistance the next time it asks to rotate anything.

Prioritize by impact, not just validity

Validity is the first and clearest signal most responders look at. Alone, this is an incomplete signal. GitGuardian research from the State of Secrets report found that validity-only triage misses 46% of critical secrets such as custom API tokens and SSH keys, because those secrets cannot be verified automatically.

While validity still matters, access to sensitive data or core systems raises urgency. If the leak happened publicly on the internet, that raises the urgency even further. Any risk scoring needs to explain which factors drove it. It also needs to be a living risk score, adjusting for new information as new context arrives. Scoring it once upon discovery is akin to trusting an initial CVSS score in a CVE disclosure. It is a helpful guide but needs to be updated based on what is really going on in your organization. The organization and its risk tolerance will decide what happens next. Having as much information centrally available as possible is key to a rapid response, as responders reach the boundaries of what systems they can actually touch.

Route the work to the right people who can push the change

Remediation requests should let the developer or platform team act immediately, without requiring the team to repeat the investigation that has already taken place.

Any request to replace a key for a production system should include validity and known exposure locations. It should list dependencies too, and where possible show how it connects to business operations. The more the person picking up the request understands about the issue, the sooner they can act. In an ideal world, all remediation requests would be routed to their named owners. In the messy reality of the enterprise, you will likely need to involve the developer who leaked the secret and gather data from commit attribution or source metadata. We need to establish communication because there is a probability that they will need further instructions or guidance.

Requests should not be ‘thrown over the fence’ and then forgotten about. Follow-up and confirmation about remediation progress should be centrally collected and shared so every department knows where the rotation process is at any given moment. This accountability proves resilience efforts when an auditor shows up.

Engineers need to be enabled with a clean way to communicate once they start the work and once their ticket is ready to close. Routing the work through existing ticketing tools keeps it alongside other engineering work, but those tickets also need to integrate with the responder’s security operational tooling.

Rotate, revoke, and vault: the order matters

Safe rotation means following a specific set of steps, in the right order, so you don’t break anything. Remember: “Availability” is one of the legs of the CIA (confidentiality, integrity, availability) security triad.

For mature organizations, this could be as straightforward as rotating the secret in a vault, since the production applications and systems relying on the credential are all already consuming it from the secrets manager. Any plaintext copies are then rendered harmless once the original offending secret is revoked.

For organizations and situations where no existing vaulted copy is in use, this process often starts with issuing a replacement credential. If the responder can do this, they should do so immediately and store it safely in the vault. Then, replace every occurrence of the plaintext secret with that vault call, across every known consumer. The remediation steps from there are to push the changed code or config live and monitor for any issues, ready to roll back if anything breaks. Only when all the new systems are safely consuming the new vaulted credential appropriately can the team revoke the original. Getting this out of order turns a security fix into an incident.

Verify the fix and keep the evidence

Only close the ticket when the key is safely revoked, and the danger is removed. There needs to be an audit trail about what you fixed and how you proved that. You need verifiable confirmation that the old credential no longer authenticates. Optionally, and depending on the circumstances, you can show that the old secret was removed from all surfaces.

Any incidents closed while the credential remains valid deserve their own review queue. This common case happens when a team rotates a key in one system while the old key remains active and valid in another. This means a siloed team only accounted for their one occurrence in their tooling that lacks a unified view or relies on manual tracking.

The incident record also serves auditors and security leaders. It shows what was found, who acted, and how long remediation took.

One credential, start to finish with GitGuardian

At GitGuardian, we have helped thousands of developers and organizations streamline their remediation process.

To help visualize how we can help, imagine a Kubernetes application that calls an external service with an API key stored in a secrets manager. During troubleshooting in our imagined scenario, a developer copies the key into a local configuration file and commits it. This is a fairly common scenario we have seen play out many times.

GitGuardian scanning finds the secret as soon as it is committed. The platform generates a new open incident and alerts the assigned team. An automatic validity check confirms the key is still active, and the Secret Analyzer lists its permissions. A presence check confirms the secret is still in the codebase. All these checks can be rerun as remediation progresses.

For customers with full platform integrations, the GitGuardian Exploration Map shows a production workload depending on it, and fingerprint matching ties the committed value to the vaulted copy. The security team can bring that context to the owning developer and operations team.

Once a replacement key is issued, everyone can track the progress of the rotation and see when the PR that accounts for the deployment configuration is pulling from the vault. There is no guessing about whether revoking the original will affect anything critical. GitGuardian then rechecks validity and confirms the committed copy is gone.

The incident might also expose a pattern worth investigating across other teams. Has this same codebase seen similar incidents? Has the same user caused the same issue before? Are vaulted credentials ending up in unmanaged files on developer laptops on a regular basis? These all point to a wider issue that needs to be addressed to prevent further incidents and an increased remediation backlog.

From fixing to preventing

Remediation reduces risk one credential at a time. It also shows where exposures keep coming from.

The third and final article in this series covers prevention, starting on developer machines and in AI coding workflows. Teams that want to see this remediation workflow can take an interactive tour of the GitGuardian platform.

What is credential remediation?

Credential remediation is the work of removing the risk from a leaked or exposed credential. The goal is to replace and revoke the leaked key quickly, in a way that does not interrupt production or break pipeline builds, and then to confirm the old credential no longer authenticates.

Why does credential remediation take so long?

The team that finds an exposed credential rarely has the authority or the knowledge to fix it alone. A security engineer can see the credential is valid and dangerous, but usually cannot tell which services will fail when it is revoked. The developers, identity, platform, and endpoint teams each see only their own slice of the credential layer, so responders spend their time gathering context instead of fixing the leak.

How do you prioritize which leaked credentials to fix first?

Prioritize by impact, not validity alone. GitGuardian’s State of Secrets Sprawl 2026 found that validation-only prioritization misses 46% of critical secrets, because some secrets cannot be verified automatically. Access to sensitive data or core systems raises urgency, and a public leak raises it further. The risk score should explain which factors drove it and change as new context arrives.

What is the safe order for rotating a leaked secret?

Issue a replacement credential and store it in the vault. Replace every plaintext copy of the old secret with a call to the vault, across every known consumer. Push the changed code or configuration live and monitor for issues, ready to roll back. Revoke the original only when every system is consuming the new vaulted credential. Getting this out of order turns a security fix into an incident.

How do you verify that a leaked credential has been remediated?

Close the ticket only when you have verifiable confirmation that the old credential no longer authenticates. Keep an audit trail of what was fixed and how it was proved, including who acted and how long remediation took. Incidents closed while the credential is still valid deserve their own review, since a team may have rotated the key in one system while it stayed active in another.

[8] The Hacker News - Drupal Fail2Install Exploit Details Emerge as Critical RCE Flaw Faces Active Exploitation
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Wed, 25 Nov 2026 16:53:26 +0530
Link: https://thehackernews.com/2026/11/drupal-fail2install-exploit-details.html

[TAM METİN - 738 kelime]
The U.S. Cybersecurity and Infrastructure Security Agency (CISA) on Tuesday added a critical flaw impacting Drupal to its Known Exploited Vulnerabilities (KEV) catalog, requiring federal agencies to apply the fixes by November 27, 2026.

The vulnerability, tracked as CVE-2026-132165 aka Fail2Install (CVSS score: 9.8), is a case of remote code execution (RCE) impacting Drupal core.

"Drupal Core contains a not failing securely vulnerability that could allow an unauthenticated attacker to take over an existing site or execute arbitrary code via the Drupal installer," CISA said. "Under specific conditions, the attacker can use install.php to attempt to reinstall Drupal on the site."

As previously reported by The Hacker News, an attacker can use the installer to attempt to reinstall Drupal, resulting in site takeover or remote code execution under certain circumstances.

According to an independent analysis by Searchlight Cyber, it has emerged that ordinary server resource limits can be abused to trick Drupal into mistakenly believing that an existing website was not installed, enter its installation process via "install.php," create an administrator-controlled account, and execute arbitrary PHP code.

While Drupal has checks in place to determine whether the website is already installed before allowing installation to proceed, the research found that it's possible to bypass these checks by exhausting server resources.

As a result, a failed database connection to the configured database and a failed database check for an existing installation are interpreted as evidence that the website has not been installed, thereby opening the door to a scenario where Drupal allows the installation to proceed but only after winning a race condition that allows a second database connectivity check to pass.

"So if the database refuses the early connections and accepts the later one, all within one request, the installer concludes the site is new and shows its setup forms on a live site," StepSecurity said in an explainer. "

"The installer's database form saves the settings it receives to settings.php. Once an attacker submits that form with their own database settings, the live site points at the attacker's database. The attacker then creates the site's administrator account."

To achieve this, the three resource exhaustion techniques have been put to use -

EMFILE, which refers to a limit on how many files a Linux process can open

Database connection exhaustion

TCP source-port exhaustion

"We started with EMFILE, then explored database and TCP limits to determine whether availability failures could become security primitives," the cybersecurity company said.

"Applying this methodology to Drupal uncovered a pre-authentication RCE affecting Drupal 8.9, 9, 10, and 11 across four common deployment combinations: Apache with PostgreSQL, Apache with MySQL, PHP-FPM with PostgreSQL, and PHP-FPM with MySQL."

This behavior can be turned into a pathway for code execution by taking advantage of the fact that Drupal's installer supports SQLite database files and allows the database path to be selected during installation. This is possible because the installer does not enforce restrictions on the file name extension.

"By selecting a path ending in .php under the document root, the installer creates an SQLite database with a PHP extension," Searchlight Cyber said. "Once the installation reaches the site configuration form, Drupal stores the submitted website name inside the SQLite database. By using PHP code as the website name, the payload bytes are written into the .php database file."

While direct access to the generated PHP file is blocked by Drupal's stock .htaccess on a stock Apache deployment, a path-handling weakness in the Apache RewriteRule could be exploited to achieve arbitrary PHP execution.

"Starting from three system-wide limits, we showed how errors that appear to affect only availability can become primitives for crossing a security boundary," Searchlight Cyber researchers Kevin Gervot and Adam Kues said.

"In Drupal, temporary database failures can produce a FAIL, FAIL, and then SUCCESS sequence that reopens the installer on an existing website and ultimately leads to pre-authentication RCE through an SQLite database containing PHP code."

The issue has been addressed in the following versions -

Drupal 11.4.x - Update to Drupal 11.4.13

Drupal 11.3.x - Update to Drupal 11.3.22

Drupal 10.6.x - Update to Drupal 10.6.22

The addition of CVE-2026-132165 to the KEV catalog follows a report received by the Drupal Security Team that it "was exploited on live sites as early as 2026-11-19 19:00 UTC."

It's worth noting that CVE-2026-132165 is the third Drupal flaw to come under active exploitation this year following CVE-2026-9082 (CVSS score: 6.5) and CVE-2026-118539 (CVSS score: 6.5).

[9] The Hacker News - Ex-OpenAI Employee Accused of Bugging Company Offices Granted $1 Million Bail
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Wed, 25 Nov 2026 16:13:07 +0530
Link: https://thehackernews.com/2026/11/ex-openai-employee-accused-of-bugging.html

[TAM METİN - 402 kelime]
A former OpenAI employee accused of concealing a covert recording device at the company's San Francisco offices will be allowed to be released from jail on a $1 million bond, a San Francisco federal court judge ruled Tuesday.

Abdulrahman Alfozan, a 35-year-old Saudi national, was arrested on October 9, 2026, for allegedly bugging the artificial intelligence (AI) company's offices with the aim of recording sensitive conversations.

Subsequent court filings have pointed out that Alfozan was in touch on WhatsApp with a member of the Saudi royal family, as well as expressed interest in moving back to Saudi Arabia following OpenAI's initial public offering (IPO).

He has been charged with using or endeavoring to use an electronic, mechanical, or other device to intercept oral communications. If convicted, Alfozan faces a maximum penalty of 5 years in prison and a $250,000 fine, followed by 3 years of supervised release.

"At this time, we have no indications of this individual inappropriately accessing or exfiltrating model weights, source code, or customer data," the ChatGPT-maker said at the time.

Alfozan pleaded not guilty to the charges during his appearance in San Francisco federal court earlier this month.

According to CNBC, the decision comes after prosecutors told the court last week that they had "found no new evidence to support the theory that Mr. Alfozan was stealing trade secrets, or to support the speculation that he was acting as an unregistered foreign agent for Saudi Arabia."

However, they said investigators have found hundreds of audio files on the defendant's personal devices, including dozens of recordings of meetings or conversations that took place at OpenAI throughout September 2026.

Some of these involved meetings with OpenAI customers and contained material protected by attorney-client privilege, prosecutors said, adding he possessed a photograph of what's believed to be a term sheet for a "strategic partnership" between OpenAI and another company.

"What the defendant did or intended to do with those audio files remains under investigation," they said.

Under the bail conditions, Alfozan will be required to wear a GPS ankle monitor, observe a curfew from 7 p.m. to 7 a.m., stay within the Northern District of California, and avoid airports and train and bus stations. He is expected to be released on Wednesday morning.

"There is no spy story that we're talking about right now," U.S. Magistrate Judge Alex G. Tse said, per The Wall Street Journal. "The government doesn't have that."

[10] The Hacker News - Hackers Exploited CVSS 10.0 Hex.pm RCE Flaw to Compromise 6 Package Registry Servers
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Wed, 25 Nov 2026 14:36:59 +0530
Link: https://thehackernews.com/2026/11/hackers-exploited-cvss-100-hexpm-rce.html

[TAM METİN - 1016 kelime]
An attacker ran operating system commands on Monday on six production servers of Hex.pm, the package registry that Elixir and Erlang developers fetch their dependencies from.

The flaw used in the attack had been in the registry's code since February 2016, and the team that maintains the service had every server on a patched build within about 15 minutes of the first malicious request.

Checks since then found no sign that any package was changed. Whether the attacker read account data such as email addresses, password hashes, and two-factor authentication secrets could not be ruled out, the team said.

What Hex Users Should Do Now

Everyone with a hex.pm account has been logged out because the maintainers replaced the keys that sign sessions and access tokens. They advise account holders to take these steps:

Change your hex.pm password.

Reset two-factor authentication by setting up a new authenticator entry, and store the new recovery codes.

Log back in to the client with mix hex.user auth or, for Erlang projects, rebar3 hex user auth.

Update the Hex client when the next release ships, expected within days, with a new registry signing system.

Watch for phishing. Exposed email addresses may be misused, so check that links in any email about Hex go to hex.pm.

API keys do not need to be replaced, according to the team: they are random 128-bit values stored only as HMAC-SHA256 hashes, which it says cannot realistically be brute-forced. Passwords are hashed with bcrypt, which slows guessing but does not stop it for weak passwords, so changing them still matters.

Developers who never created a hex.pm account and only fetch public packages have nothing to reset, Peter Ullrich, one of the report's authors, said in a maintainer clarification on the Elixir Forum. Those who signed up with GitHub should still log back in to their clients, and reset two-factor authentication if they enabled it.

Until the new client ships, the advice is to fetch packages only from repo.hex.pm and to avoid third-party mirrors. The registry signing key has not yet been replaced, and it was among the credentials the attacker could have read.

The flaw, tracked as CVE-2026-129514 with the maximum CVSS score of 10.0, sat in the parser hex.pm used to read request bodies sent in an Erlang data format. That parser ran before routing and authentication, so an unauthenticated POST request to any address on the site could reach it.

The decoding function was meant to be safe, and it did block dangerous terms such as functions, but it accepted maps with a __struct__ key, which allowed a request body to pose as any Elixir struct the application had loaded.

The safety check that would have stopped this was removed from the code on February 23, 2016, which is where the flaw's age comes from. Exploitation before Monday has not been established.

A crafted struct ran nothing by itself. It caused an error, and the error reporting code then inspected the struct. That inspection, via the Ecto and DBConnection database libraries, led to a point where the server called a function of the attacker's choice, which then ran operating system commands with the permissions of the registry's service account.

The security advisory lists hex.pm as affected from February 23, 2016 until Monday. The fix commit makes the decoder reject any term containing a map with a __struct__ key, and every hex.pm server was running it by 18:31 UTC on Monday.

Load balancer logs recorded 69 exploit requests between 18:16 and 18:29 UTC on Monday, all from two addresses, the Hex.pm team said in its incident report yesterday. Files created by the exploit appeared on six web servers, in one case just 3 milliseconds after the request. Three of those servers joined mid-attack, when a restart brought up machines still on the vulnerable build.

To check the packages, the team compared every database table against a copy taken two minutes before the first exploit request and traced each difference to normal activity. The 23 releases published during the attack all match the audit log, no older release was changed, and the package and registry files written that evening match the database records.

Database reads, shell commands, environment variables and direct reads from file storage were not logged. For that reason, "we cannot rule out that the attacker accessed data stored in the database," the team wrote. That database holds email addresses, password hashes, API key hashes, two-factor authentication secrets and session data, and private packages could also have been read without leaving a trace.

The checks found no leftover shells and no new accounts or cloud keys in the audit logs. Who was behind the attack is unknown. Activity from the two addresses going back to late last week suggests the attacker built a local copy of hex.pm to test against, according to the team.

A separate security report describing the same flaw arrived by email at 19:07 UTC on Monday, after the attack had ended. By yesterday morning the team had replaced every credential the attacker could have read except the registry signing key. On Monday night it reported the breach to the Swedish data protection authority, to CISA in the United States and to ENISA in the European Union.

Related Library Fixes

Alongside the incident, maintainers of three widely used Elixir libraries disclosed flaws of their own involving structs built from untrusted data, and shipped fixed versions yesterday. These are separate disclosures, not further attacks on Hex.pm.

Each needs its own conditions to be exploited. The Ash flaw requires keyset pagination with a client-supplied cursor, certain sort field types, and a signing secret left at its unset default, according to the Ash advisory.

The db_connection flaw "is not exploitable in db_connection alone," the DBConnection advisory says, because another component must first decode untrusted data into structs. The Flop flaw has similar conditions around its pagination cursors, according to the Flop advisory.

The exploited flaw sits in the hexpm server code, not in the Hex client, so organizations that run their own copy of the registry are affected until they deploy the patch.

[11] BleepingComputer - Palo Alto Networks warns of Fragnesia flaw exploited in PAN-OS attacks
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Wed, 25 Nov 2026 17:05:09 -0500
Link: https://www.bleepingcomputer.com/news/security/palo-alto-networks-warns-of-fragnesia-flaw-exploited-in-pan-os-attacks/

[TAM METİN - 421 kelime]
Threat actors are chaining exploits for the PHP2Shell and Fragnesia vulnerabilities to target Palo Alto Networks devices with management interfaces exposed to the internet.

PHP2Shell is the name for two critical PHP vulnerabilities tracked as CVE-2026-109618 and CVE-2026-109619, which allow unauthenticated attackers to run code remotely with the privileges of the PHP web worker.

The Fragnesia vulnerability, identified as CVE-2026-46300, is a Linux kernel vulnerability in the XFRM ESP-in-TCP subsystem that lets a local attacker with low-privileges to escalate permissions to root level.

An alert from CISA on October 28 warned that both security issues were being chained in attacks to deploy PHP webshells on vulnerable systems and execute commands as root.

Around the same time, Dream Security researchers reported observing a campaign using the same chain, targeting servers in the United States, Japan, Taiwan, and South Korea, including a U.S. government affiliate.

Palo Alto Networks has confirmed in an advisory today that such attacks also target firewalls running PAN-OS.

“Exploitation of CVE-2026-46300 has been observed targeting the Palo Alto Networks management interface exposed to the internet,” reads the security bulletin.

The networking company says that all exploitation activity it observed involved PAN-OS devices that exposed their management interface to the public internet, which goes against its recommended ‘best practices.’

Palo Alto Networks did not provide any technical details about the chaining of the PHP2Shell vulnerabilities, but noted that this is the only known way to exploit Fragnesia, and they are not aware of another exploitation pathway for the flaw.

The company has released fixes for Fragnesia for PAN-OS 10.2, 11.1, 11.2, 12.1, and 12.2, with the exact patch-carrying build varying by release.

Given that the two flaws are exploited together, administrators who applied updates that addressed the PHP2Shell flaw should be safe from these attacks.

In addition to applying the security updates, Palo Alto Networks also recommends that those who need to keep a remote management interface available restrict access to trusted internal IP addresses.

[12] BleepingComputer - Italy and Switzerland warn of autonomous AI-driven cyberattacks
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Wed, 25 Nov 2026 14:39:31 -0500
Link: https://www.bleepingcomputer.com/news/security/italy-and-switzerland-warn-of-autonomous-ai-driven-cyberattacks/

[TAM METİN - 569 kelime]
Swiss and Italian cybersecurity agencies are warning about automated cyberattacks powered by AI agents targeting organizations in the critical infrastructure sector.

The two agencies listed four AI-powered penetration testing and red-teaming frameworks that have been involved in recent breaches and hacking incidents: ARTEX AI, CyberStrikeAI, DeepSeek Harness - Red Team, and PentAGI.

Both agencies have urged organizations to reduce the number of systems exposed on the internet, prioritize patching, and monitor for unusual activity.

Large-scale attacks in Italy

CSIRT Italia says it detected large-scale malicious campaigns that used autonomous AI agents to identify and exploit vulnerabilities and misconfigurations on exposed endpoints.

In some cases, the agency observed multiple AI agents working in parallel to compromise a large number of systems within a few hours.

In one instance involving a DeepSeek Harness setup, a coordinator assigned tasks to five AI agents that undertook a broad spectrum of operational stages, from information gathering to exploitation and persistence.

The agents chained vulnerabilities, misconfigurations, and compromised credentials, leveraging exposed systems as a foothold to reach internal networks.

“During the observed campaigns, autonomous agents demonstrated the ability to carry out complex, sustained activity to detect misconfigurations, link even low-impact vulnerabilities into complex attack chains, and dynamically adapt their operating modules,” CSIRT Italia says.

“They went as far as writing malicious code in real time to evade endpoint defenses and carry out actions deeper within the infrastructure.”

The Italian cybersecurity agency advises administrators to prioritize patching internet-facing systems, focus on addressing flaws known to be actively exploited in the wild, and disable remote management interfaces if not needed.

The agency also recommends monitoring control plane operations, authentication sources, traffic to internal networks, and outbound server traffic to detect suspicious activity.

First AI breaches in Switzerland

Switzerland’s National Cyber Security Center (NCSC) warned of similar campaigns targeting organizations in the country, confirming the first cases of successful AI-assisted cyberattacks.

“In recent days, the NCSC has received its first reports of cyberattacks against organisations in Switzerland in which the attack was carried out with the assistance of an AI agent,” the Swiss NCSC said.

“Various organisations and operators of critical infrastructure across at least two sectors have been affected by these cyberattacks.”

“In all cyberattacks known to the NCSC, the use of AI agents led to the successful compromise of the victim’s IT systems.”

The agency described rapid attacks in which agents exploited weak passwords and known vulnerabilities in exposed endpoints, using frameworks to verify weaknesses and independently decide what to do next.

NCSC says that the number of AI agents publicly (and often freely) available to cybercriminals has nearly doubled since October, resulting in these massive waves against Swiss organizations, including critical infrastructure operators.

The agency recommends that administrators of internet-facing systems patch critical vulnerabilities within 24 hours of release, add multi-factor authentication (MFA) or passkey protection, and enforce least-privilege principles.

[13] BleepingComputer - CISA warns of critical unpatched flaw in Danfoss refrigeration controllers
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Wed, 25 Nov 2026 11:13:40 -0500
Link: https://www.bleepingcomputer.com/news/security/cisa-warns-of-critical-unpatched-flaw-in-danfoss-refrigeration-controllers/

[TAM METİN - 520 kelime]
The U.S. Cybersecurity and Infrastructure Security Agency (CISA) is warning about a critical vulnerability in Danfoss AK-SM 8xxA Series controllers used by food retailers to manage commercial refrigeration, HVAC, and lighting systems.

Danfoss controllers have been compromised recently in attacks at various supermarkets and food retailers across Europe where hackers remotely shut down cooling systems or raised their temperatures.

The security issue is tracked as CVE-2026-129713 and received a severity score of 9.8. It is a stack-based buffer overflow in the controller’s server.

CISA says an attacker can trigger the flaw without authentication, cause the server to restart, and potentially achieve remote code execution with the web server's privileges.

Danfoss AK-SM 8xxA controllers are central control units for supermarket and convenience-store refrigeration. They are used to manage equipment such as refrigerated display cases and compressor racks, to log temperatures, and to alert when changes occur.

Danfoss calls the product line the AK-SM 800A Series, which includes the 820A, 850A, and 880A models. Depending on the model, they can also control store lighting and HVAC.

CISA notes that the controllers are deployed worldwide, but their exact number remains unclear.

On November 20, Danfoss published a security advisory saying that the CVE-2026-129713 vulnerability would be addressed in the upcoming software version 5.5.x for Danfoss AK-SM 8xxA Series.

The company said that the update would also remove the option that allows customers to turn off ‘Strict mode’ in the product.

Currently, there is no patch available for CVE-2026-129713, and potentially impacted system administrators are advised to take the following actions:

Keep controllers off of the public internet and minimize network exposure where possible.

Segregate control systems from business networks and place them behind firewalls.

Use up-to-date Virtual Private Networks (VPNs) when remote access is required.

Prior Danfoss incidents

Danfoss systems were recently targeted in high-impact cybersecurity incidents across Europe. Between October 11 and 12, several French supermarkets and food retailers suffered significant losses after attacks attributed to Iran’s IRGC-linked Shahid Kaveh unit compromised Danfoss AK-SM/AK-SC and Copeland/Dixell XWEB refrigeration controllers and remotely altered their defrost settings.

On November 2, at least 115 supermarket stores in Belgium, the Netherlands, Sweden, Luxembourg, Ireland, Romania, and Estonia, had their refrigeration equipment shut down or their settings changed remotely.

Supplier Sabcobel said the attackers used the same vulnerability previously seen in the October attacks in France, indicating that Danfoss systems were involved again.

On the same day, attackers suspected to be linked to the same campaign targeted a refrigeration supplier for the French national blood service, altering cold-storage settings at three facilities.

[14] BleepingComputer - More Pentests Without More Pentesters: Where AI Safely Helps
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Wed, 25 Nov 2026 10:00:10 -0500
Link: https://www.bleepingcomputer.com/news/security/more-pentests-without-more-pentesters-where-ai-safely-helps/

[TAM METİN - 1026 kelime]
Demand for penetration testing keeps rising, be it due to DORA threat-led testing, NIS2, or customer security questionnaires. However, supply often cannot keep up.

Experienced testers are scarce, take months to hire and train, and every one of them spends part of each engagement on work that is not testing. For a company that sells hours, that unbillable part is the margin.

This article shows how to measure it, which parts AI can take over, and how to do that without sending client vulnerabilities to someone else's servers.

Step 1: Measure where an engagement's hours go

Most companies track billable testing time and lack a number for the rest. If you tag the last ten engagements by phase, the picture usually changes:

Calls, scope documents, access setup

Recon, exploitation, validation

Writing findings, screenshots, formatting

Senior QA, rework, client comments

Report handover, status questions, retest

Two ratios matter: documentation plus review as a share of testing time, and calendar days from last testing day to report delivery. The first sums up to the hidden cost, but only the second is what clients feel.

Step 2: Decide what AI should and should not do

The tasks that suit a language model are the ones where the tester already knows the answer and needs it written down:

Drafting finding text from tester notes, using your own vulnerability library as the template, so tone and structure stay yours.

Normalizing and de-duplicating scanner output from Burp, Nessus, Nuclei and similar tools into one set of draft findings.

Consistency review: missing remediation, severity that does not match the CVSS vector, the same issue worded three ways.

Executive summary drafts, built from the validated findings and then edited.

It will be helpful to decide beforehand in what capacity to keep humans on severity decisions, exploit validation and anything the client will rely on as a statement of fact.

A model that confidently describes and is set to run an exploit chain that nobody verified can be is worse than no help.

Run More Pentests With the Same Team, Using AI That Stays In-House

PentestPad covers the whole engagement, from scoping and scanner imports to findings, client delivery and retests.

Its AI can run against a self-hosted LLM inside your own network.

Step 3: A reference workflow

Import scanner output and tester notes into one project as draft findings.

Pull in matching entries from your reusable vulnerability library.

Let the model rewrite each draft in your house style. The tester edits and approves.

Attach evidence to the finding at the time of capture.

Senior reviewer works through comments on the report itself, not email.

Export the branded report (DOCX or PDF) or deliver it through a client portal.

Track remediation and retest status on the same findings.

If an AI agent is part of the process, give it a defined interface rather than a free hand. A typed tool interface (for example MCP) lets an agent create findings or fetch a project while permissions, audit and the data boundary stay in the platform.

Three numbers can be tracked:

Documentation-plus-review hours as a percentage of testing hours.

Days from last testing day to delivery.

Rework rounds per report.

If the first two fall and the third does not rise, the change is working.

Doing this in practice

One way to do this in practice is by using PentestPad.

It’s is a pentest reporting and project-management platform designed around this workflow: 20+ imports (Burp Suite, Nessus, Qualys, Nuclei, OpenVAS, CSV, JSON), reusable vulnerability templates, a CVSS calculator, custom branded DOCX/PDF/XLSX reports, a white-label client portal, an AI writing assistant that can run against your own self-hosted LLM, an MCP server for agents, and a choice of EU cloud or full self-hosting.

Built for agents as well as people

PentestPad is built to be driven by software as well as by a person at a keyboard, and the model doing the driving can sit inside your own network.

The writing assistant works from what a tester already has. Hand it scanner output, a Burp finding, a screenshot or a log file, attach the evidence, and it returns a structured finding written to the instructions your organization has defined, so the draft arrives in your format rather than a generic one.

PentestPad is the knowledge base, a local model does the reasoning, and a terminal harness is wired to both.

Access to the context of the tested environment greatly helps PentestPad outperform other generic automated vulnerability detection tools.

The rest of the platform at a glance

A short tour of what else is in PentestPad, for readers who want the full picture:

Reporting. Findings are added directly from terminal to the PentestPad platform. Custom branded templates, DOCX, PDF and XLSX export, encrypted PDFs with a password you set, and a built-in CVSS calculator.

Proposal management with project scoping.

Jira sync. A two-way Jira sync, so a finding becomes a ticket with its reproduction steps and priority, and when a developer marks it done the finding moves to ready for retest, and back to open if the retest fails.

Findings. Reusable vulnerability templates, with evidence attached to each finding when you capture it.

Interoperability. CSV and JSON exports, and the 20-plus imports from tools such as Burp Suite, Nessus, Qualys, Nuclei and OpenVAS.

Project management. Engagements, findings and retests in one place, which the company says cuts project-management overhead by around 60%.

Client portal. White-labelled with your logo, colours and domain, and a 2FA screen. Clients see only their own findings.

Forever free additional resources like reporting documents and vulnerability templates.

“It's definitely an essential tool for those in our line of work. Once you try it, you won't want to go back.” – Luca Epifanio, CEO, Bitcube Security

"Where a client's findings are allowed to live is decided by the team holding them, and for many that means nowhere outside their own infrastructure. That is the requirement we built the AI around, so the model runs wherever a team needs it to, including entirely inside their own network." – Luka Šikic, CEO, Secure Block.
