[15] BleepingComputer - InfraTrust warns of exploited supply chain flaws in network appliances
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Wed, 25 Nov 2026 10:00:00 -0500
Link: https://www.bleepingcomputer.com/news/security/infratrust-warns-of-exploited-supply-chain-flaws-in-network-appliances/

[TAM METİN - 630 kelime]
Eclypsium's November InfraTrust report warns that most infrastructure security advisories addressing exploited vulnerabilities over the past month involve flaws in third-party software bundled into products rather than code developed by the vendors themselves.

The November InfraTrust Pulse report tracks 373 new security advisories published by 23 infrastructure vendors between October 15 and November 19, covering 3,880 vulnerabilities.

Of these advisories, 98 were rated critical, and 38 contained vulnerabilities listed in CISA's Known Exploited Vulnerabilities (KEV) catalog.

However, 33 of those 38 advisories fixed exploited flaws in bundled software components, primarily PHP, Node.js, and OpenSSH.

These vulnerabilities pose additional risks for enterprises, as even after a software developer releases a security fix, organizations must wait for infrastructure vendors to incorporate it into their products.

"Your exposure to exploited flaws depends on how quickly each vendor rebuilds its products after an upstream fix ships," the report reads.

An example of this is the two PHP vulnerabilities known as PHP2Shell (CVE-2026-109618 and CVE-2026-109619) highlighted in last month's InfraTrust report, which are now being exploited in attacks against Palo Alto Networks and Check Point firewalls.

In both cases, threat actors chained the PHP vulnerabilities with other flaws to gain root access to compromised devices, with Check Point reporting destructive attacks that deployed data-wiping malware on VMware ESXi hosts.

As of November 20, PHP2Shell fixes were either partially available or had yet to be released for affected products from Fortinet, Juniper, Lenovo, HPE, Netgear, HP, and Siemens.

The November 19 release of another 18 PHP security advisories further complicated patching efforts for infrastructure vendors and their customers.

With administrators dealing with an increasing number of infrastructure vulnerabilities to address, InfraTrust says to prioritize the following flaws for immediate patching this month:

Cisco Expressway: CVE-2026-76466, a critical vulnerability added to CISA's KEV catalog on November 20. On November 23rd, CISA flagged this flaw as actively exploited after Russian cybersecurity firm F6 spotted hackers using it to gain root privileges.

Palo Alto Networks and Check Point: The PHP2Shell vulnerabilities and CVE-2026-123039 in Check Point gateways. Check Point warned that this flaw was being actively exploited to gain root privileges and establish persistence on vulnerable devices since late October.

Arista VeloCloud Orchestrator: CVE-2026-114625, a critical vulnerability with a CVSS score of 9.9 that was exploited in zero-day attacks.

Cisco Secure Firewall ASA and FTD: CVE-2026-76418, affecting devices configured for remote-access VPNs. The flaw was exploited in attacks to crash devices.

SonicWall SonicOS: CVE-2026-19868, allowing multifactor authentication bypass on VPNs configured with single sign-on. SonicWall warned that it received reports that the flaw was exploited in zero-day attacks.

Administrators should also prioritize installing Fortinet's October hardening updates, which addressed numerous vulnerabilities discovered using AI models.

As AI makes it easier to discover and exploit vulnerabilities, InfraTrust warns that organizations must take additional steps to protect their devices while waiting for vendors to release security updates.

These include removing management interfaces from the Internet, segmenting them internally to limit lateral movement, and investigating previously exposed devices for backdoors that may remain even after security updates are installed.

"Patching is table stakes, but what you do before the patch is available and after the exploit hits will determine whether you survive," the report concludes.

[16] BleepingComputer - Microsoft fixes Microsoft 365 bug blocking access to Office apps
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Wed, 25 Nov 2026 06:16:32 -0500
Link: https://www.bleepingcomputer.com/news/microsoft/microsoft-fixes-blank-dialog-that-made-microsoft-365-office-apps-unusable/

[TAM METİN - 473 kelime]
Earlier this week, Microsoft resolved a known issue that was preventing some Microsoft 365 customers from accessing Office applications.

This follows widespread reports on social media and Microsoft's support website about what affected users described as a persistent white screen or square that can't be dismissed, displayed over Office apps' user interfaces or Microsoft 365 sign-in windows.

"We've installed 3 separate versions of 365 on this device, but no matter how we try to get into a 365 app we get stuck on this white screen. I've launched applications in safe mode as well without any luck," one user said.

The company confirmed in a support document updated Monday that a blank dialog box prevented customers from opening Microsoft 365 Outlook, Word, Excel, PowerPoint, OneNote, and Access apps.

"When you open an Office application, a blank dialog window might appear. The dialog box doesn't show any text or buttons," Microsoft said. "If you can't dismiss it, you might be unable to continue using the application."

Microsoft says the issue is now fixed in Microsoft 365 Apps Version 2611 (Build 16.0.20625.20142), released last week.

​However, Microsoft says that customers who can't update because of this bug can also upgrade to the latest version from the command line by going through the following procedure:

Save your work and close all Office applications.

Open an elevated command prompt.

Run the following command "%programfiles%\Common Files\Microsoft Shared\ClickToRun\officec2rclient.exe" /update user updatetoversion=16.0.20625.20142.

This is the last in a series of bugs that have affected Microsoft 365 and Office customers since the start of the year.

For instance, in June, Microsoft fixed another issue that blocked Office for the web users from opening Excel and PowerPoint documents, and another that prevented Windows 365 users from downloading the Office suite.

One month later, it resolved a bug that blocked third-party apps from launching Microsoft Office apps like Word, Excel, PowerPoint, and Access and from opening documents on Windows systems.

More recently, in September, Microsoft fixed a known issue causing copy-and-paste and autofill failures for Excel users after installing the September 2026 security updates.

That same month, Microsoft also had to temporarily pause the rollout of the KB5002907 Microsoft 365 update to fix a bug that deactivated (and in some cases completely removed) perpetual Office 2016 and Office 2019 installations for some users.

[17] BleepingComputer - Hackers now exploit critical Drupal RCE flaw to hijack sites
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Wed, 25 Nov 2026 05:10:20 -0500
Link: https://www.bleepingcomputer.com/news/security/hackers-now-exploit-critical-drupal-rce-flaw-to-hijack-sites/

[TAM METİN - 447 kelime]
The Drupal security team has confirmed that a critical vulnerability that lets attackers hijack unpatched sites has been actively exploited since at least Thursday.

Drupal is a content management system (CMS) commonly used by large organizations, including government entities, major research universities, and high-profile enterprise and media organizations.

The security flaw, tracked as CVE-2026-132165, was discovered by Anthropic's OSS Scanner and can be exploited by anonymous users to gain remote code execution or take over vulnerable sites by abusing the Drupal installer.

"Drupal core ships with an install.php file for installing Drupal core. Under specific conditions, an attacker can use the installer to attempt to reinstall Drupal. This can lead to a site takeover or remote code execution," the Drupal security team warned last Wednesday. "Some exploit paths are mitigated if the web server cannot write to or change permissions on settings.php."

While it wasn't initially flagged as abused in the wild, in an update to the original security advisory, Drupal warned that this flaw has been "exploited on live sites as early as 2026-11-19 19:00 UTC."

Websites using the paid Drupal Steward Web Application Firewall (WAF) service are already protected from known attack vectors, but admins are still advised to upgrade to the latest available Drupal version in case other attack vectors are discovered.

Those who can't immediately upgrade should block access to /core/install.php in the web server configuration or the .htaccess file in the web root to secure the Drupal installer.

On Tuesday, the US Cybersecurity and Infrastructure Security Agency (CISA) has also added the vulnerability to its Known Exploited Vulnerabilities (KEV) Catalog and ordered federal agencies to secure their systems against ongoing attacks by Friday, November 27.

"These types of vulnerabilities are a frequent attack vector for malicious cyber actors and pose significant risks to the federal enterprise," the agency warned. "While BOD 26-04 applies only to FCEB agencies, CISA encourages all organizations to adopt risk-based vulnerability management and prioritize remediation of KEV Catalog vulnerabilities."

Since November 2021, CISA has tagged five Drupal vulnerabilities as abused in the wild, two of which have also been exploited by ransomware groups.

[18] BleepingComputer - Microsoft confirms authentication issues after November updates
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Wed, 25 Nov 2026 04:02:57 -0500
Link: https://www.bleepingcomputer.com/news/microsoft/microsoft-confirms-authentication-issues-after-november-updates/

[TAM METİN - 507 kelime]
Microsoft has confirmed that customers may experience authentication issues after installing November security updates that address a high-severity remote code execution vulnerability.

Tracked as CVE-2026-118737, this security flaw was found in the Windows Remote Procedure Call Runtime and stems from a timing-dependent authentication weakness that unauthenticated attackers can exploit to remotely execute code without user interaction on unpatched systems.

Microsoft says the November security updates represent the first phase of NTLM message-signing improvements, which reduce exposure to relay attacks by ensuring authenticated NTLM sessions always establish a cryptographic session key and negotiate real message signing.

However, as Microsoft explained in an updated support document, the new security improvements introduced to address this vulnerability may also cause authentication failures on patched devices.

"Microsoft is aware of compatibility issues affecting some third-party apps and devices, including authentication proxy servers, single sign-on (SSO) solutions, and application packaging solutions that use NTLM authentication," it said.

"This issue can occur when such a proxy device does not correctly implement the NTLM protocol. For example, an affected device might set the NTLMSSP_NEGOTIATE_TARGET_INFO flag in its CHALLENGE_MESSAGE but provide an empty or incomplete TargetInfo field."

While a fix is not yet available, Microsoft said affected apps and devices return an NTLMSSP_CHALLENGE message with an empty "Target Info List" in network trace signatures or Target Machine fields with "Not Found" values in event logs.

As a temporary workaround, the company advised customers to go through the following procedure on affected systems:

Set the ClientAllowUnsignedNtlm REG_DWORD registry value under HKEY_LOCAL_MACHINE\SYSTEM\CurrentControlSet\Control\Lsa\MSV1_0 to 1 (the value used for clients that do not offer SIGN)

Check whether the affected device or application supports a configuration that uses Kerberos authentication or whether the vendor provides a software or firmware update compatible with the NTLM signing requirements introduced by CVE-2026-118737.

If neither option is available, contact the device or software vendor to request an update that complies with the NTLM protocol requirements described in 3.1.5.1.2 Client Receives a CHALLENGE_MESSAGE from the Server.

However, Microsoft said the ClientAllowUnsignedNtlm registry setting is a short-term solution and may be removed in the future.

"Updating the affected product or using a compatible authentication configuration is the recommended long-term resolution," it noted.

Since it addressed installation issues affecting the November 2026 cumulative updates, Microsoft has also confirmed that this month's security updates are causing Windows activation failures and SQL Server performance issues.

The company is also investigating a known issue that may break printing documents with complex formatting for customers who have installed the latest Windows updates.

[19] BleepingComputer - ShinyHunters claims to return after FBI arrests, uses old PGP keys as proof
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Tue, 24 Nov 2026 11:30:19 -0500
Link: https://www.bleepingcomputer.com/news/security/shinyhunters-resurfaces-after-fbi-arrests-uses-old-pgp-keys-as-proof/

[TAM METİN - 993 kelime]
The ShinyHunters extortion gang claims to have returned in posts on a new data leak site after a series of arrests over the past two months targeting alleged members.

The new Tor site, launched on November 23, contains messages in both Russian and English announcing the group's return, distancing itself from alleged impostors, and denouncing a former member who was reportedly arrested and is cooperating with law enforcement.

The site also includes two verification messages signed using historical ShinyHunters PGP keys dating back to 2020, including one previously used to authenticate communications with BleepingComputer in August 2025.

BleepingComputer confirmed that both messages were signed using known PGP keys associated with the ShinyHunters extortion gang, confirming that the new operators have access to private signing keys historically used by the group.

However, these keys do not confirm whether the individuals behind the new operation are the same people who previously controlled the group, as the keys have circulated among numerous people.

The announcement follows a series of law enforcement operations targeting alleged ShinyHunters members after the group claimed to have breached FBI systems in September using an Oracle PeopleSoft vulnerability.

Later that month, Dutch police confirmed the arrest of a 24-year-old Amsterdam man suspected of involvement with ShinyHunters, who was identified in media reports as Pepijn van der Stap, previously known online as Umbreon.

In early October, another alleged ShinyHunters member known as Rey or Saif was reportedly detained in Jordan and is cooperating with law enforcement. Around the same time, Canadian cybersecurity executive Edward Dubrovsky was arrested in Pennsylvania in an extortion investigation reportedly linked to ShinyHunters.

After the arrests, ShinyHunters' main representative, who had regularly communicated with BleepingComputer and other media outlets, abruptly stopped responding, and their Telegram account was deleted. Soon after, the group's data leak site went offline.

In the new statement, the purported ShinyHunters operators say they are returning after months of rumors and misinformation circulated about the group.

"We’re finally back. A lot has happened recently, and there’s still plenty going on right now," reads the English announcement.

"To be honest, there’s just too much nonsense going on. Everyone’s telling different stories, but no one is really explaining what’s been happening over the last few months."

The operators of the new data leak site say they will communicate exclusively through the Exploit hacking forum account, which they claim to have controlled since 2020, and through the new Tor site.

The statement also addresses the reported arrest of a hacker and ShinyHunters member known as Rey, Dedale, or Saif, who was reportedly detained in Jordan in late September.

According to the announcement, Rey previously collaborated with ShinyHunters but began operating independently, creating Telegram channels without the group's approval.

"As far as we know, he has already been arrested and is now "actively cooperating" with the authorities," reads the English message.

"We already rotated the PGP key that Rey used for DLS, since it has most likely been compromised."

The message also mocks two other individuals reportedly tied to the ShinyHunters investigation, Umbreon and Canadian cybersecurity executive Edward Dubrovsky, referring to their arrests.

"It was a pleasure working with you. And it’s truly a shame that our guys got caught because of such silly mistakes, ones that could easily have been avoided if you fools hadn’t gotten involved with the wrong people," reads the post.

The operators say they plan to launch a new CDN for hosting stolen data and will no longer use any of the existing backup servers.

Old PGP keys used to authenticate new site

To attempt to prove their legitimacy, the operators published two verification messages signed using historical ShinyHunters PGP keys created in January and August 2020.

The keys have the following fingerprints:

ShinyHunters RaidForums PGP key: 1FC4D0B1DEE914BB05B57FABF1F1B98A51C989B3

ShinyHunters RaidForums PGP key: 1FC4D0B1DEE914BB05B57FABF1F1B98A51C989B3

ShinyHunters Empire Market PGP key: 828537C15F43F135A8317153CD16A1660CC7CE51

ShinyHunters Empire Market PGP key: 828537C15F43F135A8317153CD16A1660CC7CE51

BleepingComputer verified that both messages were signed using these known PGP keys associated with the ShinyHunters extortion gang.

PGP uses a pair of cryptographic keys: a public key that anyone can access and use to verify a message or data, and a private key used to create signatures. The private key must be kept secret to prevent others from impersonating the key's owner.

BleepingComputer previously verified a message received from ShinyHunters on August 26, 2025, using the 1FC4D0B1DEE914BB05B57FABF1F1B98A51C989B3 public key.

Successful verification of the November message with that same public key shows that whoever signed it had access to the same private signing key used in the earlier communications, linking it to the ShinyHunters operation behind the widespread data theft attacks in 2026.

However, both historical keys were revoked in March 2026, when ShinyHunters announced a replacement PGP key with the fingerprint F4953411767DE71BEDCDABCB76F4E26F7A20978A.

At the time, the group used both 2020 keys to authenticate the replacement and stated that the older keys would no longer be used for communications.

Despite this, the November 23 announcement once again uses the two revoked 2020 keys to authenticate another new PGP key, 27DA9D5B183247CADFC322880ABC63E30F73822E.

However, none of the November messages examined by BleepingComputer were signed using the March 2026 replacement key, leaving it unclear whether the new operators also have access to that private key.

Because it was rumored that numerous people had the original 2020 keys, these unfortunately do not prove with certainty that the ShinyHunters group has returned or that the brand has not been revived by imposters.

[20] Security Affairs - U.S. CISA adds HPE, Danfoss, Mitel MiCollab, and Vates Xen Orchestra flaws to its Known Exploited Vulnerabilities catalog
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Thu, 26 Nov 2026 06:05:09 +0000
Link: https://securityaffairs.com/204014/hacking/u-s-cisa-adds-hpe-juniper-session-smart-conductor-and-session-smart-router-flaw-to-its-known-exploited-vulnerabilities-catalog.html

[TAM METİN - 1385 kelime]
U.S. CISA adds HPE, Danfoss, Mitel MiCollab, and Vates Xen Orchestra flaws to its Known Exploited Vulnerabilities catalog

U.S. Cybersecurity and Infrastructure Security Agency (CISA) adds HPE, Danfoss, Mitel MiCollab, and Vates Xen Orchestra flaws to its Known Exploited Vulnerabilities catalog.

The U.S. Cybersecurity and Infrastructure Security Agency (CISA) added the following HPE, Danfoss, Mitel MiCollab, and Vates Xen Orchestra flaws to its Known Exploited Vulnerabilities (KEV) catalog.

CVE-2023-25914 Danfoss AK-SM 800A Series Path Traversal Vulnerability

CVE-2023-25915 Danfoss AK-SM 800A Series Improper Input Validation Vulnerability

CVE-2025-41450 Danfoss AK-SM 800A Series Improper Authentication Vulnerability

CVE-2026-103406 Hewlett Packard Enterprise (HPE) Juniper Session Smart Conductor and Session Smart Router OS Command Injection Vulnerability

CVE-2026-133275 Mitel MiCollab Unrestricted Upload of File with Dangerous Type Vulnerability

CVE-2026-133303 Mitel MiCollab XML Injection Vulnerability

CVE-2026-133311 Mitel MiCollab Weak Authentication Vulnerability

CVE-2026-133323 Mitel MiCollab OS Command Injection Vulnerability

CVE-2026-133324 Mitel MiCollab Not Failing Securely Vulnerability

CVE-2026-133469 Vates Xen Orchestra Improper Validation of Specified Type of Input Vulnerability

The first flaw, tracked as CVE-2023-25914 (CVSS score of 7.5), is a path traversal vulnerability affecting Danfoss AK-SM 800A refrigeration management systems running software versions prior to 3.3. An authenticated remote attacker could exploit improper restrictions on file paths to retrieve and read files from the underlying system through the XML interface. Access to sensitive files could provide information useful for further compromising the device. There are no publicly confirmed reports of exploitation in the wild or threat actors associated with this vulnerability. Danfoss addressed the issue in software version 3.3.

The second flaw, tracked as CVE-2023-25915 (CVSS score of 9.8), is a critical improper input validation vulnerability affecting Danfoss AK-SM 800A systems running software versions prior to 3.3. An authenticated remote attacker could exploit insufficient input validation to execute arbitrary commands on the target system, potentially gaining full control of the affected refrigeration controller. Despite its critical severity and the potential consequences for operational technology environments, there are no publicly confirmed reports linking this specific vulnerability to attacks in the wild or identifying threat actors exploiting it. Danfoss released software version 3.3 to address the issue.

The third flaw, tracked as CVE-2025-41450 (CVSS score of 8.2), is an improper authentication vulnerability affecting Danfoss AK-SM 8xxA Series systems running software versions prior to 4.2. The authentication bypass allows an attacker to generate web reports without proper authorization, potentially exposing sensitive information such as internal IP addresses, usernames and store names. The vulnerability was identified by researchers at Claroty’s Team82. Although it affects the same family of refrigeration controllers targeted in the European supermarket attacks, there is no public confirmation that attackers exploited this particular CVE during those incidents. Danfoss addressed the vulnerability in software version 4.2.

The fourth flaw, tracked as CVE-2026-103406 (CVSS score of 9.8), is a critical OS command injection vulnerability affecting HPE Juniper Session Smart Conductor. The flaw resides in the AutomatedProvisioner component and allows an unauthenticated remote attacker to execute arbitrary commands with root privileges by supplying a specially crafted Salt minion ID. The vulnerability has been added to CISA’s Known Exploited Vulnerabilities (KEV) catalog, confirming exploitation in the wild. However, no specific threat actor or ransomware campaign has been publicly attributed to these attacks. Administrators should apply the security updates provided by Juniper, including versions 7.0.5, 7.1.7 and 7.2.3 for the affected release branches.

The fifth flaw, tracked as CVE-2026-133275 (CVSS score of 5.3), is an unrestricted file upload vulnerability affecting the Audio, Web and Video Conferencing (AWV) component of Mitel MiCollab. The vulnerability affects MiCollab version 10.3 (10.3.0.18) and earlier. An unauthenticated remote attacker could exploit insufficient file type validation to upload arbitrary files to the target system. Depending on the nature of the uploaded files and the system configuration, this could provide an opportunity for further malicious activity. Mitel has not reported confirmed exploitation in the wild or identified any threat actors associated with this vulnerability. The company released a security patch for affected installations.

The sixth flaw, tracked as CVE-2026-133303 (CVSS score of 9.0), is a critical XML injection vulnerability affecting the AWV component of Mitel MiCollab. An unauthenticated remote attacker could exploit insufficient parameter validation and leverage malicious content staged in advance to perform XML injection and execute arbitrary commands within the context of the system. Successful exploitation could compromise the confidentiality, integrity and availability of the affected installation. Mitel has not disclosed any confirmed exploitation in the wild or identified threat actors behind attacks exploiting this flaw. A security patch is available for affected MiCollab installations.

The seventh flaw, tracked as CVE-2026-133311 (CVSS score of 5.9), is an improper authentication vulnerability affecting the AWV component of Mitel MiCollab. An unauthenticated remote attacker who knows a specific system-generated random identifier or has access to malicious content staged in advance could exploit the weakness to obtain sensitive information. The vulnerability is limited to the affected functionality and does not provide unrestricted access to arbitrary files elsewhere on the system. No confirmed exploitation or associated threat actors have been publicly reported. Mitel has released a security patch to address the issue.

The eighth flaw, tracked as CVE-2026-133323 (CVSS score of 10.0), is a critical OS command injection vulnerability affecting the AWV component of Mitel MiCollab. An unauthenticated remote attacker could exploit insufficient parameter sanitization to perform blind command injection and execute arbitrary commands within the context of the affected system. Successful exploitation could compromise system integrity and availability. Mitel has not reported confirmed exploitation in the wild or attributed attacks to specific threat actors. A security patch is available to address the vulnerability.

The ninth flaw, tracked as CVE-2026-133324 (CVSS score of 5.3), is an authentication bypass vulnerability affecting the AWV component of Mitel MiCollab. Improper handling of authentication could allow an unauthenticated remote attacker to bypass security controls and perform unauthorized actions within the application. Successful exploitation could compromise the security of the affected installation. No publicly confirmed exploitation or associated threat actors have been reported. Mitel has released a security patch for the affected versions.

The Danfoss vulnerabilities deserve particular attention because they affect the same family of refrigeration management systems involved in the cyberattacks targeting European supermarkets in October and November 2026.

The first wave of attacks, detected between October 11 and 12, compromised refrigeration controllers at approximately 700 retail sites in France. Attackers manipulated defrost settings, forcing refrigeration equipment to operate at excessively high temperatures. The incidents resulted in substantial food losses and at least 15 reported fires. A second wave between November 1 and 2 affected retailers across several other European countries.

The French authorities’ investigation attributed the October attacks to Shahid Kaveh, a cyber unit associated with Iran’s Islamic Revolutionary Guard Corps (IRGC). However, responsibility for the subsequent November attacks across Europe has not been conclusively established.

The technical evidence points to the exploitation of internet-exposed refrigeration management interfaces, with default credentials and outdated firmware considered likely entry points. However, investigators have not confirmed that CVE-2023-25914, CVE-2023-25915 or CVE-2025-41450 were specifically exploited during these operations. The incidents instead highlight the risks associated with exposing operational technology (OT) systems directly to the internet without adequate authentication, network segmentation and security updates.

An additional development is worth mentioning: a November 2026 advisory from Ireland’s National Cyber Security Centre reported research by Midnight Blue identifying six previously unknown pre-authentication remote code execution vulnerabilities in Danfoss AK-SM 800A controllers, affecting firmware versions up to and including 5.3.2. At the time of the advisory, these flaws had not yet received CVE identifiers and no patches were available. Researchers also warned that attackers could establish persistence surviving factory resets and firmware updates. These findings add another layer of concern to the security of refrigeration infrastructure. ncsc.gov.ie

According to Binding Operational Directive (BOD) 22-01: Reducing the Significant Risk of Known Exploited Vulnerabilities, FCEB agencies have to address the identified vulnerabilities by the due date to protect their networks against attacks exploiting the flaws in the catalog.

Experts also recommend that private organizations review the Catalog and address the vulnerabilities in their infrastructure.

CISA order U.S. federal civilian agencies to remediate eight of them by November 28, 2026, and the remaining two by December 9, 2026. The individual deadlines are:

November 28, 2026: CVE-2023-25914, CVE-2023-25915, CVE-2026-103406, CVE-2026-133275, CVE-2026-133303, CVE-2026-133323, CVE-2026-133324 and CVE-2026-133469.

December 9, 2026: CVE-2025-41450 and CVE-2026-133311.

[21] Security Affairs - Switzerland Confirms First AI-Agent Cyberattacks on Local Organisations
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Wed, 25 Nov 2026 21:06:55 +0000
Link: https://securityaffairs.com/204004/hacking/switzerland-confirms-first-ai-agent-cyberattacks-on-local-organisations.html

[TAM METİN - 597 kelime]
Switzerland Confirms First AI-Agent Cyberattacks on Local Organisations

Switzerland reports its first AI-agent cyberattacks, including critical infrastructure breaches, and urges patching internet-facing systems within 24 hours.

Switzerland ’s National Cyber Security Centre (NCSC) has now seen these attacks firsthand. In recent days, it received its first reports of cyberattacks on Swiss organisations carried out with the help of AI agents. The victims include critical infrastructure operators in at least two sectors.

“In recent days, the NCSC has received its first reports of cyberattacks against organisations in Switzerland in which the attack was carried out with the assistance of an AI agent.” reads the report published by NCSC. “Various organisations and operators of critical infrastructure across at least two sectors have been affected by these cyberattacks.”

What stands out is the result. In every AI-assisted attack known to the NCSC, the attackers successfully compromised the victims’ IT systems. The agency sees these incidents as more than isolated cases, warning that they mark a shift in the cyber threat landscape.

The attackers used what the NCSC calls “Agentic Offensive Frameworks”. These AI agents can search for vulnerabilities, choose attack tools, assess the results and decide what to do next, all with limited human input. Security teams also use the same software for penetration testing, which makes these tools dual-use.

The targets aren’t exotic. The agents go after standard or easily guessed passwords, known flaws in software, and systems connected to the internet.

“AI agents often require only a fraction of the time a human would need to locate such vulnerable systems and exploit existing vulnerabilities. Furthermore, they can react flexibly to defence mechanisms by switching to other known attack methods. This leaves organisations with significantly less time to detect an attack and respond appropriately.” continues the report.

AI agents can quickly switch tactics when a defence blocks an attack, leaving organisations less time to detect and stop intrusions. The NCSC warns that these tools speed up common attack methods and that internet-accessible agents such as ARTEX and CyberStrikeAI have almost doubled since October 2026. This could lead to more frequent, faster and more successful automated attacks against organisations of all types.

ARTEX isn’t a surprise. The FBI recently published a FLASH alert that describes it as open source, with no model of its own, plugged into external language models. The same advisory says some intrusions went from first contact to privileged access in under three hours.

The NCSC expects more successful attacks and urges companies and critical infrastructure operators to strengthen their security and resilience. It monitors the threat landscape, stays in contact with national and international partners, and shares technical indicators for detecting these tools with infrastructure operators. Its recommendations are short, and it wants them applied without delay.

Start by shrinking what’s exposed. Fewer systems facing the internet means a smaller attack surface, and where possible you can restrict access with geo-blocking or an allow list of IP addresses or network segments. Every internal resource reachable from the internet, such as webmail and VPN, should sit behind multi-factor authentication or passkeys, with no exceptions.

Then limit the damage. Grant the lowest access rights that work, and watch for deviations from normal behaviour and data volumes across identities, applications, workloads and infrastructure. Test your own infrastructure for vulnerabilities on a regular basis, yourself or with an outside specialist.

“Critical security updates for systems accessible via the internet should be installed as soon as possible, but no later than 24 hours after release.” concludes the report.

[22] Security Affairs - Electrolux Smart Boilers in Ukraine Connect to Russian Servers, Raising Privacy Concerns
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Wed, 25 Nov 2026 12:50:15 +0000
Link: https://securityaffairs.com/203978/security/electrolux-smart-boilers-in-ukraine-connect-to-russian-servers-raising-privacy-concerns.html

[TAM METİN - 1239 kelime]
Electrolux Smart Boilers in Ukraine Connect to Russian Servers, Raising Privacy Concerns

Researchers found Electrolux-branded smart boilers connecting to Russian servers, raising concerns about household data and privacy.

A router in Ukraine recorded a water heater doing something odd. The owner, Alexander Unger, saw his Electrolux EWH 80 look up a server called mqtt.cloud.rusklimat.ru and then try to connect to it. He published the records on Facebook, including a lookup timed at 18:00 on November 23, 2026.

Mysterium VPN’s research team checked the finding. They repeated the lookup on November 25 and got the same address, 130.193.49[.]173. RIPE, the registry for Europe and Russia, lists that block under Yandex.Cloud LLC at 16 Lva Tolstogo Street in Moscow.

The server belongs to Rusklimat, a Moscow group that calls itself the leader of Russia’s climate-equipment market. Its Hommyn app uses that name to reach heaters, air conditioners and ventilation units. Developers who link such devices to Home Assistant list it as the official cloud for Ballu, Electrolux, Zanussi and Royal Thermo, and a 2025 guide on Habr says the devices contact it by default.

Rusklimat sells Electrolux-branded boilers in Russia and advertises them for Hommyn and for Yandex’s voice assistant, Alisa. Mysterium VPN adds, citing Meduza, that Ukraine struck three of Yandex’s six data centers in October 2026 and disrupted Yandex Cloud, where Rusklimat’s server runs.

“The manual for the EWH SI EEC series, hosted by the Austrian arm of the home-improvement chain Hornbach, names CladSwiss AG of Zug, Switzerland, as the manufacturer and says the boiler is made in China. It says the Electrolux name is used under license from AB Electrolux, the Swedish parent company.” reads the report published by Mysterium VPN. “It adds that the remote-control app, ClimatOn, comes from CladSwiss and isn’t provided by Electrolux.”

The manual also says the Electrolux name is used under license from AB Electrolux of Sweden, and that the remote-control app, ClimatOn, comes from CladSwiss, not Electrolux. Retailers lean on the Swedish angle: Rozetka gives Sweden as the country of brand registration, and the series is sold in Serbia, Poland and Lithuania.

This single appliance has links to five countries: Sweden, Switzerland, China, Germany, and Russia. That’s quite a complicated background for a water heater installed in someone’s bathroom.

According to CladSwiss, the data collected by its ClimatOn app should be stored in Frankfurt, Germany. Mysterium VPN also found a ClimatOn server hosted on Amazon infrastructure in Germany. This means the boiler can connect to two different servers, depending on which app the owner uses.

Both apps use the same technology platform. A developer who created software to control an Electrolux SmartInverter boiler locally found that the device identifies itself on the home network as a Syncleo device. Syncleo is based in Israel, has an office in Russia, and lists Rusklimat among its customers.

Why did a buyer in Ukraine end up on the Russian app? Mysterium VPN doesn’t know. Unger, it was told, set the boiler up with Hommyn, and a buyer of another Electrolux model wrote on Rozetka in June 2026 that the Wi-Fi worked properly only through the Russian app. Mysterium VPN has asked CladSwiss which app buyers in Ukraine and the EU are pointed to.

The Russian app knows this boiler by name. Its Google Play page shows an “Electrolux SmartInverter” in the first screenshot, and version 1.20.2 of the Android app lists 118 devices, 19 of them Electrolux, including the series named in the manual. Its privacy policy names R-Klimat LLC of Moscow as the operator and says personal data stays “exclusively within the territory” of Russia, under Russian law.

“The privacy policy bundled in the app names R-Klimat LLC of Moscow as the operator and says the app stores users’ personal information “exclusively within the territory” of Russia, under Russian law. The app is written to send sign-in details (a phone number or email address) and the name and room of each device to Rusklimat servers. It keeps device history on Yandex Cloud and sends the phone’s location to a Hommyn weather service.” continues the report. “The app also carries one fixed username and password for Rusklimat’s device server, the same in every copy. We didn’t use them and are not publishing them.”

Mysterium VPN didn’t use the credentials and won’t publish them. Neither will I.

The brand’s website points the same way. English manuals send buyers to home-comfort.com, which gives CladSwiss, at an address in Baar, Switzerland, as its contact. Its contact form asks visitors, in Russian, to let a company called RK-Region process their data under Russian federal law 152-FZ. Rusklimat’s shop names RK-Region in its payment details, and ballu.ru carries the same consent text.

Swiss register data show CladSwiss was entered in December 2019 to trade technical equipment, especially air-conditioning units. Mysterium VPN found nothing that shows who owns the company or how it connects to Rusklimat beyond these records.

What can the server see? CladSwiss’s Data Act declaration lists whether a device is on, its temperature, its operating mode and its power level, about 1 MB a day on standby and 3 MB in use. The privacy policy adds the phone’s identifier, its IP address and the names of Wi-Fi networks, plus location if the user agrees.

“A record of when a household heats water shows when people are at home. The line that carries those reports also carries commands to switch the boiler on or change its temperature. For a boiler tied to mqtt.cloud.rusklimat.ru, that line ends at a Russian company’s server in Moscow.” Mysterium VPN states. “Russia isn’t among the countries the European Commission recognizes as protecting personal data adequately, and Ukraine first ordered its internet providers to block Yandex’s services in 2017.”

The same line also carries commands to switch the boiler on or change its temperature. Mysterium VPN notes that Russia isn’t among the countries the European Commission recognizes as protecting personal data adequately.

One claim remains untested. Developers who studied a Ballu ventilation unit from the same family wrote that every unit shipped with the same cloud login and didn’t check the server’s certificate. Mysterium VPN hasn’t tested that and doesn’t know whether it applies to the boiler.

Mysterium VPN sent questions to AB Electrolux, CladSwiss and Rozetka on November 25, and none had replied at publication. AB Electrolux sold its Russian business in 2022 and said then that it had no production in Russia and wouldn’t supply the company it sold. Rusklimat’s Russian shop sells heaters under the Electrolux name today.

Mysterium VPN stresses that its findings are limited. Researchers did not connect to the servers or examine the boiler itself. They only reviewed the Hommyn app’s code, so they cannot confirm what data the device actually sends.

Users can check their router logs for connections to “rusklimat” or use DNS monitoring tools such as Pi-hole, AdGuard Home or NextDNS. Blocking the Russian server stops remote access, although local control may still work over Wi-Fi. Users can also disable the boiler’s Wi-Fi and place smart appliances on a separate guest network to keep them away from computers and phones. These checks also apply to Hommyn and ClimatOn devices from Ballu and Zanussi.

“A VPN on your phone or laptop doesn’t change where an appliance on your Wi-Fi sends its data, so the fix here sits at the router.” concludes the report.

[23] Security Affairs - GT13: AI-Assisted Hackers Target 22 Organizations Through Check Point Servers
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Wed, 25 Nov 2026 10:47:10 +0000
Link: https://securityaffairs.com/203946/hacking/gt13-ai-assisted-hackers-target-22-organizations-through-check-point-servers.html

[TAM METİN - 1254 kelime]
GT13: AI-Assisted Hackers Target 22 Organizations Through Check Point Servers

GreyNoise tracks GT13, an unattributed, probably AI-assisted campaign that hit 22 organizations via Check Point management servers. It may still be running.

GreyNoise detailed an intrusion campaign against Check Point Security Management Servers. The company confirmed attacks between October 17 and November 2, 2026, and counts at least 22 victim organizations. It tracks the activity as GreyNoise Threat 13, or GT13.

“GreyNoise recently discovered an intrusion campaign targeting Check Point Security Management Servers, with confirmed attack activity between October 17th and November 2nd, 2026, impacting at least 22 distinct organizations globally.” reads the report published by GreyNoise.

The victims don’t share a country or an industry. They have assets in the United Kingdom, Ireland, France, the Netherlands, Greece, Germany, Austria, Bulgaria, Poland, India, Thailand, Vietnam, South Korea, Hong Kong, China, Australia and the United States. The sectors include financial services, energy, government, transportation, mining, engineering and manufacturing.

GreyNoise cannot attribute the campaign to any known or publicly tracked threat group. The company also says it lacks enough information to identify the attackers’ motives or country of origin.

GreyNoise tracked most of the activity through its Global Observation Grid, a network of sensors designed to attract and monitor scanning and exploitation attempts. These sensors work much like honeypots, but the company says they offer broader coverage. They allow researchers to observe entire attacks as they happen, rather than relying only on reports from victims.

The attackers used 99 IP addresses, many of them temporary. These addresses belonged either to ExpressVPN exit nodes or to compromised Check Point devices. GreyNoise recorded more than 1,400 network sessions, 800 command-line artifacts, and 1,200 files that were downloaded, uploaded, or created.

The download failed. The attackers checked running processes, tested the download link with curl, and inspected the network settings. They could not work out why the implant had failed to download, so they returned the next day and used a direct SSH connection to continue setting up their relay. GreyNoise believes this failure may point to problems in a multi-agent system, suggesting that even automated tools can run into issues.

On October 20, the attackers created a script to start an HTTP server on the compromised machine without requiring authentication. They then began uploading files. The first archive contained the results of a network route scan from a Check Point Security Management Server, showing that the attackers had already gained access to that system. They then installed Nmap and Chisel and configured Chisel to work as a reverse proxy.

That made the HFS server a relay, or “hop” in GreyNoise’s wording. The actor used it to run its operations against Check Point devices it had compromised earlier, and to store the results. GreyNoise couldn’t determine when or how the actor got into those Check Point systems, only that it was there before the HFS compromise.

Between October 20 and 26, the actor had a compromised machine in one victim’s network, which GreyNoise calls Victim A, download Chisel and run it as a client. Through that tunnel, a script for CVE-2026-16232 (a Check Point SmartConsole authentication bypass that leads to Run Script) launched more than 100 management tasks on several management servers. The tasks copied the firewall database and the internal certificate authority, minted a new certificate called pivot.p12, and unloaded security policies, which would switch the firewall off.

The actor also probed Citrix NetScaler, Ivanti Connect Secure, VMware vCenter, Microsoft Exchange, Oracle TNS and several domain controllers. It ran exploits for CVE-2020-1472 (Netlogon), CVE-2023-4966 (NetScaler), CVE-2024-21893 (Ivanti), CVE-2021-26855 and CVE-2021-34473 (Exchange), and CVE-2021-21972 and CVE-2023-34048 (vCenter). GreyNoise also lists CVE-2025-5777, CVE-2026-41089 and CVE-2026-98432 among the flaws the actor used or tried to use.

“The actor used Nmap, Chisel, Impacket, Paramiko and other Python tooling to perform intrusion activities against their victim assets, including attempts to perform internal reconnaissance, privilege escalation, and lateral movement.” continues the report. “The bulk of the adversary commands and interaction is too voluminous to dig into here, but we can expose some of the adversary’s TTPs by taking a glimpse at their tooling. The actor used at least 30 Python scripts, many of which were helpers or launchers for other scripts, and several of them were probes for vulnerabilities or runners of exploits. Excerpts are provided below to illustrate the more specialized tooling and workflows.”

Password guessing played a part too. Scripts named spray_portal.py, comb.py, ssh_comb.py and ssh_spray.py carried common, default and weak passwords, plus usernames and passwords themed to the victim. The actor either knew its target or guessed well.

When a second Check Point server wouldn’t report back to the hop, the actor wrote a pull script to steal its data through the first server. The script harvested credentials, internal CA files and the firewall configuration. It was named after the targeted device.

On October 26 at 02:17 UTC, a bulk certificate task went out to 24 compromised Check Point servers: Victim A’s and those of 21 other victims. Results came back to the hop as text files that listed victim IP addresses in increasing order, so a loop was clearly running through them. GreyNoise never saw the actor send the task, which points to another part of its infrastructure.

Every file showed an error, because the certificate task had failed. One message also flagged a preload error for /usr/lib/.libproc.so, registered in /etc/ld.so.preload, which may indicate an implant. GreyNoise saw no follow-up attempts, but the IP addresses in those files let it notify many victims, directly or through government partners.

Check Point’s position, as reported by The Hacker News, is that this isn’t a new weakness. Sergey Shykevich, its director of threat intelligence, said the activity “does not represent a new vulnerability or exploitation technique”, since CVE-2026-16232 was disclosed earlier and patches exist. The company wants customers to apply updates, follow its hardening guidance to restrict management access and look for suspicious activity.

By early November, other attackers had started scanning the hop and trying to abuse it. On November 2 at 02:28 UTC, the GT13 actor ran a script that checked the box for CVE-2026-98432, a Linux IPv4 use-after-free bug. It then used nftables to drop IPv4 packets with IP options that could trigger the flaw. The attacker did patch management on its relay, which is more than some victims managed.

GreyNoise doesn’t read much into the clock. It saw no hands-on-keyboard activity between roughly 17:00 and 00:19 UTC, and nothing in the 05:00 hour, which fits a UTC+7 or UTC+8 workday. The malware domain newpayapp[.]com was registered on March 13, 2026, with the registrant state “Hai Phong” and country code “VN”, and GreyNoise treats that as a possible coincidence. The infrastructure clearly existed long before anyone noticed it.

GreyNoise believes GT13 used AI tools to find vulnerable systems, develop exploits and automate attacks, although human operators were also involved. The company is sharing information with authorities and CERTs. Its monitoring stopped on November 2, but it warns that the campaign may still be active because attackers retain access to compromised Check Point devices.

“We suspect the GT13 actor has a mature AI-enabled offensive apparatus. We believe the actor has separate agentic harnesses and systems weaponizing vulnerabilities, scanning for vulnerable assets, and automatically exploiting them to gather sets of systems that can be later used for operational relays or true targets.” concludes the report. “We suspect some of the foibles in the timeline reflect agentic offensive operations.”

Follow me on Twitter: @securityaffairs and Facebook and Mastodon

────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

[25] Cyberscoop - FinCEN investigates cybersecurity incident, takes systems offline
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Wed, 25 Nov 2026 13:03:24 +0000
Link: https://cyberscoop.com/fincen-investigates-cybersecurity-incident-systems-offline/

[TAM METİN - 447 kelime]
FinCEN investigates cybersecurity incident, takes systems offline

The Treasury Department’s Financial Crimes Enforcement Network is investigating a cybersecurity incident in which an unauthorized party gained access to a limited subset of its systems, the agency said in a notice posted to its Bank Secrecy Act filing portal.

FinCEN, the federal government’s financial intelligence unit, said it took some systems offline as a precaution while the investigation continues. The agency did not say how the intrusion occurred, how long it lasted or who was behind it.A source told CyberScoop that the FBI, CISA, and third-party forensic examiners are working to determine the scope and impact of the incident. As of today, there is no evidence of any breach of beneficial ownership databases.

The BSA E-Filing System, the portal at the center of the disruption, is FinCEN’s web-based platform for submitting reports required under the Bank Secrecy Act, the 1970 law that forms the backbone of U.S. anti-money-laundering policy. Banks, credit unions, brokerages, casinos and other financial institutions use it to transmit suspicious activity reports, currency transaction reports and related filings electronically through a secure network.

FinCEN said the filing website itself remains operational, though some services may be temporarily unavailable. The agency said it is extending deadlines for filers affected by the disruption.

The agency also said its Financial Institutions Hotline is unaffected by the disruption and remains available to financial institutions that need to expedite delivery of suspicious activity information to law enforcement.

The incident places a temporary strain on infrastructure that the financial sector relies on to meet statutory reporting obligations. Suspicious activity reports filed through the system feed the database that FinCEN uses to support criminal, tax and national security investigations, and delays in filings can slow the flow of information to federal authorities.

It was not immediately clear how long the affected systems would remain offline. FinCEN said it is working to return all systems to normal operations as soon as possible. The agency has not said whether any data was taken or altered.

The incident was announced the same day the CIA and Office of the Director of National Intelligence discovered a data breach of an open-source intelligence analysis system. It is unclear if the two incidents are related.

[26] The Register - The security of US water systems is even leakier than we thought
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Wed, 25 Nov 2026 20:40:17 +0100
Link: https://www.theregister.com/security/2026/11/25/the-security-of-us-water-systems-is-even-leakier-than-we-thought/5312243

[TAM METİN - 669 kelime]
The security of US water systems is even leakier than we thought

And intruders don't always need to exploit bugs to break in

Feds warn grocers: Don't chill on cyber defenses as freezer hacking spree heats up
20 days ago

Cybercrims go beyond stealing data to attack physical building systems
October 17, 2026

Researchers warned attackers could 'defrost the food' months before French supermarkets got hit
October 15, 2026

AI systems are fully capable of carrying out nightmare attacks against infrastructure and nobody's ready
October 11, 2026

'Not a theoretical risk,' feds warn as attackers use AI-made code to hack critical infrastructure controllers 
August 20, 2026

American water systems are even less secure than we thought, according to a study of 236 technology vendors that provide gear to US drinking water, wastewater, and industrial water systems.

The federal government has issued 4,014 security advisories for industrial control systems and cataloged 1,792 vulnerabilities known to have been exploited in cyberattacks. A new index, called Water Supply Chain Watch and published by exposure management firm Securin, however, found almost twice as many vulnerabilities as public records identified – including 20 exploited flaws that weren't even in the federal government's Known Exploited Vulnerabilities (KEV) catalog.

The security provider compared data on water-tech vendors with records from the US Cybersecurity and Infrastructure Security Agency (CISA), the Environmental Protection Agency (EPA), the FBI, and WaterISAC, drawing on CISA advisory data through November 20 and KEV catalog entries through November 23.

It found that federal water advisories hit their highest level this year since 2020.

However, the volume of security alerts isn't a reliable predictor of real-world attacks, the researchers noted.

Five vendors – Rockwell Automation (58), Advantech (48), Schneider Electric (35), Siemens (26), and ABB (24) – account for 191 of the 376 water-tagged security advisories. The No. 2 firm, Advantech, accounted for the highest number of vulnerabilities rated critical, but none of the CVEs in its advisories appear in CISA's KEV catalog. Unitronics, on the other hand, accounted for only two water-tagged security advisories, but at least 75 of its controllers were compromised between November 2023 and January 2024 by attackers taking advantage of default or missing passwords.

The report also highlights another disconnect between water-related CVEs and real-world cyberattacks, which don't always require attackers to exploit software bugs. The July intrusions, which security researchers suspect were the work of Iran-linked attackers, targeted more than 100 water systems, with incidents reported across at least 12 states, and involved poorly secured internet-connected controllers.

According to Securin, 791 Rockwell MicroLogix 1400 controllers across multiple industries in the US were exposed to the internet as of November 17.

Additionally, research by Forescout cited by the security shop found that most exposed Rockwell controllers in the US – seven in 10 – sit on mobile carrier networks, where poorly secured cellular modems can provide an entry point for attackers. A CISA advisory from July describes this technique:

The authoring agencies observed Iranian-affiliated APT actors using several foreign-based IP addresses to access internet-facing PLCs manufactured by Rockwell Automation/Allen-Bradley, Schneider Electric, Siemens, and potentially other manufactured PLCs [T0883]. The actors used leased, third-party hosted infrastructure and manufacturers’ PLC programming software to connect to misconfigured victim PLCs. Inbound malicious traffic has been observed targeting PLC devices on the following ports: 44818, 2222, 102, and 502, as well as targeting modems on port 22.

These cellular modems, often installed by third-party contractors, frequently aren’t documented.

Unsurprisingly, the top recommendation for water utilities is to find every cellular modem in the environment, including those installed by contractors, make sure they aren’t exposed to the open internet, and put a gateway with multifactor authentication (MFA) in front of them to stop would-be intruders from gaining access via the modem and controller combo.

“Water utilities are spending time and money fixing the vulnerabilities they're told to worry about,” Securin CEO Srinivas Mukkamala said in a statement. “Meanwhile, attackers are getting in through equipment nobody knew was connected or passwords nobody bothered to change.” ®

[27] The Register - Microsoft's NTLM security changes trip up third-party logins
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Wed, 25 Nov 2026 18:01:00 +0100
Link: https://www.theregister.com/security/2026/11/25/microsofts-ntlm-security-changes-trip-up-third-party-logins/5312085

[TAM METİN - 466 kelime]
Microsoft's NTLM security changes trip up third-party logins

It's not our fault if you've implemented it wrong

Microsoft fixes Office dialog that had nothing to say and wouldn't go away
16 hours ago

Microsoft 365 Backup stretches recovery window to ten years
19 hours ago

Microsoft refreshes Copilot app search for users without an AI subscription
1 day ago

Microsoft mega outage: Report reveals testing gaps and emails that never arrived
2 days ago

Everything going down at once wasn't evidence of a conspiracy – but the truth may be scarier
2 days ago

Microsoft's November security updates can break authentication in some third-party applications and devices following changes to NTLM message signing.

Affected systems include authentication proxy servers, single sign-on (SSO) products, and application packaging tools, the company's support notice says.

The failures stem from products that claim to supply required information during NTLM authentication but return incomplete or empty data, Microsoft said: "An affected device might set the NTLMSSP_NEGOTIATE_TARGET_INFO flag in its CHALLENGE_MESSAGE but provide an empty or incomplete TargetInfo field."

Administrators can use the notice's network trace and event log examples to identify affected products. For confirmed cases, the temporary workaround is to set the ClientAllowUnsignedNtlm Registry value to 1 on affected clients. This stops the client offering signing, backing out the new default rather than fixing the incompatible product. The recommended long-term options are a vendor update or Kerberos authentication, where supported.

The value sits under HKEY_LOCAL_MACHINE\SYSTEM\CurrentControlSet\Control\Lsa\MSV1_0 and defaults to 0, enabling clients to offer signing on non-anonymous connections.

The NTLM changes arrived in the November 10 update as phase 1 in a 2-phase process. In phase 1, "Windows client devices begin offering NTLM signing on all connections by default. Servers accept both signed and unsigned clients. Auditing events identify clients not using signing."

Phase 2, scheduled for May 2027, will make Windows servers require NTLM signing by default. "Clients that do not offer signing will be rejected by secure servers," Microsoft stated.

Ominously, Microsoft warned that it "might remove the registry keys at a future date," so the company is not kidding about the temporary workaround thing.

The additional protections were added to address CVE-2026-118737, a vulnerability in which "improper verification of cryptographic signature in Windows Remote Procedure Call Runtime allows an unauthorized attacker to execute code over a network."

NTLM is on Microsoft's hit list, having joined the Deprecated Features list in 2024. It dates back to the era of Windows NT, and Microsoft has made clear that it wants customers to move to Kerberos, which became the default in 2000. Even so, NTLM lingers on.

Deprecation doesn't remove Microsoft's obligation to secure NTLM while it remains in supported Windows releases. For administrators, though, the immediate task is identifying incompatible products before temporary exceptions give way to stricter enforcement. ®

[28] The Register - US Treasury financial crimes unit becomes latest fed breach victim
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Wed, 25 Nov 2026 17:40:16 +0100
Link: https://www.theregister.com/security/2026/11/25/us-treasury-financial-crimes-unit-becomes-latest-fed-breach-victim/5312165

[TAM METİN - 493 kelime]
US Treasury financial crimes unit becomes latest fed breach victim

FinCEN takes systems offline and extends filing deadlines while investigating 'unauthorized access'

Gatwick drop-off customers' personal data exposed in supplier breach
18 hours ago

'We are not ShinyHunters,' says criminal hacking crew that looks and acts just like ShinyHunters
1 day ago

Orgs that leak data 'prioritize corporate interests over customers' says Have I Been Pwned’s Troy Hunt
2 days ago

Cleverbridge admits attackers stole customers' credit card and CVV numbers. Yep, you read that right
2 days ago

Visa, Mastercard theft included info useful for phishing, but not draining bank accounts, say researchers
5 days ago

The US Treasury's Financial Crimes Enforcement Network (FinCEN) has taken some systems offline following unauthorized access, disrupting regulatory filings for financial institutions.

An alert has gone up on FinCEN's Bank Secrecy Act E-Filing website, as shown in this archived copy, informing those who need to submit reports that they might not be able to while the agency deals with the situation. FinCEN not only enforces compliance with the Bank Secrecy Act and associated regulations but also collects and analyzes financial intelligence for sharing with other US agencies and foreign government partners.

In other words, it has some juicy info in its databases.

“FinCEN is investigating a cybersecurity incident that resulted in unauthorized access to a limited subset of its systems,” the alert states. “As a proactive measure, FinCEN has taken some systems offline.”

FinCEN hasn't said which systems intruders accessed, but clues in the deadline extension notice the agency posted alongside the warning suggest the disruption affects its reporting services for banks and other financial institutions required to submit reports detailing certain financial transactions and suspicious activity that may indicate money laundering or other illicit activity.

FinCEN's Secure Data Transfer Mode, one method of submitting those reports, appears to be affected, as do services companies use to submit beneficial ownership information reports and individuals use to update their FinCEN identifiers. Eligible organizations and individuals with filings or updates due from November 18 through December 29 now have until December 30 to submit them.

The Register has learned that there’s no evidence of any unauthorized access to Bank Secrecy Act data or beneficial ownership databases. That said, the investigation is ongoing - so that could change.

“Treasury is working with the Federal Bureau of Investigation, the Cybersecurity and Infrastructure Security Agency, and third-party forensic examiners to determine the scope and impact of the incident,” a FinCEN official told us in an email.

Several US government agencies have experienced cybersecurity incidents in recent months, including the ATF and the FBI, and reports emerged this week that intruders had also compromised an unclassified CIA system. While US authorities have linked previous cyberespionage campaigns to Chinese or other government-backed groups, financially motivated criminal groups have also targeted federal agencies.

As the FinCEN breach is still under investigation, the agency hasn’t stated who may be involved and for what purpose.  ®

[29] The Register - Gatwick drop-off customers' personal data exposed in supplier breach
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Wed, 25 Nov 2026 12:16:18 +0100
Link: https://www.theregister.com/security/2026/11/25/gatwick-drop-off-customers-personal-data-exposed-in-supplier-breach/5312060

[TAM METİN - 561 kelime]
Gatwick drop-off customers' personal data exposed in supplier breach

AutoPay login and vehicle records affected, alongside limited payment information and customer service emails

US Treasury financial crimes unit becomes latest fed breach victim
13 hours ago

Orgs that leak data 'prioritize corporate interests over customers' says Have I Been Pwned’s Troy Hunt
2 days ago

Cleverbridge admits attackers stole customers' credit card and CVV numbers. Yep, you read that right
2 days ago

Visa, Mastercard theft included info useful for phishing, but not draining bank accounts, say researchers
5 days ago

2013 breach at Russian cybercrime forum resurfaces with millions of victims' credentials
14 days ago

London Gatwick Airport has warned customers that a breach of its drop-off payment service exposed personal information and, in limited cases, payment records.

A notification email seen by The Register identifies the affected system as AutoPay, operated by ParkPark and used by Gatwick's drop-off zone manager, Times24 UK Limited.

AutoPay lets drivers register their vehicle and payment details in advance so drop-off charges are collected automatically.

Gatwick told us it was unable to provide details about how many customers were affected by the breach, or how far back the affected records go.

The email said most affected customers would have only some of the listed data exposed. The could include names, home addresses, cities, postcodes, contact details, and AutoPay login credentials such as email addresses and bcrypt-hashed passwords were affected.

Other records implicated in the breach include details of vehicles linked to AutoPay accounts, those vehicles' entry and exit times at Times24-managed parking locations, and vehicle registrations.

The email also states that payment records, including amounts, card types, card expiry dates, payment references, transaction statuses, transaction IDs, and drop-off locations and times were affected, but a Gatwick spokesperson said this applied only to "very limited cases."

Again, in a minority of cases, "some limited emails between the customer service team and customers" were also exposed, the email stated.

Gatwick said Times24 "immediately suspended" AutoPay after detecting the intrusion. The service remains disabled while the investigation continues.

Airport officials are overseeing the response with outside cybersecurity specialists. Gatwick warned of possible disruption to its website but said flights were unaffected.

Customers can still use Gatwick's drop-off zones, although they'll have to use alternative payment methods, and all other airport services are still operating as usual.

The notification email urged customers to exercise caution when receiving communications from Gatwick, Times24, AutoPay, or ParkPark, especially those that ask for payment or bank details.

Times24, which operates across more than 200 car parks in the UK, did not immediately respond to our request for more information.

Dray Agha, senior manager at Huntress's tactical response division, warned that criminals could use the data for convincing scams long after news coverage of the breach has faded.

"The real damage often comes after the breach," he said. "Criminals rarely use stolen data straight away; they'll wait until the headlines fade, then send a text about a 'parking fine' or an email offering a 'refund' that references a real booking. Even partial card details add a layer of credibility that makes people far more likely to hand over the rest.

"Gatwick customers should assume any unexpected message about their parking could be a scam, check directly with the airport, and keep a close eye on their bank statements over the coming months." ®

[30] TechCrunch Security - 1Password said hackers broke into its internal systems, but customer passwords are safe
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Wed, 25 Nov 2026 18:49:52 +0000
Link: https://techcrunch.com/2026/11/25/1password-said-hackers-broke-into-its-internal-systems-but-customer-passwords-are-safe/

[TAM METİN - 624 kelime]
Password manager maker 1Password has confirmed that some customer information was taken in a recent intrusion into its internal systems, but that customer password vaults and other secrets were not accessed.

The Toronto-based tech company said in a blog post published Wednesday that it detected an intrusion on November 19, in which an attacker gained access to an employee’s corporate account with GitHub, which the company uses to share and store its source code.

The attacker used that employee’s access to push malicious code to the company’s internal servers, which allowed the hacker to download some data about 1Password customers, including their names and the email address that they use for logging into their account. The company said that the attacker also took details about when a 1Password account was created and when a user last logged in.

1Password said that customer vaults, which store their passwords, were “at no point” available to the attacker. The company also said that it doesn’t have access to customer account passwords or their secret keys, which are used to scramble their passwords so that 1Password cannot see them.

In addition, the company said that its apps and extensions are unaffected by the incident.

It’s unclear how the attacker gained access to the employee’s corporate account, and a spokesperson for 1Password did not immediately respond to TechCrunch’s questions about the incident.

1Password did not say whether it had notified customers whose information was taken in the breach, or how many customers had information taken. We’ve also asked if the company has received any communication or demands from the hackers. We’ll update if we hear back from 1Password.

The company touts millions of customers and more than 200,000 businesses who use its password manager products to store their passwords, credentials, payment card information, and more. Security experts have long recommended password managers as a better way of generating stronger credentials and keeping track of online accounts.

[31] TechCrunch Security - DOJ accuses former cyber executive of defrauding ransomware victims by secretly paying hackers
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Wed, 25 Nov 2026 17:36:51 +0000
Link: https://techcrunch.com/2026/11/25/doj-accuses-former-cyber-executive-of-defrauding-ransomware-victims-by-secretly-paying-hackers/

[TAM METİN - 715 kelime]
The U.S. Department of Justice has indicted a former executive of a ransomware recovery firm after he allegedly defrauded victims of cyberattacks by claiming the company could get their encrypted files back.

According to federal prosecutors, Juan Ricardo Palacio Escobar, 52, was the chief technology officer at RansomwareHelp, which purported to offer data recovery services to victims of ransomware. The company’s website, which currently reads as “under maintenance,” claimed it could decrypt hacked data by using its proprietary technology, according to an archived copy of the site from earlier this year. RansomwareHelp touted a success rate of “over 99%.”

But the company allegedly had no technical ability to unscramble data and instead “negotiated with and paid a ransom to the ransomware actors to decrypt the victim data,” while charging the ransomware victim more and keeping the difference,” per the Justice Department’s statement.

Palacio Escobar then allegedly used the funds to buy several properties in Florida.

The DOJ said the FBI carried out an undercover operation in June, in part posing as a company that claimed it had been hit by ransomware. The company claimed to the undercover agents that it never negotiated with hackers or paid a ransom. But then Palacio Escobar paid the apparent hackers some $6,000 in cryptocurrency, without the knowledge that FBI agents were also posing as the ransomware hackers.

404 Media first reported the indictment last week against Palacio Escobar. He faces three charges of wire fraud, and could receive 20 years in prison on each count.

Palacio Escobar is the latest, albeit rare, example of cybersecurity professionals going rogue. Earlier this year, two ransomware negotiators working for cybersecurity companies were jailed on hacking and extortion charges, after being caught moonlighting with the ransomware gangs that they were ostensibly investigating. A third ransomware negotiator was also convicted of helping hackers deploy ransomware against several companies.

Last month, independent cybersecurity journalist Brian Krebs reported that FBI agents arrested a Canadian cybersecurity executive in Philadelphia on allegations that he was involved with the theft of FBI personnel records by the hacking and extortion gang ShinyHunters. The hacking group told TechCrunch that they took credit for the theft of FBI data in September, which they said they would not publicly release, a departure from its earlier extortion-based cyberattacks.

The federal charges against the Canadian cybersecurity executive relate to conspiracy to commit extortion, though much of the court docket — and his alleged links to the hacking group — remains under seal.

[32] TechCrunch Security - Allstate suffers multiday tech outage ahead of Thanksgiving
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Wed, 25 Nov 2026 15:08:37 +0000
Link: https://techcrunch.com/2026/11/25/allstate-suffers-multi-day-tech-outage-ahead-of-thanksgiving/

[TAM METİN - 718 kelime]
U.S. insurer Allstate has been experiencing a tech outage since Monday morning, leaving customers nationwide unable to access their accounts, review their policy information, file claims, pay their bills, and more, just ahead of the U.S. Thanksgiving holidays.

The outage has been impacting both the company’s website and mobile app, TechCrunch confirmed.

The breakdown follows a report from earlier this month that Allstate was planning to lay off up to 10% of its staff as part of an AI-driven efficiency push. (The company had not confirmed the report, which was attributed to unnamed sources.)

Allstate’s outage is the latest in a series of disruptions to services such as Microsoft Outlook and Teams, the airlines, Apple Pay, and several banks. Trump administration officials have said the outages are not being caused by cyberattacks, but are rather being caused by a recent set of upgrades to systems, many of which run on older technology. Those systems are now in need of more frequent security and bug fixes as AI makes it easier and faster to discover vulnerabilities.

At present, a message on Allstate’s website advises customers trying to log in that its services aren’t available, and that the company is “working to restore access as soon as possible.” On X, Allstate is employing what appear to be automated replies to customer complaints, asking them to DM the Allstate account with more information.

Downdetector, a crowdsourced outage-reporting website, shows users began reporting Allstate’s outage from Monday morning. However, the company hasn’t yet issued a formal statement about the disruption, what’s causing it, or when it may be resolved. In the meantime, local insurance agents have been fielding calls and emails from customers who aren’t able to use the company’s online services.

The timing of the outage is particularly bad given that Thanksgiving is a big travel holiday in the U.S., which means there will be car accidents. Customers will be left without a way to get in touch with their auto insurer to file claims and get roadside assistance through Allstate’s app.

Allstate did not immediately return a request for comment. On Wednesday evening, after publication, the company sent the following statement (below). It did not answer questions about the outage’s cause or planned resolution.

Allstate customers can reach us in many ways, including online, by phone and through local offices. Some access has been restricted over the last 48 hours, but we continue to be available for customers and respond to and settle claims. To the extent service interruptions impact a customer’s ability to make a payment, they won’t lose coverage or be charged late fees.

Updated after publication with Allstate’s comment.
