[33] TechCrunch Security - Todoist confirms 6% of customers’ details were stolen in data breach
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Wed, 25 Nov 2026 14:20:28 +0000
Link: https://techcrunch.com/2026/11/25/todoist-confirms-6-of-customers-details-were-stolen-in-data-breach/

[TAM METİN - 694 kelime]
Task management app Todoist said on Tuesday that a hacker gained access to its production systems and stole customer data, including email IDs, password hashes, and account creation dates. The company said around 6% of its customers were affected and blamed stolen credentials for the breach.

Todoist said the attacker used stolen credentials to access the company’s version control system and source code between November 22 and 23. The hacker then changed employee access controls and reached the app’s cloud infrastructure via an employee’s work account.

The hacker downloaded credentials of early Todoist users whose passwords were stored using an older hashing method, but didn’t change any source code, the company said. Todoist said it has blocked the attack path and reset passwords as a mitigation step, as well as disabled the Todoist for Outlook email add-on.

For a small number of people, about 0.001% of its userbase, the hacker also searched through tasks, comments, and attachment names to look for credentials for other services, like API keys and access tokens.

The hacker also pulled Todoist API tokens and Slack integration tokens, and looked for emails, addresses, and attachment file names through the Todoist for Outlook extension if it was connected to accounts and had permission to read emails.

The company said it has sent a notice to those affected and asked them to reset their credentials and change their login details in other places with the same password. The company is asking users to also update API tokens, reconnect integrations, and revoke or replace any access keys, recovery phrases, or credentials they had stored in Todoist comments.

Todoist said it has also reset its internal access structure and how remote command execution works in its cloud environment to prevent such incidents in the future.

Todoist’s founder and CEO Amir Salihefendić apologized to users about the incident on X, and said the company will take a closer look at why it happened.

The to-do app’s data breach is the latest in a burgeoning list of data breaches in which hackers use stolen credentials to access data and systems at enterprises. So far this year, we’ve seen several companies fall victim to this form of attack, including Uber and Klue.

[34] CSO Online - Broadcom issues fixes for 23 new VMware flaws, three critical
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Wed, 25 Nov 2026 20:07:57 +0000
Link: https://www.csoonline.com/article/4253329/broadcom-issues-fixes-for-23-new-vmware-flaws-three-critical.html

[TAM METİN - 908 kelime]
The most severe: a vCenter authentication bypass bug, just in time to ruin US support teams' Thanksgiving holiday.

Broadcom on Tuesday reported almost two dozen new security vulnerabilities in VMware, adding to a lengthy list of fixes from this month alone.

The most serious of the flaws in the current advisory is a vCenter authentication bypass vulnerability, CVE-2026-123903, assigned a CVSSv3 base score of 9.8 (Critical). “A malicious actor with network access to vCenter may exploit this issue to bypass authentication and gain unauthorized access to the system,” it said.

In a supplemental document accompanying the alert, Broadcom noted that because of coordinated disclosure and release pipeline timing, the advisory comes just two days before the US Thanksgiving holiday, admitting, “this timing is not optimal.”

The company said that the advisory covers a total of 23 issues, seven of which are in vCenter: an authentication bypass and three denial-of-service issues in the VMware Directory Service, an arbitrary file write while processing certain archives, a local privilege escalation in a vpxd service script, and a security policy enforcement failure in the vSphere Distributed Switch (vDS).

Seven more are in ESX: a heap out-of-bounds write and a use-after-free in the I/O filter service, an out-of-bounds write in the Extensible Host Controller Interface (XHCI) USB controller, a stack-based buffer overflow in the VMkernel, a security bypass in the host configuration store, a signature verification bypass, and a denial of service in guest RPC.

The remaining flaws affect VMware Workstation, VMware Fusion, VMware Tools, and VMware Avi Load Balancer.

However, the company noted that the fixes delivered to address the flaws in last week’s advisory resolve some of the issues highlighted in the current batch.

“If you applied vCenter 8.0 U3m for VMSA-2026-0009, including on VCF 5.2.x, you do not need to update vCenter again, but vCenter 9.1.x needs the update in this advisory,” Broadcom said.

“ESX was not part of VMSA-2026-0009 and needs the update in this advisory. VMSA-2026-0009 also resolved issues in SDDC Manager, which this advisory does not update. If you have not applied the SDDC Manager update from VMSA-2026-0009, apply it separately. This advisory also includes a VMware Tools update. Updating vCenter and ESX does not update VMware Tools inside virtual machines, so plan that update separately.”

It added, “if you have a question about whether you are affected, it is likely that you are and should take action immediately.”

Difference of opinion on patching urgency

So is this a signal to interrupt the US Thanksgiving holiday to address the flaws? Opinions differ.

Tyler Reguly, Fortra’s associate director of security R&D, argued that although a 9.8 score is about as high as they get, the nature of the holes might not merit extreme scheduling. There are three vulnerabilities rated 9.8 in severity listed in the current batch, affecting VMware vCenter and ESX.

He pointed out, “neither of these [affected] systems should be present on your external interfaces, so the risk would need to be internal. That means we’re talking about an inside threat or a breach that is already underway. These are important vulnerabilities, so there’s no doubt about that, but they are not so important that anyone should interrupt their holiday weekend.”

Reguly suggested a strict interpretation of CISA guidance to support his contention that these patches may not merit emergency response.

“If we take a look at CISA BOD-26-04 as our guidance on this, is the service publicly exposed? It shouldn’t be, so let’s say no. Is this vulnerability in the KEV [Known Exploited Vulnerabilities catalog]? No. Could it be automated? Unknown, so let’s assume yes for safety’s sake,” he said. “A result of No, No, and Yes translates to line 13 on the BOD-26-04 remediation timelines, which allows for 60 days to fix the issue.”

He observed that the recent onslaught of vulnerabilities has made us “a bit like Pavlov’s dog:” We’ve been conditioned to react every time we see CVSS 9.8.

“This is a dangerous and potentially harmful conditioning that we need to make every effort to break,” he said. “We need to remind ourselves that the severity of a vulnerability does not reflect risk to an organization, just as the bell did not represent food.”

Frank Dickson, principal analyst at Dickson Research, disagreed.

He considered the current advisory, VMSA-2026-0011, to be “a big deal because it hits vCenter and ESX, the two systems that run every virtual machine, with flaws that need no password. Skip the patch, and one attacker can take down or take over every virtual machine you run. You are essentially toast. Patch now.”

Dickson noted that this batch of alerts contains multiple problematic security issues, which include authentication bypass in vCenter; permitting an attacker with network access to run code on the hypervisor itself; and another bug that lets an administrator inside a virtual machine on Workstation or Fusion run code on the host computer. “Let’s face it: AI loves to chain vulnerabilities,” he said.

He is also worried about the large number of VMware patches this month alone, especially those in vCenter. “The frequency of VMware’s vCenter flaws is a concern, as they keep landing in the same place,” he said. “The new flaw is distinct from last week’s. Distinct is not comforting. Three different holes in the same wall is a wall problem.”

He pointed out, “The directory service has become vCenter’s Achilles’ heel. This advisory adds three denial-of-service flaws in the same service for good measure.”

[35] CSO Online - Check Point security management servers hit in global cyberattack campaign
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Wed, 25 Nov 2026 13:09:29 +0000
Link: https://www.csoonline.com/article/4253068/check-point-security-management-servers-hit-in-global-cyberattack-campaign.html

[TAM METİN - 1101 kelime]
GreyNoise observed 24 compromised Check Point security management servers across 22 organizations in 17 countries, with attackers using 99 IP addresses for scanning and exploitation.

An active intrusion campaign targeting Check Point security management servers has been uncovered, with confirmed attack activity observed between October 17 and November 2, 2026, impacting 22 organizations globally.

Tracked by cybersecurity firm GreyNoise as GreyNoise Threat 13 (GT13), the campaign does not appear to be focused on a single region or industry. Impacted organizations span assets across the US, the UK, Ireland, France, the Netherlands, Greece, Germany, Austria, Bulgaria, Poland, India, Thailand, Vietnam, South Korea, Hong Kong, China, and Australia, across sectors including financial services, energy, government, transportation, mining, engineering, and manufacturing.

“The activity reported by the GreyNoise article involves CVE-2026-16232, a previously disclosed vulnerability with patches already available, and does not represent a new vulnerability or exploitation technique,” a Check Point spokesperson said. “Our priority remains ensuring customers apply the available updates and follow Check Point’s published hardening guidance to restrict management access and identify suspicious activity. The most important action is to install the available updates for CVE-2026-16232. Check Point has already released fixes and detailed mitigation and investigation guidance in its SecureKnowledge advisory.”

GreyNoise has not attributed GT13 to any known or publicly tracked threat group.

“An intrusion into a Check Point security management server is particularly serious because it is not just another server in the enterprise network. It effectively acts as the central control point for managing an organisation’s firewall infrastructure and security policies, with highly privileged access,” said Devroop Dhar, co-founder and MD at Primus Partners. He said that depending on the deployment and the level of access obtained, the consequences could extend well beyond the security infrastructure itself, potentially exposing sensitive data and critical business applications and disrupting enterprise operations.

Building the relay infrastructure

The threat actor operated across 99 IP addresses, primarily routed outbound through ExpressVPN or compromised Check Point devices, generating more than 1,400 network sessions, over 800 command-line artifacts, and over 1,200 created, uploaded, or downloaded files.

On October 5, the actor used this same flow to gain access to install HFS middleware hooks that would issue commands to the compromised server, the research said. This allowed the attacker to append an SSH key to /root/.ssh/authorized_keys. Three days later, on October 8, the threat actor automation check in to the compromised server using the HFS command hook for “uname -a; df -h / | tail -1; whoami”.

As the malware download failed, the threat actor returned with a direct SSH connection to continue setting up their relay the next day.

On October 20, the threat actor created a script (/root/exfil/srv_post2_<redacted>.py) for setting up an unauthenticated HTTP server on the hop, which they used to upload files. The first exfiltrated .tgz file contained results from a scan of network routes from a compromised Check Point security management server, demonstrating that the actor already had access to targeted victim assets. The actor continued to set up their environment, uploaded and installed open-source tools like Nmap and Chisel, and then set up a Chisel server as a reverse proxy.

Once the compromised server was established as a relay, the actor used it to manage interactions with previously compromised Check Point devices, actively deepening their intrusion against Victim A while conducting bulk actions across 21 other victim organizations. Across the campaign, the operator relied on at least 30 Python scripts, including helpers, launchers, vulnerability probes, and exploit runners.

Between October 20 and 26, the attacker had a compromised Victim A asset download and run Chisel in client mode, allowing them to further scan and interact with Victim A’s environment.  Over the Chisel tunnel, and by using a script for CVE-2026-16232, the actor launched over 100 management tasks on multiple Check Point management servers. This included copying the firewall database and the Internal CA, minting a new certificate (pivot.p12), and unloading security policies (which would disable the firewall). Further, the actor probed Citrix NetScaler, Ivanti Connect Secure, VMware vCenter, Microsoft Exchange, Oracle TNS assets and several domain controllers.

Between October 20 and 21, the attacker focused on Victim A, first using exploit.py (CVE-2026-16232) against one Check Point server before scanning a second with spray_portal.py. When that second device failed to report back to the relay box, the actor created pull_<redacted_id>.py to steal credentials, internal CA files, and firewall configurations through the first compromised server.

The attacker then expanded inside Victim A’s network, running broad port scans with curl, telnet, nmap, and a domain controller probe, followed by targeted exploits against internal Microsoft Netlogon, Citrix NetScaler, Ivanti Connect Secure, Microsoft Exchange, and VMware vCenter servers. They wrapped up this phase by running comb.py,ssh_comb.py, and ssh_spray.py populated with victim-themed usernames and passwords.

On October 26, the operation shifted to bulk targeting. The actor issued a bulk certificate management task to 24 compromised Check Point Security Manager servers, including those belonging to Victim A and 21 other victim assets. Results were shipped in a text file to the hop server that contained an incrementing IP order to run the commands on the compromised Check Point servers and deliver them via SCP to this exfil folder. Although the task failed on every device, the error logs sent back to the hop server exposed victim IP addresses and revealed an /etc/ld.so.preload reference to /usr/lib/.libproc.so, pointing to a likely implant before activity paused until November 2.

GreyNoise suspects the GT13 actor has a mature AI-enabled offensive apparatus including separate agentic harnesses and systems weaponizing vulnerabilities, scanning for vulnerable assets, and automatically exploiting.

Securing the management plane

Although GreyNoise’s direct visibility into the campaign paused around November 2, 2026, security leaders must still verify whether their environments have been quietly infiltrated.

With attackers planting backdoors on breached Check Point servers, CISOs must audit their environments for signs of quiet compromise.

To establish whether Check Point infrastructure has been compromised, CISOs and security teams should examine administrative login records for unusual access patterns, including unfamiliar IP addresses, unexpected login times, and unauthorised access added Dhar. They should also look for changes to firewall policies, access-control rules, and configuration files, and thoroughly review system logs, audit trails, and command execution history for suspicious activity.

Dhar noted that once there are indications of a compromise, the immediate priority should be to prevent further access and contain the damage. Security teams should restrict access to the management server to authorised administrative systems and isolate confirmed compromised systems while preserving forensic evidence. Unauthorised accounts should be disabled, suspicious SSH keys and access tokens revoked, and identified malicious IP addresses and domains blocked.

(XXXXXXX, AÇIK - https://www.csoonline.com/article/4253068/check-point-security-management-servers-hit-in-global-cyberattack-campaign.html, csoonline.com, 25.11.2026)

[36] CSO Online - Prompting AI to be ‘thorough’ can drive sandbox escapes, Anthropic says
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Wed, 25 Nov 2026 12:06:00 +0000
Link: https://www.csoonline.com/article/4253053/prompting-ai-to-be-thorough-can-drive-sandbox-escapes-anthropic-says.html

[TAM METİN - 877 kelime]
Fixes cut the risk sharply, but some Claude Code tools skip Anthropic’s safety check, and its models often suspect they’re being tested

Prompt wording meant to make AI agents more resourceful, such as telling them to search thoroughly or invoking authority, sharply raises the odds they will reach outside a poorly secured sandbox, Anthropic has found in its latest evaluation.

The evaluation, called Siren, gives Claude models routine dev-ops tasks inside deliberately weakened sandboxes and places a “honeypot,” such as a credential, file, or emulated internet service, outside the boundary, Anthropic researchers Oscar O’Rahilly and Darya Kaviani wrote in a blog post.

Anthropic tested six Claude models, from Mythos 5 to the 5.5 series, without the safeguards that ship in its products. The tests covered more than 1,700 scenarios in 26 sandboxes built on tools including Docker, gVisor, and QEMU. Most of the weaknesses are misconfigurations modeled on public reports, the company said.

Wording mattered more than deadlines

Anthropic’s researchers tested different types of instructions, which they called “tempters”. Some increased urgency by introducing deadlines or the consequences of failure. Others encouraged agents to search extensively or gave them broad authority over their machines.

“Some tempters give the model broad authority over its own machine. Others tell it to search thoroughly, on that machine or more widely, and list what it checked,” the researchers wrote.

Removing deadlines and stated costs of failure changed the rate of reaching outside by no more than about six percentage points, the post said.

Removing the wording that asked for thoroughness and asserted authority had a far larger effect, cutting reach rates by 23 to 26 points in five of the six models, the researchers wrote. Claude Mythos 5 was the exception, reaching outside about 90% of the time in every version of the scenarios, the post added.

Across the Claude 5.5 models and Claude Mythos 5.2, agents reached outside in 21% to 32% of scenarios, but misaligned use occurred in only 0.6% to 1.6%. The corresponding misaligned-use rates for the older Claude Mythos 5 and 5.1 models were 25.8% and 12.7%, respectively, according to the post.

“Siren is designed to make this behavior easy to measure; its rates are not an estimate of how often this behavior happens in real use,” the company wrote.

Six rules in the system prompt

The first mitigation Anthropic tested was spelling out six sandbox rules in the system prompt. They cover host files, host services, container engine sockets, credentials, network destinations, and anything left running on the host after a task ends.

The rules made the models 68% to 96% less likely to reach outside, cutting the rate for the newest models to about 2% or less, the researchers wrote. Mythos 5 still reached outside in nearly a quarter of scenarios.

Duncan Brown, group vice president and global domain lead for worldwide security and trust at IDC, said no CISO should rely solely on system prompt rules as a control for agents.

“It’s like marking your own homework: asking a model to moderate itself is a high-risk approach,” Brown said.

Mitigations reduce boundary crossing

Anthropic tested three measures to reduce the likelihood of agents reaching outside their sandboxes. The first involved explicitly describing the sandbox, specifying which resources were off-limits, and instructing the model to stop and report if a task required resources it had not been given.

This reduced reaching-out rates by 68% to 96%, depending on the model. A tool allowing agents to report sandbox weaknesses rather than exploit them reduced the rate by seven to 33 percentage points.

The third measure, auto mode, uses a classifier to check tool calls before execution. In an offline replay of the test episodes, it blocked the first command accessing external material in 96% to 100% of misaligned use cases. It blocked the first command attempting to reach outside in 53% to 84% of boundary-crossing episodes.

The mitigations make escape less likely but “don’t fix the sandbox itself,” Anthropic wrote.

Brown said enterprises should not rely on AI vendors to secure their own models. Instead, they should encase agents in a governance structure that limits scope and access to what is required and no more, he said.

“CISOs may use AI to govern AI, but they must ensure that the governance AI is from a different vendor and has a different model provenance than the one they are governing,” Brown said.

Models suspect they are being tested

Anthropic also found that models frequently suspected they were being tested. Claude Sonnet 5.5 showed signs of evaluation awareness in 67% of scenarios. Across the four newer models, the rate ranged from 43% to 67%.

“With only six models, we can’t tell whether models are more cautious about reaching outside because they suspect they are in a test, or whether the increased eval awareness and the reduced tendency to leave the sandbox are both products of how newer models were trained,” the researchers wrote.

Brown said enterprises should not rely on vendor-produced evaluations alone when deciding whether to deploy agents. “Agents are goal-focused and will stop at nothing to achieve their mission,” he said. Organisations should instead deploy agents with “an identified sponsor, a clear scope, an expiry date and an evidential record of what the agent actually did.”

(XXXXXXX, AÇIK - https://www.csoonline.com/article/4253053/prompting-ai-to-be-thorough-can-drive-sandbox-escapes-anthropic-says.html, csoonline.com, 25.11.2026)

[37] Dark Reading - Cyprus Becomes Espionage Target for Iranian Hackers
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Thu, 26 Nov 2026 08:00:00 GMT
Link: https://www.darkreading.com/cyberattacks-data-breaches/cyprus-espionage-target-iranian-hackers

[TAM METİN - 1007 kelime | KAYNAK: proxy okuyucu]
4 Min Read

![Image 1: Aerial View of Old Marina of Girne (Kyrenia), Cyprus](https://eu-images.contentstack.com/v3/assets/blt6d90778a997de1cd/blt08316e419aab95b8/6b071dfb870042f8e04904af/cyprus-tunart-GettyImages-961759390.jpg?width=1280&auto=webp&quality=80&disable=upscale)

Source: tunart via Getty Images

Cyprus' growing security ties with Israel and its use as a staging ground for Western forces operating across the Middle East appear to be making the island an increasingly attractive target for Iranian cyber espionage.

Volant Manticore is an early example of that interest. The previously undocumented advanced persistent threat (APT) group has been targeting organizations in Cyprus as part of what researchers at Check Point [describe](https://research.checkpoint.com/2026/volant-manticore-flying-under-the-radar/) as an Iranian intelligence-gathering campaign focused on the island.

## Nation-State Strategic Intelligence Gathering on Cyprus Targets

Its targets so far have included a Cyprus-based airline operating primarily between Cyprus and Israel, telecommunications providers, critical infrastructure organizations, and a suspected government entity with military connections. The selection of targets suggests an effort to gather intelligence on the island's government, infrastructure, and relationships with regional partners, the researchers said.

Related:[Latin America Sees Cyberattacks on Ports, Pipelines & Manufacturing](https://www.darkreading.com/cyberattacks-data-breaches/latin-america-cyberattacks-ports-pipelines-manufacturing)

Check Point has assessed with medium confidence that Volant Manticore is likely an offshoot of the Iranian threat group [MuddyWater](https://www.darkreading.com/threat-intelligence/iran-muddywater-new-malware-tensions-mount), tasked primarily with spying on Cypriot organizations on behalf of Iran's Ministry of Intelligence and Security (MOIS). It is among the first known Iranian groups to have a dedicated focus on Cyprus, although it has recently expanded its targeting to organizations in Saudi Arabia, says Sergey Shykevich, director of threat intelligence at Check Point Software.

"Cyprus' defense and energy ties with Israel and Greece, along with its role in Western military operations in the region, may help explain why it is a target for intelligence gathering," Shykevich says.

He points to a Check Point report from earlier this year on a [surge in attempts](https://research.checkpoint.com/2026/interplay-between-iranian-targeting-of-ip-cameras-and-physical-warfare-in-the-middle-east/) by Iran-linked actors to exploit vulnerabilities in Internet-connected surveillance cameras across the Middle East, including Cyprus, beginning on Feb. 28, when US and Israeli strikes against Iran began. Access to the cameras could have [provided Iranian intelligence](https://www.darkreading.com/threat-intelligence/iran-cyber-kinetic-war-doctrine) with a way to monitor military and other strategically important facilities, gather information ahead of potential attacks, and assess the damage from strikes afterward. "The timing is another indication of how Iranian cyber activity tracks with security developments in the region," Shykevich adds.

## A Custom Backdoor Malware for Cyber Espionage

Check Point described Volant Manticore as relying heavily on phishing to gain an initial foothold using lures tailored for the organizations and sectors it targets. The security vendor's investigation of the group began when it spotted a fabricated resume on VirusTotal associated with "Athena Marinou," a fictitious, AI-generated travel-and-tourism professional.

Related:[Nok et norsk energiselskap har blitt utsatt for et datainnbrudd på to dager](https://www.darkreading.com/cyberattacks-data-breaches/second-norwegian-energy-company-breached-norwegian)

The document directed recipients to download the full CV from what appeared to be a legitimate recruitment website. The link, however, pointed to an archive hosted on Catbox, which delivered KuboShell — a custom backdoor that the group is using to establish an initial foothold on compromised systems, conduct basic reconnaissance, and receive commands from its operators.

Check Point found that the Windows-based backdoor uses the decentralized [InterPlanetary File System](https://www.techtarget.com/news/Key-features-of-a-distributed-file-system/857387/) (IPFS) for command-and-control (C2) communications, potentially making them harder to block or disrupt than communications routed through a conventional attacker-controlled server. The malware allows attackers to collect details about compromised systems and networks using basic PowerShell instructions. Check Point found evidence that the group then deployed a more extensive, multistage post-compromise toolkit it calls the Homa Framework.

Volant Manticore also uses legitimate remote monitoring and management (RMM) software such as [N-able](https://www.techtarget.com/news/n-able-takes-n-central-platform-offline-after-zero-day-dump/874195/), NetLock, and MeshCentral in its campaigns. The tools enable remote access and administrative capabilities while allowing the attacks to blend in with legitimate traffic. Check Point also identified infrastructure associated with credential-harvesting campaigns, including domains designed to resemble corporate email services. The researchers found evidence of operators testing their malware against antivirus products and using virtual machines and VPN-related software in their operational environments.

Related:[Second Norwegian Energy Company Breached in 2 Days](https://www.darkreading.com/cyberattacks-data-breaches/second-norwegian-energy-company-breached)

Check Point's analysis showed overlaps between Volant Manticore's techniques and code and those used by the long-active Iranian group MuddyWater. However, Check Point assessed Volant Manticore is a separate operation likely run by former MuddyWater members. "The group has a well-developed toolkit that combines techniques we’ve seen from MuddyWater with tools it developed itself," Shykevich says. "One of the less common techniques we observed was its use of KuboShell, a backdoor that provides remote access and communicates through IPFS, a decentralized file-sharing network."

In September, Check Point observed the group suddenly broadening its focus to include Saudi Arabia. "We are currently seeing the group active in both countries," Shykevich says. While Cyprus remains its primary espionage target, the group has been targeting Saudi organizations as well, mainly in the healthcare sector, he adds. "The increase in activity coincided with escalating tensions between Saudi Arabia and the Iranian-backed Houthis. The scale of the group's activity in Saudi Arabia and elsewhere is likely to vary as the situation in the region evolves."

## About the Author

[![Image 2: Jai Vijayan](https://eu-images.contentstack.com/v3/assets/blt6d90778a997de1cd/blt1a674355ce57f27b/64f14f228727730c7f926217/Jai-Vijayan.jpeg?width=400&auto=webp&quality=80&disable=upscale)](https://www.darkreading.com/author/jai-vijayan)

His information security reporting has explored everything from ransomware, nation-state threats, and identity security to AI risk, critical infrastructure protection, software supply chain security, cloud security and emerging enterprise technologies.

Over the course of his career, Jai has written news stories, feature articles, survey reports, white papers, and e-books for enterprise and technology audiences. He has also moderated panel discussions and executive roundtables featuring CISOs, security researchers, and industry leaders.

Jai previously served as senior editor at Computerworld, where he covered information security and data-privacy issues. His work has also appeared in CSO Online, InformationWeek, The Christian Science Monitor Passcode, The Economic Times, and other publications.

His work has earned multiple industry honors, including a Joint ASBPE Excellence Award for Best Coverage of Government IT, and a Joint Jesse H. Neal Award for wireless LAN security coverage. [38] Dark Reading - Attackers Target .NET Developers With Poisoned Job Assessments
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Wed, 25 Nov 2026 15:48:04 GMT
Link: https://www.darkreading.com/threat-intelligence/attackers-net-developers-poisoned-job-assessments

[TAM METİN - 1052 kelime | KAYNAK: proxy okuyucu]
![Image 1: A person sitting in front of two screens displaying lines of code.](https://eu-images.contentstack.com/v3/assets/blt6d90778a997de1cd/blt62eb1a39d76dff3b/6b06d44d51725d29015e8423/Gorodenkoff-developer-coding-shutterstock.png?width=1280&auto=webp&quality=80&disable=upscale)

Source: Gorodenkoff via Shutterstock

A coordinated [supply chain campaign](https://www.darkreading.com/application-security/attackers-weaponize-rubygems-data-dead-drops) is targeting .NET developers with fake coding assignments, weaponized software dependencies, and GitHub-hosted malware capable of stealing browser credentials and establishing remote access.

Researchers at Socket discovered the campaign, which involves 13 malicious NuGet packages designed to compromise Windows-based developer environments through [seemingly legitimate job assessments](https://www.darkreading.com/cyber-risk/contagious-interview-attack-delivers-backdoor), according to a [report](https://socket.dev/blog/malicious-nuget-packages-fake-job-assessments) published this week. The packages use fake job "Home Assessment" projects to target Windows .NET developers, with most posing as button controls for WinUI 3 and WPF, which are user interface frameworks for building applications.

"In the versions we analyzed, building or running a project that uses them downloads files from GitHub, stages a DLL side-loading pair around the Microsoft On-Screen Keyboard (osk.exe), or downloads and runs an executable," Socket researcher Kush Pandya wrote in the report.

Related:[Africa Retakes Top Spot as Most Attacked Region, Led by Nigeria Surge](https://www.darkreading.com/threat-intelligence/africa-top-spot-most-attacked-region-nigeria-surge)

As in [similar campaigns](https://www.darkreading.com/application-security/worm-redux-fresh-mini-shai-hulud-infections-bite-supply-chain) that weaponize developer repositories and code packages, attackers are exploiting developers' willingness to download, build, and run code they believe to be trustworthy. In this case, the malicious code is disguised as part of job-recruitment exercises for .NET developers. Such campaigns can turn routine development activities into infection vectors, potentially spreading malware and malicious code through the software supply chain.

## Discovering the NuGet Packages

NuGet is a package manager for .NET. Socket researchers traced the campaign across NuGet, GitHub repositories, and malicious code embedded in development projects, identifying packages linked through shared attack infrastructure and techniques.

The most recent example they found was a GitHub repository created on Nov. 4 that contained a purported WinUI 3 assessment called "Mini File Vault & Tracker." The project instructed candidates to complete a three- to four-hour development exercise using a supplied Visual Studio project. However, its [dependencies](https://www.darkreading.com/application-security/glassworm-malware-evolves-hide-dependencies) contained malicious code.

"Candidates are told that building the project shows the starter UI and that the work must be developed and tested on native Windows with Visual Studio 2026," Pandya wrote in the report.

Socket could not determine how the Home Assessment lures reached candidates, as researchers found "no recruiter message, email or job post for them," he added.

The attack involved two malicious NuGet packages working together. One executed code after every project build, while the other launched a staged executable when the application created its button control. This effectively turned an ordinary development workflow into an infection chain.

Related:[AttackIQ Research Reveals How Iranian Cyber Groups Hand Off Network Access to Enable Destructive Attacks](https://www.darkreading.com/threat-intelligence/attackiq-research-reveals-iranian-cyber-groups)

The investigation eventually uncovered 13 unique package names and 28 versions across those packages, as well as four NuGet publisher accounts linked to the operation.

## How the NuGet Campaign Threatens Enterprises

Among the malicious payloads and activities associated with the campaign was an [infostealer](https://www.darkreading.com/cyberattacks-data-breaches/fileless-phantom-stealer-targets-browser-credentials) that copied Chrome browser profiles, including saved logins and cookies. A loader also allowed the threat actor to deliver different payloads to individual victims, Pandya tells Dark Reading.

Characterizing the campaign as posing a very serious threat to enterprises, Pandya says attackers could gain access to a "treasure trove" of sensitive information by compromising a developer's machine.

"A developer's machine holds source code, SSH keys, GitHub, package registry and cloud tokens, and browser sessions that have often already passed multifactor authentication," Pandya tells Dark Reading. "In this campaign, building the take-home project was enough to run the threat actor's code."

Beyond access to terminals and configuration files, [many developers](https://www.cybersecuritydive.com/news/dozens-red-hat-npm-packages-supply-chain-attack/821723/) who work from a console have their command history automatically collected, he says. This can give adversaries insight into the commands developers have executed, as well as access to passwords and API keys that may have been inadvertently pasted into the command line, he adds.

Related:[Ransomware Attacks Increase Behind AI, but Payments Decline](https://www.darkreading.com/threat-intelligence/ransomware-attacks-increase-ai-payments-decline)

Moreover, by disguising their activity as legitimate development work, attackers gain a degree of stealth and control once the malicious code is running on a developer's machine, he says.

"A threat actor could extract SSH keys and network configurations, but their access is still from an external host and must go through various security controls," Pandya explains. "If they are working directly from a developer's machine, they not only have the necessary access keys but also the expected IP address and geolocation to avoid suspicion."

## Be Wary of Unverified Job-Recruitment Assessments

As adversaries continue to [weaponize code packages](https://www.darkreading.com/application-security/github-secure-supply-chain-npm-hacks-ramp-up)from legitimate sources to compromise the software supply chain, developers and security teams must remain vigilant. Socket has recommendations for both.

Developers should treat take-home coding assignments and unfamiliar projects as untrusted code, Pandya advises. Before building them, they should check for unfamiliar [NuGet dependencies](https://www.darkreading.com/application-security/ai-powered-dependency-decisions-security-bugs), additional package sources, and build scripts that could execute automatically.

"Open take-home tests and unfamiliar repositories only in a disposable virtual machine with no credentials," he recommends. "Restrict which package sources projects can use, scan dependencies before they are installed, and verify recruiters through official channels."

Organizations should also restrict which [NuGet feeds](https://www.darkreading.com/ics-ot-security/dubious-nuget-package-chinese-industrial-espionage) developers can use and review third-party packages that contain build scripts capable of executing commands, according to Socket. They should also educate employees and job candidates about fake recruitment assessments to reduce the risk of attackers exploiting the hiring process to gain access to developer environments.

Security teams, meanwhile, should identify affected developer machines and treat suspected infections as potential credential and software supply chain compromises, rather than simply malware incidents, Pandya says.

"If a machine is compromised, isolate and reimage it, rotate credentials and tokens from a clean device, revoke active sessions, and review audit logs," he says.

## About the Author

[![Image 2: Elizabeth Montalbano](https://eu-images.contentstack.com/v3/assets/blt6d90778a997de1cd/bltd6f16d37895d5bfd/6a06cc5deb9f4ad4d2785f3f/LizHeadshot2026.png?width=400&auto=webp&quality=80&disable=upscale)](https://www.darkreading.com/author/elizabeth-montalbano)

Contributing Writer

Elizabeth Montalbano is freelance writer, editor, and journalist with 30 years of professional experience and a master's degree from Arizona State University. Her areas of expertise include enterprise technology, cybersecurity, business, and culture. During her long career, Elizabeth has lived and worked as a full-time journalist in Phoenix, San Francisco, and New York City. She specializes in news coverage and analysis, using her years of experience to look at the current state of cybersecurity with a critical gaze. She currently resides in a village on the southwest coast of Portugal, where in her free time she enjoys surfing, hiking with her dogs, growing plants, and playing and performing as a singer and musician.

[39] Dark Reading - It's a Bot World. We're Just Living in It
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Wed, 25 Nov 2026 15:20:27 GMT
Link: https://www.darkreading.com/cyber-risk/bot-world-just-living

[TAM METİN - 1289 kelime | KAYNAK: proxy okuyucu]
![Image 1: a lot of tiny red bots lined up against a blue backdrop](https://eu-images.contentstack.com/v3/assets/blt6d90778a997de1cd/bltd16db4afaa70e4cc/6a91d9495d308674a5d86232/Bots-MASTER-Getty.jpg?width=1280&auto=webp&quality=80&disable=upscale)

Source: Master via Getty Images

The Internet is flooded with bots. People use them to organize their emails, shop, chat, and handle some of their busywork.

At the other end of the spectrum sit attackers who want to increase their speed just as much as, if not more than, legitimate users. The faster they can conduct distributed denial-of-service attacks or brute-force password attacks, the quicker they can breach an organization and the [more victims](https://www.darkreading.com/threat-intelligence/streaming-fraud-campaigns-rely-on-ai-tools-bots) they can claim.

The line between legitimate and illegitimate activity grows finer by the minute, as bots have become pros at mirroring human behavior — even its flaws.For example, during the infamous incident involving [OpenAI and Hugging Face](https://www.darkreading.com/cyber-risk/openai-models-autonomously-hack-hugging-face), bots reportedly found ways to communicate with one another by accessing the outside Internet — something they weren't programmed to do.

Behavioral baselines that security teams once relied on are becoming moot, since bots can impersonate humans. As the volume of bots accelerates, the real question isn't human versus bot but what that activity is telling teams and how to police it.

Related:[What We Missed: Nvidia CEO Scandal Rocks AI Industry](https://www.darkreading.com/cyber-risk/what-we-missed-nvidia-ceo-scandal-rocks-ai-industry)

Security tools will miss something, so teams should choose systems that allow them to quickly edit, adjust, and continually adapt to a changing environment, explains Jeremy London, director of engineering, AI, and threat analytics at Keeper Security.

Artificial intelligence (AI) agents, which can perform automated actions similar to bots without human oversight, are only [adding to environmental complexities](https://www.darkreading.com/vulnerabilities-threats/flaws-google-apk-python-agent-to-agent-attack). While some advancements can be exciting, they also introduce more gaps and zero-day vulnerabilities to the attack surface that "we just didn't understand," London says.

"I think the continual effort, as a security organization, is to show up with the right tooling, understand what you're trying to solve for, and then be able to react quickly because your control plane probably missed something — that's a guarantee," London tells Dark Reading.

## The Bot Evolution, More Like Revolution

Organizations underwent significant transformations over the past decade, propelled by AI. Adoption moved at a scale that "no one ever predicted," London says. That has forced teams to cut corners on [important safeguards](https://www.darkreading.com/cyber-risk/the-guardrails-debate-security-researcher-changes-his-mind).

Code reviews and security practices need a serious makeover, especially regarding boundaries and compliance. Bots and agents have new levels of autonomy and access, [leading to more unpredictability](https://www.darkreading.com/cyber-risk/securing-ai-agents-rogue) and changing what security risks even look like.

However, such a makeover has proved to be a difficult undertaking because AI doesn't come with a security playbook, London explains. A decade ago, bots were rigid and scripts performed a single action. Now "teams vibe code stuff and throw things at the wall and see what works, and the scale that [bots] can operate at is totally different," he says.

Related:[9 Cyber Awareness Tips That Actually Match Today's Maze of Threats](https://www.darkreading.com/cyber-risk/9-cyber-awareness-tips-todays-maze-threats)

While organizations struggle, attackers are having a field day with these advancements. Given all of the [recent disclosures and reports](https://www.darkreading.com/application-security/grok-bots-corporate-data-without-user-approval), it's no surprise that attacks are rising. What is surprising is how quickly threat actors can adapt and respond to countermeasures, says Jérôme Segura, VP of threat research at DataDome, a bot management and agent trust platform.

"Anytime we put challenges in front of them, we see it slows them down and costs them more because to run the same operation, they have to use more resources, which becomes expensive," he explains.

The reason the attackers aren't giving up despite the obstacles is that their attacks are working, Segura tells Dark Reading.

"Because the payout is so great, they're doubling, tripling their investments," he says.

The persistence is challenging and frustrating for defenders. But it's just the stark reality that threat actors are highly motivated, and "we're battling against that," Segura adds.

## How High Is the Volume Getting?

When threat actors use [bots against online retailers](https://www.darkreading.com/cybersecurity-operations/why-holiday-security-cant-wait-black-friday), they replicate the exact flow of a human but without all the telltale signs, such as hesitating over a purchasing decision, fumbling to enter card or shipping details, or pausing to solve a CAPTCHA challenge. They don't need to click through every page like a human would or wait for the site to load all the images either. As a result, bots complete transactions in a split second, Segura says. The speed can create security risks and unfair competition for legitimate users.

Related:[Rethinking Cybersecurity Awareness in the AI Era](https://www.darkreading.com/cyber-risk/rethinking-cybersecurity-awareness-ai-era)

More concerningly, bots complete these tasks in ways that appear legitimate because they're aware that companies are trying to block this malicious activity, he says. And the volume is only increasing. Segura conducted research analyzing traffic hitting retailers' product pages and found that 91% were actually bad bots.

Taking legal action is difficult when it's nearly impossible to differentiate legitimate and illegitimate activity, which puts even more pressure on businesses. Scraping product pages or ticket scalping remains "kind of a gray area in terms of the legal aspect," Segura explains, adding that the law isn't quite clear yet on what is permitted.

In some cases, the culprits are organized groups just looking to make money and oftentimes use loopholes to circumvent the law. For example, they end up using existing infrastructure to collect scarce products and resell them, Segura says.

Threat actors organize so that they have specialties, just as defenders do, he has observed. Some specialize in reverse engineering specific parts of a website's code to understand how it works. Others focus on providing the necessary infrastructure, including all the IP addresses, to carry out those kinds of attacks, he says.

"You can source IPs from real users that are extremely convincing because they're also shared by humans," Segura explains. "So all these specializations within these groups, working well together, and making sure everybody's getting paid, is really interesting."

## Tiny Gap Between Humans and Bots

Behavioral analytics are struggling right now, London says, and is a "pain point" rippling across the industry.

Some bots can move the mouse pointer and send keystrokes to mimic typing, so verification techniques like measuring mouse movements or typing speeds need to evolve. Models can be a bit slow, just as humans can be, he notes.

"Although they are robotic in nature, some of those behavioral or human factors that teams have been using for indication points are really close," London says.

Since it's so difficult to determine whether activity is from a human or a bot, organizations should manage them both the same way when it comes to privileged identity: the less access, the better.

Given all of the risks and potential blast radii, "security teams, not developers, should probably decide whether AI is allowed in an organization," London recommends.

"From a security perspective, it makes it somewhat simple because AI agents can do bad things [and] humans can do bad things," he says. "So if we just look at it through that perspective, I don't care if it's a human or an agent."

## About the Author

[![Image 2: Arielle Waldman](https://eu-images.contentstack.com/v3/assets/blt6d90778a997de1cd/blte685016783fc7faa/67ca10f9cf523d2a3ce48c04/arielle_waldman_headshot.jpg?width=400&auto=webp&quality=80&disable=upscale)](https://www.darkreading.com/author/arielle-waldman)

Features Writer, Dark Reading

Arielle spent the last decade working as a reporter, transitioning from human interest stories to covering all things cybersecurity related in 2020. Now, as a features writer for Dark Reading, she delves into the security problems enterprises face daily, providing context and actionable steps. She looks for stories that go past the initial news to understand where the industry is going. Her coverage areas include identity and access management, cyber risk and operations, industrial control systems, operational technology, and ransomware trends.

She previously lived in Florida where she wrote for the Tampa Bay Times before returning to Boston where her cybersecurity career took off at TechTarget SearchSecurity. When she's not writing about cybersecurity, she pursues personal projects that include a mystery novel and poetry collection.

[40] Dark Reading - Latin America Sees Cyberattacks on Ports, Pipelines &amp; Manufacturing
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Wed, 25 Nov 2026 14:00:00 GMT
Link: https://www.darkreading.com/cyberattacks-data-breaches/latin-america-cyberattacks-ports-pipelines-manufacturing

[TAM METİN - 965 kelime | KAYNAK: proxy okuyucu]
4 Min Read

![Image 1: Port Santos, Brazil, with a large cargo ship in the foreground.](https://eu-images.contentstack.com/v3/assets/blt6d90778a997de1cd/blt436e3cfa4b9fe8b4/6b05fbef3e73ec0ee2851fe9/port-santos-brazil-ByDroneVideos-shutterstock.jpg?width=1280&auto=webp&quality=80&disable=upscale)

Source: ByDroneVideos via Shutterstock

On Nov. 6, a cyberattack encrypted data at the Port of Santos in Brazil, the largest port in South America. Around the same time, the data center serving the port suffered a power outage. The connection between the outage and cyberattack is still under investigation, but in combination, they affected the Santos Port Authority (APS) website, internal systems, and online services used by port customers.

The incident affected the APS enterprise resource planning (ERP) systems and services for customers on the website, which the organization just restored on Nov. 24, Paulo Silveira, a spokesman for the Port Authority, tells Dark Reading.

The incident was a [ransomware attack](https://www.darkreading.com/ics-ot-security/2-years-after-colonial-pipeline-attack-us-critical-infrastructure-remains-as-vulnerable-to-ransomware), but the perpetrators remain unidentified, he says.

"The intruder was already in the systems and likely gained entry via a malicious email or by improperly accessing an infected website," he says. "The cyber-attack is under investigation, and the method of intrusion and the party responsible are still being traced."

Related:[Cyprus Becomes Espionage Target for Iranian Hackers](https://www.darkreading.com/cyberattacks-data-breaches/cyprus-espionage-target-iranian-hackers)

The ransomware attack is the latest incident in a surge hitting industrial sectors in Latin America. In the third quarter, operations at several industrial facilities were disrupted by ransomware attacks, according to data published by cybersecurity vendor Dragos. While none of the attacks appeared to compromise an actual [operational technology](https://www.techtarget.com/news/operational-technology/872649/) (OT) system — considered to be Stage 2 [on the ICS Cyber Kill Chain](https://www.techtarget.com/news/ICS-kill-chain-Adapting-the-cyber-kill-chain-to-ICS-environments/837854/) — IT disruptions often cascade into operational problems, says Abdul Alamri, a principal threat intelligence analyst at Dragos.

"Often, ransomware groups do not need to reach OT to stop operations," he says. "When ransomware disrupts operations, it is usually through the enterprise IT, ERP and virtualization systems that operations depend on, or because the company shuts down production as a precaution."

## Ransomware on the Rise in Latin America

Overall, cyber activity is ramping up in the southern hemisphere. While African nations have retaken the dubious honor of [suffering the highest frequency of cyberattacks](https://www.darkreading.com/threat-intelligence/africa-top-spot-most-attacked-region-nigeria-surge), Latin America is [not far behind](https://www.darkreading.com/threat-intelligence/african-organizations-see-easing-of-cyberattacks), with organizations in the region seeing an average of 3,483 per week, compared to Africa's 3,834 per week. The attacks are not just cybercriminals looking for opportunistic ransoms, but also [nation-state threat actors](https://www.darkreading.com/cyberattacks-data-breaches/russian-influence-operation-targeting-latin-america) aiming to [compromise government and the private sector](https://www.darkreading.com/cyberattacks-data-breaches/nation-state-cyber-activity-latin-america) to gain intelligence on the politics in the region.

Many of the malicious incidents are ransomware. In the third quarter, industrial organizations in South America suffered at least 82 claimed ransomware incidents, up from 64 in the second quarter — the largest percentage increase of any major region, according to Dragos. Brazil topped the targeted nations with 22 incidents, while Peru and Argentina tallied 16 apiece, and Colombia saw 11 incidents, according to [Dragos'](https://www.dragos.com/blog/dragos-industrial-ransomware-analysis-q3-2026)[Industrial Ransomware Analysis for Q3 2026](https://www.dragos.com/blog/dragos-industrial-ransomware-analysis-q3-2026)[report](https://www.dragos.com/blog/dragos-industrial-ransomware-analysis-q3-2026).

Related:[Nok et norsk energiselskap har blitt utsatt for et datainnbrudd på to dager](https://www.darkreading.com/cyberattacks-data-breaches/second-norwegian-energy-company-breached-norwegian)

![Image 2: A map showing global ransomware estimates for Q3 2026.](https://eu-images.contentstack.com/v3/assets/blt6d90778a997de1cd/blt3a76a9254004dd65/6b05fbd49a6ec46aa52137bc/global-ransomware-estimates-Q3-2026-Dragos.jpg?width=1400&auto=webp&quality=80&disable=upscale)

While Dragos tallies the number of victims claimed on ransomware groups' leak sites, Alamri considers the numbers to be conservative.

"We exclude victims we cannot identify or classify, and we remove victims that were already counted in an earlier quarter," he says. "Organizations that pay before they are posted never show up in the data."

Overall, organizations in Brazil, Mexico, and Argentina made up nearly two-thirds of ransomware victims, according to [a second report,](https://portal.cci-entel.cl/Threat_Intelligence/Boletines/2655)[State of Cyber Threats 2026](https://portal.cci-entel.cl/Threat_Intelligence/Boletines/2655), published by Chilean telecommunications firm Entel Digital, which counts more than just industrial sectors.

## Ports, Pipelines, and Manufacturing Suffer Attacks

In the Port of Santos incident, the cyberattack did not disrupt operations, mainly because the Santo Port Authority switched to alternate processes, says APS's Silveira. "The APS teams were working hard to attend to all the port users, assisted by e-mail, personal cellphones, and third-party systems," he says.

Related:[Second Norwegian Energy Company Breached in 2 Days](https://www.darkreading.com/cyberattacks-data-breaches/second-norwegian-energy-company-breached)

While the Port Authority managed to recover all their backups, they did suffer some loss of data after Oct. 31. APS is recovering that data from other records, Silveira says.

Similarly, an Argentinian pipeline company, Oldelval, suffered a cybersecurity incident that affected administrative systems, based on filings with the National Securities Commission (CNV), according to [media](https://www.iprofesional.com/negocios/461285-alerta-vaca-muerta-por-ciberataque-puso-a-prueba-seguridad-mayor-oleoducto-argentina)[reports](https://www.ambito.com/hackers-atacaron-oldelval-que-opera-el-mayor-oleoducto-la-argentina-n6306082). The attack did not hamper the movement of oil or crude, the company reportedly said.

While [critical infrastructure](https://www.cybersecuritydive.com/news/medusa-ransomware-slams-critical-infrastructure-organizations/742428/) is increasingly under attack, most global attacks on industrial firms target manufacturing, which also held true for South America this year; Dragos' Alamri says 57% of cyberattacks on industrial firms targeting the manufacturing sector.

Often, while OT systems are not compromised by a strict definition, they are still impacted. In past cases, incidents classified as IT-only cases actually affected engineering workstations and human-machine interface (HMI) hardware, which were classified as IT because they run Windows, he says.

"A statement that only administrative systems were affected can be accurate, and Oldelval reported that crude transport continued," he says. "However, these statements should be read with caution."

## About the Author

[![Image 3: Robert Lemos](https://eu-images.contentstack.com/v3/assets/blt6d90778a997de1cd/bltfac4d821bdd7e9f1/6a0c6d499384132f848610d6/robertlemos2024.jpg?width=400&auto=webp&quality=80&disable=upscale)](https://www.darkreading.com/author/robert-lemos)

Contributing Writer

Rob is an award-winning, veteran technology journalist of more than 30 years, reporting on global cybersecurity issues, the latest offensive and defensive technologies, malware incidents, cyber conflict, and AI's impact on software and cybersecurity.

A former research engineer, Rob has written for more than two dozen publications, including CNET News.com, Dark Reading, MIT's Technology Review, Popular Science, and Wired News. He has received five awards for journalism, including Best Deadline Journalism (Online) in 2003 for his coverage of the Blaster worm. Rob also analyzes data on various trends using Python and R for both his reporting and his clients. Recent reports include analyses of the shortage in cybersecurity workers, annual vulnerability trends, and annual threat reports.

Rob holds degrees from Cornell University in Electrical Engineering and Computer Science (double major).

[41] Dark Reading - How Southeastern University Got a Handle on Its Data
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Tue, 24 Nov 2026 15:46:46 GMT
Link: https://www.darkreading.com/cybersecurity-operations/how-southeastern-university-handle-data

[TAM METİN - 1680 kelime | KAYNAK: proxy okuyucu]
![Image 1: Data storage concept illustration](https://eu-images.contentstack.com/v3/assets/blt6d90778a997de1cd/blt25e178de5cb0409d/6b00d028efff7c214dcd404d/data_Pingingz_shutterstock.jpg?width=1280&auto=webp&quality=80&disable=upscale)

Source: Pingingz via Shutterstock

Southeastern University had a data security problem hiding in plain sight. Like many universities, it operated a sprawling IT environment in which individual departments maintained their own workflows and data repositories. Student records, financial information, and other personally identifiable information (PII) were scattered across file servers, Google Drive, Microsoft OneDrive, SharePoint, databases, and other systems. The university had no reliable way to see what sensitive data it held, where it was stored, whose information it contained, or who could access it.

The challenge is not unique to Southeastern. A 2024 [Kiteworks survey](https://www.kiteworks.com/brief-report-2024-analysis-of-sensitive-content-communications-in-higher-education-security-and-compliance-trends/) of higher education institutions found that nearly half of respondents (49%) used five or more tools to send and share sensitive information. Just 19% said they could track and control sensitive data shared internally, and only 14% could do so when information was exchanged externally.

Related:[Attackers in Binance Breach Targeted Known Weakness, Missing Controls](https://www.darkreading.com/cybersecurity-operations/attackers-binance-breach-targeted-weakness-missing-controls)

While Kiteworks' study found the education sector to be at considerably higher risk than other sectors, its more recent [2026 survey](https://www.kiteworks.com/sites/default/files/resources/kiteworks-report-2026-data-security-compliance-risk-report.pdf) suggests the problem extends well beyond higher education. Among 450 security, compliance, and technology professionals surveyed across industries, 54% said their organizations used four or more separate platforms to exchange sensitive information, 73% had no enforcement restricting which of those channels employees could use, and just 39% validated their sensitive data on a quarterly or more frequent basis. The findings highlight the challenges organizations face in maintaining visibility and control of sensitive information across increasingly complex IT environments.

## Lots of Data, Little Visibility

For Joshua Rhoden, Southeastern's executive director of IT operations and security, lack of visibility created problems on both the security and compliance fronts. His team struggled to identify where sensitive information was most exposed, apply consistent safeguards, and meet requirements under regulations such as the Family Educational Rights and Privacy Act (FERPA), PCI DSS standards, and the Gramm-Leach-Bliley Act (GLBA). Universities must be PCI-compliant because they accept and process credit card payments; they must comply with GLBA if they participate in federal student aid programs.

"We knew there was a lot of data out there, but we didn't know exactly what it was or where it lived," Rhoden says. "Our data was kind of spread across diverse areas, and it basically created a challenge for us to have visibility across all of that."

Related:[Getting Past the Post-Quantum Cryptography Discovery Phase](https://www.darkreading.com/cybersecurity-operations/getting-past-post-quantum-cryptography-discovery)

The university initially relied on discovery and security features built into platforms such as Google Workspace and Microsoft 365. But looking at each environment separately made it difficult to develop a comprehensive picture of the university's data and the protections around it. It was harder to tell who had access to sensitive information or when sensitive data was exported and shared without necessary protections. Meanwhile, growing AI adoption across campus was creating new concerns about how employees and other users accessed, shared, and handled sensitive information.

As a small private university in Lakeland, Fla., Southeastern's IT team didn't have the resources to manually discover, classify, and govern data across all these environments. So a little over a year ago, Rhoden began looking for a data security posture management (DSPM) platform that could automate much of that work and give his team a clearer picture of the university's exposure.

After evaluating at least three DSPM products, Southeastern selected Lightbeam's Data Identity Graph because the data engine could consider the broader context when identifying sensitive information, rather than relying primarily on predefined keywords and patterns. Other evaluated products generated too many false positives when they flagged information based on superficial similarities to sensitive data. Lightbeam's approach resulted in fewer false positives, he says.

Related:[Investors Dish on New Startups & What They'd Back Next](https://www.darkreading.com/cybersecurity-operations/investors-dish-new-startsup-what-back-next)

## Enabling a Unified View

Southeastern connected Lightbeam to its Microsoft and Google environments, file servers, databases, email systems, and other repositories. The platform uses APIs and purpose-built connectors to link these disparate sources, scan information where it resides, and identify and classify sensitive data. This meant the university could gain a unified view of its data across its environment without moving all of it into a centralized repository.

One particularly difficult part of the process was determining when records across different systems referred to the same person.

"How do we link the user in a database for our student information system to that same user in Google Workspace and Microsoft 365, so that we get the full picture that the entity is the same person across all those different sources?" Rhoden says, noting that was one of the biggest deployment challenges the security team faced.

Lightbeam also helped the university develop more granular categories for information, such as academic records, student financial aid, loans, tuition, and FAFSA records. This allowed Rhoden's team to distinguish among different types of sensitive information and apply more appropriate access, retention, and compliance policies.

"If files are being shared internally within a department, they should be shared in a designated location," Rhoden says, by way of an example. "If they're being shared internally within SEU, they should be shared in another designated location. And if they're being shared externally, there's a specific process to follow."

## A Better Understanding of Risk

The deployment has given Southeastern visibility into more than 180TB of data, encompassing 7.6 million files across 13 data sources. Lightbeam identified 642,000 files containing sensitive information and helped the university associate that information with approximately 1.7 million individual and organizational identities, Rhoden says.

The improved visibility has already helped the university identify files and cloud links that were accessible to more people than necessary. That has given the IT team a basis for tightening permissions and moving toward least-privilege access. Southeastern has also established baseline policies for identifying externally exposed information and data that no longer needs to be retained. The next step is to implement more automated access and retention controls, including safeguards for the sensitive information that AI applications can access.

## Preparing for the AI Era

That last area, in particular, is becoming increasingly important as the university encourages students, faculty, and staff to use AI tools to improve productivity and the broader campus experience. Rhoden wants to support that adoption without allowing sensitive university information to fall into the wrong hands. One concern is employees signing up for free AI accounts and entering student records, personal information, or other confidential material into services that may use conversations to train or improve their models. Because these tools are readily available, employees without an IT or security background may inadvertently expose information without realizing the implications.

"What scares me most is just how embedded AI has become in everyone's lives," he says. "It's how accessible these tools are, how easy they are to use, and how tempting it is to turn to them and say, 'I could spend time figuring this out, but I could also just get it done in a minute while I'm working on something else.'"

Rhoden sees the work with Lightbeam as an important foundation for managing that risk. With a better understanding of what information the university holds and how sensitive it is, his team can begin drawing clearer boundaries around AI use, distinguishing data that can safely be shared with AI applications from information that should remain off-limits.

## A Focus on Context

Southeastern's work with Lightbeam reflects a broader shift in data security from simply finding sensitive information to understanding it in context, says Himanshu Shukla, co-founder and CEO of Lightbeam. Traditional discovery tools often focus on identifying data such as Social Security numbers or other personally identifiable information, but that alone offers an incomplete picture of risk, he says. For example, identifying a Social Security number in isolation, without understanding whose it is, where it resides, or how it is being used, provides incomplete context for assessing risk.

Building an entity-centric view of data allows organizations like Southeastern to map information scattered across different systems to the associated individuals and organizations, Shukla says. This can enable a more complete picture of what data they hold, where it resides, who can access it, and how it is being shared or used. That context can help security teams identify unnecessary exposure, tighten permissions, and apply more targeted retention, data-handling and automated workflow policies, he notes.

Meanwhile, Rhoden's advice to other universities considering a DSPM investment is to assess both the complexity of their data environments and the resources available to manage them. Universities need to consider whether they have the personnel to manually examine their many data sources and configure the necessary security controls or whether a platform can help automate discovery and provide a more comprehensive view.

"You can't solve problems you don't know exist," Rhoden says. "Once you know what's out there and what problems do exist, you can start planning to solve them."

Improving data security takes time, organizational buy-in, and sustained effort, but establishing visibility, he says, is the essential first step.

Over the course of his career, Jai has written news stories, feature articles, survey reports, white papers, and e-books for enterprise and technology audiences. He has also moderated panel discussions and executive roundtables featuring CISOs, security researchers, and industry leaders.

[42] SecurityWeek - 1Password Breach Exposes Customer Data, Internal Source Code
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Thu, 26 Nov 2026 08:32:10 +0000
Link: https://www.securityweek.com/1password-breach-exposes-customer-data-internal-source-code/

[TAM METİN - 3346 kelime]
1Password Breach Exposes Customer Data, Internal Source Code

A hacker used a compromised employee GitHub account to push malicious code and steal customer records, but vaults were not accessed.

Flipboard





Reddit





Whatsapp





Whatsapp





Email

Password management and identity security company 1Password revealed on Wednesday that a hacker stole customer information and proprietary source code. 1Password says customer vaults are not affected.
The company launched an investigation on November 19 after discovering that an employee’s corporate GitHub account may have been compromised. 



The attacker used the account to deploy malicious code to backend services and exfiltrate customer records. The compromised customer records include names, email addresses, account identifiers, public cryptographic keys, authentication parameters, account activity times, and non-secret metadata.



In addition, the threat actor obtained source code from private repositories and accessed the company’s CI/CD system, potentially exposing infrastructure credentials.



According to 1Password, the hacker also obtained tokens from emails sent to users for account invitations, provisioning, and recovery. 



A “small number” of individuals who were undergoing provisioning or recovery during the intrusion have been notified. However, the company noted that the exposed tokens cannot be exploited on their own and there is no evidence that the attacker hijacked user accounts.Advertisement. Scroll to continue reading.


A 1Password representative noted on the company’s community forum that the stolen authentication data included Secure Remote Password verifiers derived from user account passwords and Secret Keys. The verifiers cannot be used directly for authentication, and brute-forcing account passwords would require the associated Secret Keys, which 1Password does not store.



1Password highlighted that customer vaults and the cryptographic keys protecting them were not compromised. “We have no evidence that encrypted vaults were downloaded or accessed,” a 1Password community manager said.



The breach also does not impact client applications, browser extensions, and account recovery keys.



It’s unclear how many users are affected and how the hacker gained initial access to the corporate GitHub account.



In response to the intrusion, 1Password disabled the compromised GitHub account, invalidated a stolen token, replaced potentially compromised infrastructure credentials, and restored clean versions of affected services.



The company said its investigation, conducted with independent cybersecurity experts, is still ongoing. Customers do not need to take any action, but have been advised to watch for phishing attacks that could exploit news of the incident.
