[43] SecurityWeek - Ground Truth: CISO Matt Martin on Building a Real Security Program Without an Enterprise Budget
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Wed, 25 Nov 2026 13:30:00 +0000
Link: https://www.securityweek.com/ground-truth-ciso-matt-martin-on-building-a-real-security-program-without-an-enterprise-budget/

[TAM METİN - 25911 kelime]
Ground Truth: CISO Matt Martin on Building a Real Security Program Without an Enterprise Budget

Matthew Martin is Chief Information Security and Privacy Officer at Western Carolina University.

Flipboard





Reddit





Whatsapp





Whatsapp





Email

A CISO who traded enterprise budgets for higher ed on what “creativity within constraints” really means, and the one habit worth stealing from how he leads.I have known Matt Martin for a few years, and the first thing I tell anyone about him has nothing to do with security. It is that he treats the work as a way to serve other people. I first knew Matt as the security leader who spends his own time flying to Ghana and Uganda to teach executives and students how to protect themselves. That instinct, more than any tool or certification, explains how he runs a security program.



Matt spent two decades running security organizations in financial services, including at LPL Financial, with an enterprise budget and any tool he needed. A few years ago he made a deliberate choice to take on higher ed instead, because he wanted to prove he could build a resilient security program under real constraints. Today he is the Chief Information Security and Privacy Officer at Western Carolina University.



I asked Matt to share his journey from a conference in Accra to a university in the mountains of North Carolina. His story is really about doing more with less, and right now, that is all of us.



Matthew Martin, Chief Information Security and Privacy Officer at Western Carolina University.

Q. You have built real social impact into your career, sharing security knowledge and consultancy with underserved communities. How did that begin?
A. It started unexpectedly. After two decades in financial services I decided to go out on my own. The whole point was to support small and medium organizations, especially in developing countries, that could not afford the security consultancy they needed. I heard about the GC3B conference in Accra, Ghana, which was about building cyber resiliency in developing countries. They only accepted companies, so I applied as the LLC I had just started, and a few weeks later I received a letter from the government of Ghana inviting me.



I flew over not knowing a single person. The first day had a light schedule, so I hired a tour guide and he took me to see Elmina Castle and the Door of No Return. That one day changed my life. Seeing that side of the story of humanity as an adult, after growing up in the American South, connected so much for me about how tied together we all are. I carried that into every meeting at the conference.



Afterwards I developed a partnership with a nonprofit called Africa Digital Rights Hub, and it has since taken me back to run masterclasses for executives and students in Ghana and Uganda. Part of the reason Western Carolina appealed to me was that they wanted to help me expand that work.Advertisement. Scroll to continue reading.


Q. You had about three weeks between that invitation from the Ghanaian government and actually getting on the plane. What was it like to just drop everything and go?
A. It was a scramble. Three weeks to get all my immunizations, book everything, and fly to Ghana. Actually, my yellow fever vaccine wasn’t fully effective by the time I landed, I was a couple of days short, so I had to talk my way through the medical booth in the airport before I could even clear immigration.



Funny enough, it’s risk acceptance in real life. I knew my control wasn’t operating effectively yet, I understood the exposure, and I decided to go anyway. That’s the same type of call we make in security every day.



Q. I’ve heard you say that security and GRC have become an elite sport. Job postings demand degrees and a wall of certifications before you can apply, but attackers have none of them. You are largely self-taught. What has that taught you about who belongs in this field?
A. I started with a history degree, and some of the best security people I know do not have a security degree either. Think how this community started. In the beginning you couldn’t go get a degree in cybersecurity, it was creative people figuring it out as they went. There are brilliant people all over the world who could do this work and simply haven’t had the opportunity. That is why the teaching matters so much to me. There is a real chance to democratize security education and take down some of the barriers the industry has put up.



Q. You have a phrase for how you think about the work now, “creativity within constraints.” When you started in the new context of higher ed, what did you assume your constraints would be?
A. I assumed it would be budget. In financial services, I could get almost anything as long as it was reasonable. Budgets are definitely tight in higher ed, but it was not my number one problem. I needed to learn the environment.A university has done things a certain way for a long time, knowledge is meant to be open and shared, and the place can feel like family. Once I really understood the environment, I could get creative and start designing security that worked with the culture instead of against it.



Q. You’ve been candid with me that you got the first few months wrong, and I think that honesty helps other leaders even more than sharing wins. What happened?
A. I went in thinking I already knew how everything should work, and I started fixing things. I probably  pushed harder than I should have before I understood the place. What I should have done was go in to learn, listen more than I talked, and document what I found before I tried to solve anything. Overseeing the security program at a large company does not mean you know how to build one everywhere.



Q. So with a small team and a tight budget, how do you actually run a real program? Walk me through the mechanics.
A. It starts with risk, not compliance. If you do risk well, compliance comes almost for free, because it tells you what you need to do and in what order. So I built a real risk register. I went to the technology leaders first and asked what was broken and what was holding them back, wrote it all down, and mapped it to a framework.



Then the honest problem is capacity. I cannot hire a team of analysts to test every control or spend weeks each year on a self-assessment. Self-attestation is not enough on its own. Anyone who has run a program knows that asking whether a control works can get you different answers depending on who you ask, so I needed real evidence, not opinions. The only way to get that at scale was to embrace automation and continuous monitoring.



Q. You have now lived on both sides. What would you take from higher ed back into a big enterprise?
A. Fiscal discipline, honestly. The habit of asking “do we really need this, or is it a nice-to-have,” and whether a given feature is three million dollars better than what we already have. In large enterprises you also end up with people inserting themselves into processes to justify a role, and everything takes longer. But look, every company of every size has something it could do better. I’ve really embraced how scarcity forces you to be more efficient.



Q. How can you judge whether someone will make a good CISO?
A. This one question tells me what I need to know: Do you know how your company makes money? Can you actually tell me how your organization makes money, or in a university, how the institution succeeds? Once you understand that, you can prioritize risk in a way that matters to the business. If you cannot answer it, you are going to protect the wrong things.



Q. What kind of CISO actually thrives in a place like higher ed?
A. You have to want to get your hands dirty. If you just want to run a team of forty and just go to meetings, it will not work, because your team is smaller and you will be doing some of the work yourself. You have to be creative enough to build things that are not the industry standard. And most of all, you have to see the business-enablement side of security, not only the technical side. You are engaging with the colleges, the chancellor, even the athletics department, and your job is to enable them. If you come in 100% security or bust, you will not last long in higher ed.



Q. Matt, thank you for this. What is on your radar right now? Is there anything you are most excited to build next?
A. Giving students a real place to learn. I want more working CISOs to share with students what the career actually looks like. Where I would love to take it is a hands-on cybersecurity lab on campus, for students to get real-world practice. I would also love to keep building the connections with universities in Africa, so students can learn from each other in both directions.



Ground truth for your program
Matt’s whole approach is a posture, not a tactic. He leads as a teacher and a collaborator, and treats security as a way to help organizations succeed rather than a roadblock.



The lesson I would take to any team, at any budget: name your real constraint before you invest against the wrong one. Matt walked in certain it was budget, and it turned out to be understanding the environment. Assume it’s money and you pour your limited resources into tools, when the real problem was one money could not solve. Get the constraint right, though, and every dollar and every control is aimed at a real business objective, which is what makes a program effective at any size.



So before your next purchase or project, ask yourself two questions: What do I actually believe my top constraint is, and how do I know? And am I about to spend against it, or around it?



Join the CISO Forum 2026 Year-End Review Webinar | December 2, 2026

Matt spent two decades running security organizations in financial services, including at LPL Financial, with an enterprise budget and any tool he needed. A few years ago he made a deliberate choice to take on higher ed instead, because he wanted to prove he could build a resilient security program under real constraints. Today he is the Chief Information Security and Privacy Officer at Western Carolina University.



I asked Matt to share his journey from a conference in Accra to a university in the mountains of North Carolina. His story is really about doing more with less, and right now, that is all of us.



Matthew Martin, Chief Information Security and Privacy Officer at Western Carolina University.

Q. You have built real social impact into your career, sharing security knowledge and consultancy with underserved communities. How did that begin?

Afterwards I developed a partnership with a nonprofit called Africa Digital Rights Hub, and it has since taken me back to run masterclasses for executives and students in Ghana and Uganda. Part of the reason Western Carolina appealed to me was that they wanted to help me expand that work.Advertisement. Scroll to continue reading.

Where I would love to take it is a hands-on cybersecurity lab on campus, for students to get real-world practice. I would also love to keep building the connections with universities in Africa, so students can learn from each other in both directions.

Actually, my yellow fever vaccine wasn’t fully effective by the time I landed, I was a couple of days short, so I had to talk my way through the medical booth in the airport before I could even clear immigration.



Funny enough, it’s risk acceptance in real life. I knew my control wasn’t operating effectively yet, I understood the exposure, and I decided to go anyway. That’s the same type of call we make in security every day.



Q. I’ve heard you say that security and GRC have become an elite sport. Job postings demand degrees and a wall of certifications before you can apply, but attackers have none of them. You are largely self-taught. What has that taught you about who belongs in this field?
A. I started with a history degree, and some of the best security people I know do not have a security degree either. Think how this community started. In the beginning you couldn’t go get a degree in cybersecurity, it was creative people figuring it out as they went. There are brilliant people all over the world who could do this work and simply haven’t had the opportunity. That is why the teaching matters so much to me. There is a real chance to democratize security education and take down some of the barriers the industry has put up.



Q. You have a phrase for how you think about the work now, “creativity within constraints.” When you started in the new context of higher ed, what did you assume your constraints would be?
A. I assumed it would be budget. In financial services, I could get almost anything as long as it was reasonable. Budgets are definitely tight in higher ed, but it was not my number one problem. I needed to learn the environment.A university has done things a certain way for a long time, knowledge is meant to be open and shared, and the place can feel like family. Once I really understood the environment, I could get creative and start designing security that worked with the culture instead of against it.



Q. You’ve been candid with me that you got the first few months wrong, and I think that honesty helps other leaders even more than sharing wins. What happened?
A. I went in thinking I already knew how everything should work, and I started fixing things. I probably  pushed harder than I should have before I understood the place. What I should have done was go in to learn, listen more than I talked, and document what I found before I tried to solve anything. Overseeing the security program at a large company does not mean you know how to build one everywhere.



Q. So with a small team and a tight budget, how do you actually run a real program? Walk me through the mechanics.
A. It starts with risk, not compliance. If you do risk well, compliance comes almost for free, because it tells you what you need to do and in what order. So I built a real risk register. I went to the technology leaders first and asked what was broken and what was holding them back, wrote it all down, and mapped it to a framework.



Then the honest problem is capacity. I cannot hire a team of analysts to test every control or spend weeks each year on a self-assessment. Self-attestation is not enough on its own. Anyone who has run a program knows that asking whether a control works can get you different answers depending on who you ask, so I needed real evidence, not opinions. The only way to get that at scale was to embrace automation and continuous monitoring.



Q. You have now lived on both sides. What would you take from higher ed back into a big enterprise?
A. Fiscal discipline, honestly. The habit of asking “do we really need this, or is it a nice-to-have,” and whether a given feature is three million dollars better than what we already have. In large enterprises you also end up with people inserting themselves into processes to justify a role, and everything takes longer. But look, every company of every size has something it could do better. I’ve really embraced how scarcity forces you to be more efficient.



Q. How can you judge whether someone will make a good CISO?
A. This one question tells me what I need to know: Do you know how your company makes money? Can you actually tell me how your organization makes money, or in a university, how the institution succeeds? Once you understand that, you can prioritize risk in a way that matters to the business. If you cannot answer it, you are going to protect the wrong things.



Q. What kind of CISO actually thrives in a place like higher ed?
A. You have to want to get your hands dirty. If you just want to run a team of forty and just go to meetings, it will not work, because your team is smaller and you will be doing some of the work yourself. You have to be creative enough to build things that are not the industry standard. And most of all, you have to see the business-enablement side of security, not only the technical side. You are engaging with the colleges, the chancellor, even the athletics department, and your job is to enable them. If you come in 100% security or bust, you will not last long in higher ed.



Q. Matt, thank you for this. What is on your radar right now? Is there anything you are most excited to build next?
A. Giving students a real place to learn. I want more working CISOs to share with students what the career actually looks like. Where I would love to take it is a hands-on cybersecurity lab on campus, for students to get real-world practice. I would also love to keep building the connections with universities in Africa, so students can learn from each other in both directions.



Ground truth for your program
Matt’s whole approach is a posture, not a tactic. He leads as a teacher and a collaborator, and treats security as a way to help organizations succeed rather than a roadblock.



The lesson I would take to any team, at any budget: name your real constraint before you invest against the wrong one. Matt walked in certain it was budget, and it turned out to be understanding the environment. Assume it’s money and you pour your limited resources into tools, when the real problem was one money could not solve. Get the constraint right, though, and every dollar and every control is aimed at a real business objective, which is what makes a program effective at any size.



So before your next purchase or project, ask yourself two questions: What do I actually believe my top constraint is, and how do I know? And am I about to spend against it, or around it?



Join the CISO Forum 2026 Year-End Review Webinar | December 2, 2026

I flew over not knowing a single person. The first day had a light schedule, so I hired a tour guide and he took me to see Elmina Castle and the Door of No Return. That one day changed my life. Seeing that side of the story of humanity as an adult, after growing up in the American South, connected so much for me about how tied together we all are. I carried that into every meeting at the conference.



Afterwards I developed a partnership with a nonprofit called Africa Digital Rights Hub, and it has since taken me back to run masterclasses for executives and students in Ghana and Uganda. Part of the reason Western Carolina appealed to me was that they wanted to help me expand that work.Advertisement. Scroll to continue reading.


Q. You had about three weeks between that invitation from the Ghanaian government and actually getting on the plane. What was it like to just drop everything and go?
A. It was a scramble. Three weeks to get all my immunizations, book everything, and fly to Ghana. There is a real chance to democratize security education and take down some of the barriers the industry has put up.



Q. You have a phrase for how you think about the work now, “creativity within constraints.” When you started in the new context of higher ed, what did you assume your constraints would be?
A. I assumed it would be budget. In financial services, I could get almost anything as long as it was reasonable. Budgets are definitely tight in higher ed, but it was not my number one problem. I needed to learn the environment.A university has done things a certain way for a long time, knowledge is meant to be open and shared, and the place can feel like family. Once I really understood the environment, I could get creative and start designing security that worked with the culture instead of against it.



Q. You’ve been candid with me that you got the first few months wrong, and I think that honesty helps other leaders even more than sharing wins. What happened?
A. I went in thinking I already knew how everything should work, and I started fixing things. I probably  pushed harder than I should have before I understood the place. What I should have done was go in to learn, listen more than I talked, and document what I found before I tried to solve anything. Overseeing the security program at a large company does not mean you know how to build one everywhere.



Q. So with a small team and a tight budget, how do you actually run a real program? Walk me through the mechanics.
A. It starts with risk, not compliance. If you do risk well, compliance comes almost for free, because it tells you what you need to do and in what order. So I built a real risk register. I went to the technology leaders first and asked what was broken and what was holding them back, wrote it all down, and mapped it to a framework.



Then the honest problem is capacity. I cannot hire a team of analysts to test every control or spend weeks each year on a self-assessment. Self-attestation is not enough on its own. Anyone who has run a program knows that asking whether a control works can get you different answers depending on who you ask, so I needed real evidence, not opinions. The only way to get that at scale was to embrace automation and continuous monitoring.



Q. You have now lived on both sides. What would you take from higher ed back into a big enterprise?
A. Fiscal discipline, honestly. The habit of asking “do we really need this, or is it a nice-to-have,” and whether a given feature is three million dollars better than what we already have. In large enterprises you also end up with people inserting themselves into processes to justify a role, and everything takes longer. But look, every company of every size has something it could do better. I’ve really embraced how scarcity forces you to be more efficient.



Q. How can you judge whether someone will make a good CISO?
A. This one question tells me what I need to know: Do you know how your company makes money? Can you actually tell me how your organization makes money, or in a university, how the institution succeeds? Once you understand that, you can prioritize risk in a way that matters to the business. If you cannot answer it, you are going to protect the wrong things.



Q. What kind of CISO actually thrives in a place like higher ed?
A. You have to want to get your hands dirty. If you just want to run a team of forty and just go to meetings, it will not work, because your team is smaller and you will be doing some of the work yourself. You have to be creative enough to build things that are not the industry standard. And most of all, you have to see the business-enablement side of security, not only the technical side. You are engaging with the colleges, the chancellor, even the athletics department, and your job is to enable them. If you come in 100% security or bust, you will not last long in higher ed.



Q. Matt, thank you for this. What is on your radar right now? Is there anything you are most excited to build next?
A. Giving students a real place to learn. I want more working CISOs to share with students what the career actually looks like. Where I would love to take it is a hands-on cybersecurity lab on campus, for students to get real-world practice. I would also love to keep building the connections with universities in Africa, so students can learn from each other in both directions.



Ground truth for your program
Matt’s whole approach is a posture, not a tactic. He leads as a teacher and a collaborator, and treats security as a way to help organizations succeed rather than a roadblock.



The lesson I would take to any team, at any budget: name your real constraint before you invest against the wrong one. Matt walked in certain it was budget, and it turned out to be understanding the environment. Assume it’s money and you pour your limited resources into tools, when the real problem was one money could not solve. Get the constraint right, though, and every dollar and every control is aimed at a real business objective, which is what makes a program effective at any size.



So before your next purchase or project, ask yourself two questions: What do I actually believe my top constraint is, and how do I know? And am I about to spend against it, or around it?



Join the CISO Forum 2026 Year-End Review Webinar | December 2, 2026

Q. I’ve heard you say that security and GRC have become an elite sport. Job postings demand degrees and a wall of certifications before you can apply, but attackers have none of them. You are largely self-taught. What has that taught you about who belongs in this field?
A. I started with a history degree, and some of the best security people I know do not have a security degree either. Think how this community started. In the beginning you couldn’t go get a degree in cybersecurity, it was creative people figuring it out as they went. There are brilliant people all over the world who could do this work and simply haven’t had the opportunity. That is why the teaching matters so much to me. There is a real chance to democratize security education and take down some of the barriers the industry has put up.

Q. Matt, thank you for this. What is on your radar right now? Is there anything you are most excited to build next?
A. Giving students a real place to learn. I want more working CISOs to share with students what the career actually looks like. Where I would love to take it is a hands-on cybersecurity lab on campus, for students to get real-world practice. I would also love to keep building the connections with universities in Africa, so students can learn from each other in both directions.



Ground truth for your program
Matt’s whole approach is a posture, not a tactic. He leads as a teacher and a collaborator, and treats security as a way to help organizations succeed rather than a roadblock.



The lesson I would take to any team, at any budget: name your real constraint before you invest against the wrong one. Matt walked in certain it was budget, and it turned out to be understanding the environment. Assume it’s money and you pour your limited resources into tools, when the real problem was one money could not solve. Get the constraint right, though, and every dollar and every control is aimed at a real business objective, which is what makes a program effective at any size.



So before your next purchase or project, ask yourself two questions: What do I actually believe my top constraint is, and how do I know? And am I about to spend against it, or around it?



Join the CISO Forum 2026 Year-End Review Webinar | December 2, 2026

Q. So with a small team and a tight budget, how do you actually run a real program? Walk me through the mechanics.
A. It starts with risk, not compliance. If you do risk well, compliance comes almost for free, because it tells you what you need to do and in what order. So I built a real risk register. I went to the technology leaders first and asked what was broken and what was holding them back, wrote it all down, and mapped it to a framework.



Then the honest problem is capacity. I cannot hire a team of analysts to test every control or spend weeks each year on a self-assessment. Self-attestation is not enough on its own. Anyone who has run a program knows that asking whether a control works can get you different answers depending on who you ask, so I needed real evidence, not opinions. The only way to get that at scale was to embrace automation and continuous monitoring.



Q. You have now lived on both sides. What would you take from higher ed back into a big enterprise?
A. Fiscal discipline, honestly. The habit of asking “do we really need this, or is it a nice-to-have,” and whether a given feature is three million dollars better than what we already have. In large enterprises you also end up with people inserting themselves into processes to justify a role, and everything takes longer. But look, every company of every size has something it could do better. I’ve really embraced how scarcity forces you to be more efficient.



Q. How can you judge whether someone will make a good CISO?
A. This one question tells me what I need to know: Do you know how your company makes money? Can you actually tell me how your organization makes money, or in a university, how the institution succeeds? Once you understand that, you can prioritize risk in a way that matters to the business. If you cannot answer it, you are going to protect the wrong things.



Q. What kind of CISO actually thrives in a place like higher ed?
A. You have to want to get your hands dirty. If you just want to run a team of forty and just go to meetings, it will not work, because your team is smaller and you will be doing some of the work yourself. You have to be creative enough to build things that are not the industry standard. And most of all, you have to see the business-enablement side of security, not only the technical side. You are engaging with the colleges, the chancellor, even the athletics department, and your job is to enable them. If you come in 100% security or bust, you will not last long in higher ed.



Q. Matt, thank you for this. What is on your radar right now? Is there anything you are most excited to build next?

I would also love to keep building the connections with universities in Africa, so students can learn from each other in both directions.

[44] SecurityWeek - Plagiarism Checker Turnitin Investigating Cyberattack
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Wed, 25 Nov 2026 10:40:07 +0000
Link: https://www.securityweek.com/plagiarism-checker-turnitin-investigating-cyberattack/

[TAM METİN - 2821 kelime]
Plagiarism Checker Turnitin Investigating Cyberattack

The edtech company says the systems are fully operational and safe to use while it investigates the incident.

Flipboard





Reddit





Whatsapp





Whatsapp





Email

Education technology platform Turnitin has disclosed a cyberattack involving certain customer-facing systems.
A subsidiary of Advance Publications, Turnitin provides plagiarism checking solutions used by thousands of education and research institutions worldwide.



“We are writing to let you know that we recently identified unauthorized activity involving certain customer-facing Turnitin systems,” CEO Chris Caren revealed in a November 23 letter on the company’s website.



The company immediately initiated its incident response plan, notified the relevant authorities, and launched an investigation with support from external cyber experts.



“We can confirm our systems are fully operational and safe to use and that these customer-facing systems do not collect government identification credentials or institutional financial information,” Caren said.



The company continues to monitor the affected customer-facing systems while its investigation into the nature and scope of the incident continues.Advertisement. Scroll to continue reading.


Customer-facing Turnitin systems include the submission portal for students, a grading interface for instructors, integration APIs for institutions, and reporting dashboards. The company did not say if any or which of these systems was breached.



Caren did not say if any data was accessed or how many customers might have been affected, but the University of Colorado Boulder said on Tuesday it was in touch with the company to understand if any CU Boulder information was compromised.



“At this time, we have no indication that student or instructor information, assignments, or other CU Boulder data was exposed. Since Turnitin is primarily accessed via a Canvas integration, OIT has added additional monitoring to ensure Canvas has not been impacted,” the university’s Office of Information Technology said.



SecurityWeek has emailed Turnitin for additional information on the incident and will update this article if the company responds.

Related: 1.5 Million Impacted by Data Breach at South Korean Electric Company KEPCO

[45] SecurityWeek - Hackers Exploit Unpatched Flaw in Defunct Idunno AntiCsrf Library
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Wed, 25 Nov 2026 09:05:13 +0000
Link: https://www.securityweek.com/hackers-exploit-unpatched-flaw-in-defunct-idunno-anticsrf-library/

[TAM METİN - 1880 kelime]
Hackers Exploit Unpatched Flaw in Defunct Idunno AntiCsrf Library

Attackers can exploit CVE-2026-131914 to achieve remote code execution via a single web request containing specially crafted data.

Flipboard





Reddit





Whatsapp





Whatsapp





Email

According to preemptive cybersecurity firm WatchTowr, the issue exists because AntiCsrf does not perform sufficient checks on data from incoming form submissions before reconstructing it into program objects.Advertisement. Scroll to continue reading.


“The vulnerable check runs before the application’s own page logic, so it can affect many pages on an affected site, not just ones that use a login form. A successful attack gives the attacker control over the process that runs the website, which can lead to full compromise of the server,” WatchTowr says.



On Tuesday, the US cybersecurity agency CISA added CVE-2026-131914 to its Known Exploited Vulnerabilities (KEV) list, urging federal agencies to address it within three days.



As no official patch is available for the vulnerability, all organizations relying on the 18-year-old AntiCsrf to validate anti-CSRF tokens on form submissions should immediately discontinue the module and transition to an actively supported solution.



“WatchTowr Intel detected exploit probes across our honeypots that used creative .NET compiled gadget payloads to confirm if a given system is vulnerable. We validated the exploit and confirmed that, if the host was vulnerable, the server would be tricked into redirecting to a unique URL that would discreetly confirm control of the web server to the attacker,” WatchTowr principal threat intelligence specialist Yordan Ganchev told SecurityWeek.



“There is no patch, and likely there won’t be one. Defenders should audit ASP.NET products to identify ones packaging the Idunno.AntiCSRF library and review their audit logs for signs of exploitation. Where possible, the library should be replaced in affected applications. Where that’s not possible, apply WAF rules until alternative workarounds are identified,” Ganchev added.



Related: Palo Alto Networks Patches Fragnesia Flaw Used to Root Firewalls
Related: Drupal Flaw Found by Anthropic Scanner Exploited in Attacks
Related: phpAfterShell Vulnerability Revives PHP2Shell’s Attack Path
Related: Azure Flaw Exploited to Target Secrets at Norwegian Energy Firms

[47] Help Net Security - Proxmox 9.3 adds production snapshots for SAN-backed VMs
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Thu, 26 Nov 2026 06:07:23 +0000
Link: https://www.helpnetsecurity.com/2026/11/26/proxmox-9-3-adds-production-snapshots-for-san-backed-vms/

[TAM METİN - 330 kelime]
Proxmox 9.3 adds production snapshots for SAN-backed VMs

Proxmox Virtual Environment 9.3 adds production support for snapshots on shared LVM storage over iSCSI and Fibre Channel SANs. The open-source virtualization platform also gives each guest an identity that survives migration, backup and restore.

If your VMs sit on a SAN, you can now snapshot them before a risky change in a setup the vendor supports for production. The snapshots work as volume chains on thick-provisioned LVM, the layer that carves SAN disks into guest storage. Admins turn it on in the advanced storage settings with the “Allow Snapshots as Volume-Chain” option.

The guest identity matters for backups. It stays the same even when a guest gets a new VMID or is restored to another cluster, so backup software and outside management tools can recognize the same machine across its life.

SAN setup moves into the web interface

Multipath means several network routes to the same storage LUN, and admins used to configure it node by node. A guided workflow now sets it up across cluster nodes for iSCSI, Fibre Channel and NVMe over Fabrics. A new Datacenter > SAN view shows LUN path state across all nodes in real time.

New logs and network isolation

The web interface gains a color-coded, filterable System Log, an API Access Log, and an APT History of past package transactions and changelogs. Simple SDN zones can use a dedicated VRF, a separate routing table, with custom static routes. WireGuard fabrics report interface and peer status.

Ceph key rotation is still a preview

Ceph Umbrella 21.2 ships as a technology preview, with a hardened default cipher for CephX authentication and online key rotation for daemons. Preview status means you test it before trusting it. Ceph Tentacle 20.2 is the stable version.

Underneath, 9.3 runs on Debian 13.8 with Linux kernel 7.0 as the default and kernel 7.3 as an opt-in. Upgrades from older versions run through APT, and a full ISO installs on bare metal.

[48] Help Net Security - More than 4,400 malicious domains are waiting for holiday shoppers
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Thu, 26 Nov 2026 06:00:53 +0000
Link: https://www.helpnetsecurity.com/2026/11/26/fortinet-holiday-shopping-cyber-threats-report/

[TAM METİN - 725 kelime]
More than 4,400 malicious domains are waiting for holiday shoppers

Cybercriminals are positioned to exploit the holiday shopping season through fraudulent storefronts, retailer impersonation, payment fraud and account takeover attacks, according to FortiGuard Labs’ Holiday Season Cyber Threats Report 2026.

Researchers identified more than 24,000 newly registered domains containing keywords associated with seasonal shopping over a 90-day period, including more than 4,400 classified as malicious. Underground marketplaces are facilitating the trade in stolen customer information, access to e-commerce infrastructure and services that support financial fraud.

Distribution of holiday-related themes across the newly-registered domain dataset (Source: FortiGuard Labs)

Delivery scams and retailer impersonation

Parcel, delivery and shipping terminology accounted for 34.88% of newly registered holiday-related domains, making it the most prevalent theme in the dataset. Other registrations incorporated references to discounts, rewards, gift cards and retail brands.

Attackers can use imitation delivery websites to request payment information under the pretext of missed deliveries, customs fees or delivery rescheduling. Domains referencing DHL, UPS, FedEx and USPS were identified during the investigation.

Researchers identified a fraudulent website impersonating Walmart and advertising Black Friday deals. The storefront reproduced the retailer’s branding, color scheme and product listings to create the appearance of a legitimate shopping destination.

Consumers directed to such websites through advertisements, search results or promotional emails could be tricked into disclosing login credentials, submitting payment information or purchasing products that never arrive.

Fraudulent storefronts do not always rely on impersonating retailers in domain names. Separate research from Doppel found that Black Friday scams began appearing earlier this year, with most confirmed fake pages using web addresses that do not resemble those of the impersonated brands. Approximately one in four lookalike pages were hosted on legitimate platforms such as Google Sites, Blogger and GitHub Pages.

More than 1.7 million retail-related infostealer logs circulate underground

Between August and October 2026, researchers collected approximately 1.72 million infostealer log entries associated with seven major e-commerce platforms from a Russian marketplace. Amazon accounted for approximately 1.49 million entries, followed by eBay with 82,900 and AliExpress with 66,410.

Stolen credentials and session data could enable unauthorized account access, fraudulent purchases and misuse of stored payment information.

Cybercrime services facilitate retail fraud

Underground forums contain advertisements for subscription-based phishing services, automated payment card validation tools and phishing kits advertised as capable of bypassing MFA.

Criminals are advertising banking credentials, compromised gift card information and access to merchant systems. Fraudulently obtained gift cards can be resold or converted into cash or cryptocurrency.

Researchers also identified an advertised refund-as-a-service operation targeting retailers, food delivery companies, electronics vendors and travel platforms. The operator claims to secure refunds on purchases in exchange for a commission. Such services could exploit return policies and customer support processes.

Communications infrastructure is another component of the underground economy. Listings advertise caller ID spoofing, SMS delivery and bulk email distribution services that could support impersonation campaigns.

Sellers also advertise ready-made social media accounts, advertising campaign management and artificial engagement services. Purchased followers, comments and views could make fraudulent social media profiles and deceptive product advertisements appear more credible.

E-commerce vulnerabilities put retailers at risk

Cybercrime forums contain advertisements for alleged zero-day vulnerabilities, exploit chains and unauthorized access to e-commerce platforms such as Magento, PrestaShop, WordPress and OpenCart.

Access to compromised retail infrastructure could allow attackers to modify checkout pages, deploy payment-skimming scripts, redirect shoppers to fraudulent payment pages and extract customer databases.

Documented exploitation of vulnerabilities affecting Adobe Commerce (Magento) and other web application environments adds to the risks facing online businesses. Security flaws involving remote code execution, unrestricted file uploads and authorization bypass can leave merchant systems vulnerable to compromise.

AI tools enter the holiday fraud ecosystem

Cybercriminals are exploring the use of AI to automate parts of their operations, develop malicious software and produce social engineering content tailored to specific audiences.

One forum user sought an unrestricted LLM capable of generating Romanian-language communications that reflect local financial practices. Such capabilities could make fraudulent messages more convincing to recipients.

Another user described developing an orchestration framework that reportedly coordinates multiple AI agents. The developer claims the framework can circumvent model safety restrictions. Such systems could reduce the manual effort involved in reconnaissance and attack development.

AI-generated videos present another potential avenue for deception. Commercial media generation platforms could be misused to produce fabricated customer testimonials, misleading product demonstrations and advertisements impersonating established brands.

[49] Help Net Security - Google mandates MCP or CLI access for Workspace AI agents starting February 2027
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Thu, 26 Nov 2026 05:30:05 +0000
Link: https://www.helpnetsecurity.com/2026/11/26/google-ai-agent-security-requirements/

[TAM METİN - 642 kelime]
Google mandates MCP or CLI access for Workspace AI agents starting February 2027

Google has announced security controls across Google Workspace and Google Cloud to address the risks associated with autonomous AI agents accessing enterprise data, executing tasks, and interacting with cloud infrastructure.

The measures include dedicated agent identities, human approval controls for sensitive operations, prompt injection screening, and restrictions on how third-party AI tools access and process organizational data.

The company also announced the general availability of its Workspace Model Context Protocol (MCP) servers and command-line interface (CLI), along with new requirements for AI agents connecting to its productivity services.

“Policy checks, data boundaries, and human confirmation have to live in the request path,” Chandu Thota, VP of Engineering at Google Workspace, and Brad Calder, President of Google Cloud Platform and SRE, wrote.

Google mandates new API access rules

Under the new Workspace AI Tools Addendum, AI agents must connect through Google’s Workspace MCP servers or CLI. Technical enforcement is scheduled to begin on February 1, 2027, while conventional applications can continue using existing REST APIs.

The MCP servers support Gmail, Google Drive, Docs, Sheets, Slides, Calendar, Chat, and Tasks, with support for Google Keep planned. Agent requests are checked against organizational permissions, DLP policies, and classification labels.

The Workspace CLI supports locally deployed agents and terminal-based automation through scoped OAuth authentication.

Dedicated agent identities and human approval

External AI agents connecting to Workspace must use dedicated OAuth client IDs, allowing administrators and users to distinguish AI agents from conventional applications.

Google is implementing least-privilege OAuth scopes and usage quotas to limit agent permissions and resource consumption. Time-limited approvals are planned for a future release, while quota-related billing is scheduled to begin on April 1, 2027.

In Google Cloud, agents receive individual identities through Agent Registry without relying on shared or long-lived keys. Their access and resource consumption are governed by identity and access management (IAM) policies and quotas.

Workspace MCP servers and the CLI enforce user confirmation for sensitive operations, such as sending emails, modifying Drive sharing permissions, or performing bulk edits.

Google Cloud customers can configure policies requiring MFA or human approval before agents access sensitive Cloud Storage buckets, change IAM permissions, or modify production infrastructure.

Workspace administrators can audit agent activity and revoke access through the admin console. Google plans to extend these capabilities to users.

Google Cloud customers can monitor agent behavior through Security Command Center, Agent Observability, Cloud Audit Logs, and Wiz. According to Google, revoking a Cloud Agent Identity or OAuth grant immediately terminates the associated access.

Prompt injection and unauthorized data access

Google Cloud Agent Gateway introduces protocol-level security controls for LLM, MCP, and Agent2Agent (A2A) communications.

The gateway verifies agent identities against Agent Registry, enforces IAM policies, restricts outbound connections through default-deny rules, and integrates Google’s Model Armor service to screen requests for prompt injection.

Google plans to introduce additional access restrictions that will enable Workspace administrators and users to prevent external AI agents from retrieving designated emails, files, and folders. These restrictions will build on existing Workspace DLP and client-side encryption capabilities.

New restrictions on enterprise data handling

The Workspace AI Tools Addendum prohibits the permanent storage of raw Workspace data, its use for model training, and unauthorized API scraping.

The addendum also establishes a 30-day retention limit for Google Technical Information, which includes vector embeddings, memory indexes, user profiles, and organizational graphs but excludes user data.

AI tool providers must comply with data retention and deletion requirements, including removing user data and associated technical information when an integration is disconnected or its access is revoked.

Google recommends that Workspace administrators review connected applications and agents, configure DLP policies, and monitor OAuth activity logs. Developers are advised to migrate their agent integrations to the supported interfaces and implement user confirmation and prompt injection protection mechanisms ahead of the February 2027 enforcement deadline.

[50] Help Net Security - People are willing to pay to vanish from search results
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Thu, 26 Nov 2026 05:00:06 +0000
Link: https://www.helpnetsecurity.com/2026/11/26/incogni-online-privacy-concerns-report/

[TAM METİN - 628 kelime]
People are willing to pay to vanish from search results

More than half of U.S. adults would like to see a log of everyone who has searched for their name online. In a survey of 1,008 adults by Incogni, 53% said they would like that record to cover the past five years, and 55% said they would pay to have their personal information permanently removed from all public search results.

“You can learn an extraordinary amount about someone without ever speaking to them. Their address, work history, and family connections may all be just a few searches away,” said Darius Belejevas, Head of Incogni.

“The real concern is no longer only what someone can find about you, but what AI can infer by connecting the dots. An address, an old workplace, or a family connection might seem harmless in isolation. Together, they can reveal patterns about someone’s life that they never intended to make public.”

Mixed feelings about being searched

Looking someone up before a date, a job interview or a meeting has become routine. Thirty-nine percent of respondents considered it a normal part of life for a friend, date or co-worker to search for them. Others would feel uncomfortable or consider it an invasion of privacy. Four in ten would worry if someone they had researched found out about it.

Curiosity and concern were the most common reactions to the idea of being researched in detail. Other respondents said they would feel anxious, upset, happy or excited.

Paying to find out who’s searching

Nearly half would pay to learn who has looked them up. Interest was highest for Gen Z, with 64% willing to pay.

Almost half would pay to know who’d searched for them online (Source: Incogni)

Conventional search engines do not provide such a record. People generally cannot see who has searched for them, what information was found or how it was interpreted.

Googling yourself first

Just over half of respondents have searched for their own name in the past five years. Applying for a job, renting a home and going on a date were the most common specific occasions for checking their online presence.

In each situation, someone else’s impression of them could influence an important decision. A self-search gives people an idea of what a hiring manager, landlord or potential partner might discover.

Hiding posts from family

Four in ten respondents have deleted or hidden a social media post out of concern about who might see it. Gen Z respondents were the most likely to do so.

Family members topped the list of people respondents wanted to hide their posts from, ahead of employers and ex-partners. Government and law enforcement ranked last.

Personal relationships can influence what people choose to share online. People may share different aspects of their lives with relatives, friends, partners and colleagues.

Data brokers and people-search sites compile home addresses, phone numbers, family connections and employment histories into searchable profiles. Individuals may never have chosen to publish this information, and adjusting their social media privacy settings will not remove it from third-party websites.

While 71% of Gen Z respondents would pay for permanent removal from public search results, only 37% of baby boomers would do the same. The numbers come from a hypothetical offer and do not measure actual purchases.

Older generations grew up when personal records were less accessible. Younger adults have had much of their lives documented online from an early age. For them, removing personal information may be a way to regain some control over their privacy.

“We can’t control every search made about us, but we can limit some of the information available to those searching. Reviewing your digital footprint and removing unnecessary personal details from websites is a good place to start,” Belejevas concluded.

[51] Help Net Security - Shufti Fraud Hub exposes suspicious links between seemingly legitimate accounts
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Wed, 25 Nov 2026 14:45:00 +0000
Link: https://www.helpnetsecurity.com/2026/11/25/shufti-fraud-hub/

[TAM METİN - 676 kelime]
Shufti Fraud Hub exposes suspicious links between seemingly legitimate accounts

Shufti has announced the launch of its Fraud Hub, a connected fraud intelligence layer designed to detect suspicious verification attempts, identify coordinated fraud networks and monitor emerging risks across the user lifecycle.

Shufti Fraud Hub connects identity, device, biometric, and behavioural signals to help businesses spot threats that individual checks may miss and make context-aware decisions before fraud escalates.

Traditional fraud prevention often relies on assessing accounts individually, particularly during onboarding. Yet passing identity verification does not guarantee that an account will remain trustworthy.

Fraudsters can operate multiple accounts using different identities, shared devices or payment details, with coordinated behaviours that appear legitimate when examined separately. Even when one fraudulent account is blocked, others within the same network may remain undetected and continue operating.

Shufti’s Identity Fraud Report 2026, based on data from its verification network across 11 industries during the first half of 2026, found that 98.78% of linked fraud clusters involved two to ten identities. It also found that 65.68% of matches between separate fraudulent verification attempts involved the reuse of forged identity documents.

Shufti Fraud Hub brings these signals together through four layers of fraud detection: Capture Integrity, Device & Network Intelligence, Document Forensics, and Biometrics. This connected fraud intelligence identifies threats such as manipulated camera feeds, suspicious devices, forged or altered documents, and biometric attacks. The findings contribute to a unified real-time risk score, giving businesses a clearer understanding of potential fraud during identity verification.

Beyond individual verification attempts, Fraud Hub examines relationships across a business’s entire user base to identify suspicious connections, recurring fraud patterns, and coordinated activity. Its account clustering capabilities help uncover potential fraud rings by analysing shared devices, payment details, and behavioural similarities.

This can also help identify previously flagged users attempting to return with different account or identity information.

These capabilities are particularly relevant in industries where fraudsters exploit multiple accounts to evade individual checks. In iGaming, fraudsters may create multiple player accounts using different identities to exploit welcome bonuses, promotional offers, or referral incentives.

While each account may pass KYC independently, shared devices, payment methods, or behavioural patterns can reveal coordinated bonus abuse. Fraud Hub helps operators identify these connections, uncover suspicious player networks, and investigate potential multi-accounting before further losses occur.

A similar challenge exists in FinTech, where fraudsters may open multiple accounts using seemingly unrelated identities and devices, or return after an account has been blocked. Individual accounts may appear legitimate, but connections through shared devices, IP addresses, or recurring behavioural patterns can indicate coordinated fraud or attempts to bypass existing controls. Fraud Hub helps FinTech platforms recognise these relationships, identify repeat offenders, and assess risks before additional accounts or transactions are affected.

The hub also extends fraud detection beyond onboarding through continuous monitoring. Changes in account behaviour, new connections to suspicious profiles, or signs of repeated abuse can trigger further risk assessment. At critical moments, such as account reactivation or withdrawals, businesses can use current risk intelligence to determine whether additional verification or investigation is necessary.

For fraud and risk teams, this connected view changes the investigation process. Instead of reviewing disconnected alerts individually, investigators can examine relationships between suspicious accounts, understand the indicators contributing to risk, and prioritise cases that may represent a broader threat.

“Fraudsters don’t operate one account at a time, but fraud prevention systems often assess them that way. An account can pass every identity check and still become part of a coordinated fraud operation. Shufti Fraud Hub addresses this gap by connecting the signals and relationships surrounding each user, giving businesses the context to recognise emerging threats and make better risk decisions,” said Raja Hassan Fayyaz, Vice President of Product at Shufti.

Fraud Hub also supports Shufti’s Onboard, Monitor and Assure approach, connecting fraud detection during initial verification with ongoing risk intelligence and additional checks at critical points in the user lifecycle. Existing Shufti users can activate this fraud intelligence within their current verification integration, without additional SDKs, separate API calls, or changes to the user-facing verification journey.

[52] Help Net Security - Critical Drupal installer flaw under active attack (CVE-2026-132165)
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Wed, 25 Nov 2026 13:37:28 +0000
Link: https://www.helpnetsecurity.com/2026/11/25/drupal-installer-rce-cve-2026-132165-exploited/

[TAM METİN - 404 kelime]
Critical Drupal installer flaw under active attack (CVE-2026-132165)

Attackers have been exploiting CVE-2026-132165, a flaw that lets them take over unpatched Drupal sites without logging in, since at least November 19, the Drupal Security Team has warned.

Drupal is a free and open-source web content management system used by government agencies, universities, media outlets and large enterprises.

The vulnerability is one of three that the US Cybersecurity and Infrastructure Security Agency (CISA) added to its Known Exploited Vulnerabilities catalog on Tuesday, and federal civilian agencies have until November 27 to fix it.

“These types of vulnerabilities are a frequent attack vector for malicious cyber actors and pose significant risks to the federal enterprise,” the agency noted.

The installer as a way in

The vulnerability was found by Anthropic’s OSS Scanner in install.php, the script that sets up a new Drupal site, and Drupal released fixes for it on November 18.

The team had no reports of attacks when the patches came out, but a later update to the advisory said the flaw was “exploited on live sites as early as 2026-11-19 19:00 UTC,” a day after the fixes. The advisory rates the flaw 20 out of 25, the top tier on Drupal’s risk scale.

On November 24, Searchlight Cyber researchers Kevin Gervot and Adam Kues, who found the same bug after Anthropic’s scanner had reported it, published a technical write-up showing how it can be exploited on stock Drupal configurations.

Updates and workarounds

The flaw affects Drupal 10 before 10.6.22, Drupal 11 before 11.3.22, and Drupal 11.4.x before 11.4.13, and admins should upgrade to 10.6.22, 11.3.22 or 11.4.13.

Drupal 11.2.x, 10.5.x and older branches, including Drupal 8 and 9, are end-of-life and won’t get a fix, so sites on those versions need to move to a supported branch.

“Some (but not all) exploit paths are mitigated if the web server cannot write to or change permissions on settings.php. In addition to upgrading, the Drupal Security Team recommends securing the Drupal installer after installation,” the advisory states.

This can be done by blocking access to /core/install.php in the web server configuration or in the .htaccess file in the web root, and admins who can’t upgrade right away should do this in the meantime.

Sites using Drupal Steward, the project’s paid web application firewall service, are protected against the known attack vectors, though Drupal still advises them to update in case attackers find other ways in.

[53] Help Net Security - Bots exploited leaked AWS key from Docker image in under 8 seconds
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Wed, 25 Nov 2026 11:22:32 +0000
Link: https://www.helpnetsecurity.com/2026/11/25/docker-aws-key-exposure-bots/

[TAM METİN - 889 kelime]
Bots exploited leaked AWS key from Docker image in under 8 seconds

A cloud access key left inside a newly published Docker image can be found and used by bots in less than eight seconds, Billy Tsouvalas and Nick Nikiforakis of Stony Brook University discovered after planting 300 decoy images on public container registries.

Docker images are packaged copies of software, bundled with everything needed to run it, and developers publish them to public registries such as Docker Hub, which hosts over 14 million of them, so others can download and use them.

Passwords and API keys left in these images have been studied before, and an earlier analysis of over 300,000 images, cited in the paper, found some type of secret in 8.5% of them. Tsouvalas and Nikiforakis wanted to know who goes looking for those secrets and what they do with them.

Over four months, they published 300 copies of the official nginx image on eight registries, including Docker Hub, Amazon ECR and Red Hat Quay. Each copy held decoy AWS, OpenAI and Anthropic API keys.

The images were not advertised anywhere, but the AWS keys still received over 140,000 API calls from more than 5,000 IP addresses, and the OpenAI and Claude keys were drained of their entire balances. The researchers call the scanners behind this activity “ContainerBots.”

“In effect, publication and compromise are, for practical purposes, the same event,” they wrote.

Keys used within minutes

In the first round, all 100 images went to Docker Hub. More than half of the AWS keys were used within a minute of publication, 93% within five minutes and all of them within 15 minutes. The median time to a first call was 51 seconds, and the fastest call came 7.86 seconds after the image was pushed.

Docker Hub’s pull counter lags behind actual downloads by about 5 to 6 minutes. “This means that even a vigilant image owner monitoring their own pull count cannot react before a leaked credential has already been used,” the authors noted.

Bots skip running the images

Each image also had hidden callbacks that would alert the team if someone built a container from it, ran it or opened a shell inside it. None fired in the first round, and only three fired in the second round of 200 images.

The researchers used their observations to reconstruct the likely workflow. A bot queries a Docker Hub catalog endpoint that lists recently published or updated images and fetches each image’s manifest.

The manifest includes the build history, so the bot can tell which layers, the stacked pieces an image is built from, probably hold credentials. It downloads just those layers, unpacks them and searches the files for strings that look like keys or login details, and the whole thing takes a few seconds.

Almost every bot started by asking AWS a simple “who am I” question to see whether a key worked, and about half of the IP addresses stopped there.

The OpenAI and Claude keys followed what the researchers call a validate-wait-exploit pattern. OpenAI keys saw small bursts of use after each image push, then went quiet for several weeks.

About a month after the last push, a single key received 143 requests in under 24 hours, burning through over 18 million input tokens and emptying the account. The Claude keys were drained in a single day after the image update on day 8.

“We speculate that the wait phase reflects validated keys being resold and held idle until a buyer eventually uses them,” the paper reads.

In the second round, some OpenAI and Anthropic keys had locked-down permissions, and none of them were used to consume tokens. “Not a single Restricted key received any token-consuming call, indicating that scanners are able to discriminate between Open and Restricted permissions,” the researchers wrote.

Sensitive names draw more pulls

The second round spread 200 images over eight registries and varied their names. Some had bland names like “silverporgy/bait-tin-stack,” and others hinted at sensitive infrastructure, such as “policyengine/classified-rule-store.”

On Docker Hub, the second group drew more than double the pulls per image, and the difference grew after every update. The paper suggests that a share of the bots favor “images whose namespaces and names evoke high-value, sensitive infrastructure.”

The researchers propose two changes that registry operators could make on their own. The first is to scan images for known key formats when they are pushed and warn the uploader. “This would not solve the problem, but it would close the overwhelmingly common case of accidental disclosure,” they wrote.

The second targets the Docker Hub catalog endpoint that lists the newest images. Legitimate users of that feed, such as CI/CD pipelines and registry mirrors, usually have authenticated access already, so the authors suggest requiring authentication, adding per-account rate limits or limiting what the endpoint shows publicly.

“The design question is whether a publicly unauthenticated list of ‘newest image’ metadata is actually necessary,” they wrote.

When the paper was written, Docker had acknowledged the disclosure and its security team was reviewing the findings.

The experiment cost about $60 in OpenAI and Anthropic credits, all of it spent by attackers. “While we recognize that making funds available to underground ecosystems is less than ideal, we could not have learned about the behavior of attackers without these keys,” the researchers concluded.

[54] Help Net Security - Anthropic wants to catch rogue AI activity without retaining your data
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Wed, 25 Nov 2026 09:20:14 +0000
Link: https://www.helpnetsecurity.com/2026/11/25/anthropic-enterprise-frontier-safeguards-public-beta/

[TAM METİN - 444 kelime]
Anthropic wants to catch rogue AI activity without retaining your data

Anthropic has launched a public beta of Enterprise Frontier Safeguards (EFS), enabling organizations to detect potentially harmful AI activity across multiple interactions while maintaining zero data retention (ZDR).

The capability uses automated safety systems to analyze conversations stored in customer-controlled cloud environments and flag suspicious behavior for security teams to investigate.

“With EFS, customers can keep sensitive data in their own environments, protected with their own encryption keys, while still preserving the specialized safety mechanisms frontier capabilities require. That balance, keeping enterprises in control of their data without abandoning frontier-model safety, is critical to broader adoption of advanced AI in security-sensitive organizations like Cisco,” said Omar Santos, Distinguished Engineer at Cisco.

Detecting AI misuse across multiple requests

Legacy ZDR safeguards screen individual requests for signs of misuse. However, sophisticated attacks can span several interactions, each of which may appear harmless when examined separately.

Anthropic’s September threat intelligence report documented an operation targeting roughly 50 organizations in which an AI agent delegated attack tasks to other agents and retained stolen credentials between sessions.

EFS addresses this limitation by adding a layer of automated monitoring that identifies patterns of malicious behavior across requests.

When the system detects a potential threat, it creates a Safety Case that security teams can retrieve from their cloud storage or access through an API. Cases can be integrated into security information and event management (SIEM) systems and contain neither prompts nor responses.

The monitoring can help identify both deliberate misuse and instances of AI agents acting beyond their users’ intentions, such as exploiting software vulnerabilities to gain unauthorized access to internal databases or external systems.

Keeping sensitive data under customer control

Organizations store AI inputs and outputs in their own cloud environments. According to Anthropic, neither the company nor its cloud partners retain copies once the information has been transferred to customer-controlled storage.

Customers configure access policies and audit logging and encrypt safety data with their own keys. Provider-side access to conversation content is restricted to automated safety monitoring systems. Neither Anthropic nor cloud provider personnel can review it, and the information is not used to train models or classifiers.

EFS is available through the Claude Platform, Claude Platform on AWS, Amazon Bedrock and Claude on Google Cloud. For Amazon Bedrock and Google-managed deployments, the respective cloud provider operates the safeguards, and the entire data flow remains within its environment. Support for Microsoft Foundry is coming soon.

Depending on the deployment, customers can use Amazon S3 or Google Cloud Storage and are responsible for the associated storage and usage costs.

Eligible organizations will receive instructions on how to get started.

[55] The Record - Microsoft: China’s Salt Typhoon hackers are using Artex
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Wed, 25 Nov 2026 21:18:00 GMT
Link: https://therecord.media/microsoft-china-salt-typhoon-using-artex-ai-tool

[TAM METİN - 592 kelime]
Microsoft: China’s Salt Typhoon hackers are using Artex

State-backed hacking groups in China and North Korea are using a powerful AI hacking tool in attacks, according to a new report from Microsoft.

Microsoft explained that state-sponsored and financially motivated hackers are using Artex, an AI-powered platform that can automate multiple phases of cyber intrusions at machine speed.

The study emerged hours after The New York Times and NextGov reported that Artex was allegedly used in attacks on the CIA, the National Geospatial-Intelligence Agency and the Office of the Director of National Intelligence.

Microsoft said on Wednesday that Chinese state-backed groups Crystal Typhoon and Salt Typhoon have integrated Artex into their suite of tools, using it for “network reconnaissance, credential attacks, and web application exploitation.”

“In the past few weeks, we’ve reported several distinct campaigns that abused Artex to search public repositories for exposed secrets, enable supply chain compromise, and scan internet-facing systems for exploitation,” Microsoft explained.

Salt Typhoon is a prolific Chinese hacking group that U.S. agencies believe was behind the breach of at least nine telecommunications giants in 2024. The hack alarmed White House officials in 2024 because it allegedly gave China’s hackers access to communications from the phones of President Donald Trump and Vice President JD Vance as well as several Democratic leaders.

Salt Typhoon was also accused of attacking several European governments and critical infrastructure organizations globally.

Artex was released in July, and according to Microsoft, it has been steadily added to the arsenal of multiple North Korean and Chinese groups.

Artex allows threat actors to define specific goals and use AI agents to attack targets. Microsoft said Artex “can advance from reconnaissance to persistent, privileged access to a target organization in a matter of minutes.”

“ARTEX does not introduce new attack techniques; it uses common, pre-existing penetration testing tools and techniques. ARTEX does sometimes transmit distinctive characteristics associated with its integrated penetration testing tools,” Microsoft explained.

“However, threat actors using ARTEX can and do vary network indicators like source IP address or user agent string, especially to bypass static or simple signature-based detection and blocking. The most reliable and durable signal of ARTEX activity is behavioral: ARTEX sends a wide variety of requests in a brief time and retries variations on the same technique or moves on to next-stage actions at machine speed.”

By default, Artex does not take measures to hide its activity, the researchers added.

While the Chinese researcher behind the tool initially removed the source code from GitHub last month, other developers made copies, and at least one Chinese company now offers commercial access to a version of it.

Microsoft noted that defenders can tell when Artex is being used based on bursts of machine speed activity and by the “characteristically large volume of anomalous traffic from a single source in a short time.”

The FBI released its own advisory on Artex two weeks ago, warning that it is being used prolifically by cybercriminals and nation-states to breach government agencies and dozens of critical sectors worldwide.

The tool was recently implicated in multiple attacks on South Korean and Japanese financial institutions, causing national scandals in both countries. Microsoft previously said Artex was used by Chinese state-backed actors in a recent supply chain attack on IT management software company ConnectWise.

is a Breaking News Reporter at Recorded Future News. Jonathan has worked across the globe as a journalist since 2014. Before moving back to New York City, he worked for news outlets in South Africa, Jordan and Cambodia. He previously covered cybersecurity at ZDNet and TechRepublic.

"FIFA is working closely with the relevant authorities, including law enforcement,” a spokesperson said.

The hacking group emerged last week and little is known about its members or associations.

The FIFA incident follows other recent attacks on European soccer leagues. Spain’s top soccer league LaLiga said on Monday that hackers stole personal information belonging to fans in a cyberattack affecting its digital services and those of several Spanish clubs.

In May, Dutch police arrested a 35-year-old man suspected of hacking into the computer systems of Ajax, one of the Netherlands’ most prominent soccer clubs. The French Football Federation also disclosed a cyberattack in 2025.

[57] The Record - Florida ransomware recovery CEO indicted for allegedly paying hacker ransoms
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Wed, 25 Nov 2026 17:30:00 GMT
Link: https://therecord.media/ransomware-doj-indictment-recovery

[TAM METİN - 593 kelime]
Florida ransomware recovery CEO indicted for allegedly paying hacker ransoms

The CEO of a ransomware recovery firm was indicted by a federal grand jury after prosecutors accused him of lying about his services and simply paying out ransoms to cybercriminals.

Colombian national Juan Ricardo Palacio Escobar, 52, is the co-founder of a company based in Florida that marketed itself as a data recovery service for ransomware victims.

Palacio claimed he had decryption tools that allowed him to help victims without negotiating or paying ransomware gangs. Instead, Palacio allegedly paid ransoms and charged his customers enough to make a profit.

Federal agencies have increasingly sought to crack down on the murky industry of ransomware recovery and cybercriminal mediation in recent months.

“As alleged, the defendant preyed on ransomware victims by falsely marketing a solution he did not possess, secretly negotiating with cybercriminals, and profiting from the difference,” said Charles Calenda, first assistant U.S. attorney.

“Businesses and individuals seeking to recover from a cyberattack should not be victimized for a second time by those they turn to for help.”

He is facing three counts of wire fraud, which carry a maximum sentence of 60 years in prison.

Before the DOJ announced the charges, the news outlet 404 Media and independent journalist Brian Krebs published details of Escobar’s operation, writing that he ran a company called Ransomwarehelp.com. The company’s website currently says it is under maintenance.

On Wednesday, Assistant Attorney General A. Tysen Duva confirmed previous reports that the FBI staged an operation in June where they posed as a ransomware gang and victim.

The FBI went to Palacio for assistance and was told his company “never negotiated with or paid the ransom to ransomware attackers but instead could recover the data through its sophisticated decryption technology.”

“The indictment alleges that despite these representations, Palacio did negotiate with and make a payment in the amount of approximately $6,000 in Bitcoin to the purported ransomware actor, which was also an undercover FBI operative, in exchange for the decryption key,” prosecutors said.

“Palacio charged the undercover company approximately $13,260 for his company’s purported decryption technology services.”

He was arrested in Las Vegas on October 22 before being transferred to Rhode Island. He was released on pretrial bond on November 20.

“As alleged, Mr. Palacio Escobar is the proverbial wolf in sheep’s clothing, representing both himself and his company as experts in data recovery, when all the while he was paying the perpetrators of these ransomware attacks, under the guise of helping his clients,” FBI agent Ted Docks said. “Little did they know he was ripping them off.”

A cybersecurity firm that spoke to Krebs claimed it was hired by a ransomware victim who alleged Palacio had deleted the ransomware notes from its system so that he would be the sole person in charge of communicating with the hackers.

Last month, the DOJ charged another Florida-based owner of a ransomware recovery firm for conducting a similar scheme.

Two ransomware negotiators were given four-year sentences in May after pleading guilty to conducting their own covert ransomware attacks and coordinating with ransomware gangs while purportedly negotiating ransoms on behalf of at least five victims.

Another cybersecurity executive was recently arrested and indicted for allegedly being involved in a prominent cybercriminal group that hacked the FBI.
