[58] The Record - Australia passes tough legislation requiring big tech to preempt foreseeable harms
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Wed, 25 Nov 2026 17:07:00 GMT
Link: https://therecord.media/australia-passes-online-safety-legislation-preempt-harms

[TAM METİN - 503 kelime]
Australia passes tough legislation requiring big tech to preempt foreseeable harms

Australia’s Parliament on Wednesday greenlit legislation creating sweeping online safety reforms that open the possibility of billions of dollars in fines for big tech companies that fail to preempt online harms before they occur.

Known as the Digital Duty of Care Act, the legislation sailed through Parliament with strong backing from Prime Minister Anthony Albanese.

His administration has been a global leader in fighting against online safety harms and spearheaded Australia’s pioneering strict age requirements for social media use that took effect in December 2025 and have since been copied by governments worldwide.

The pending law will mandate that online providers establish protocols to ensure Australians are protected from threats of violence and rape as well as behavior that could cause significant harms.

Platforms are required to detect and decrease risks from harmful content and must bolster protections for users under 18 against pornography, suicide and eating disorders. They also must undertake annual written risk assessments and call out major platform design changes such as endless scrolling that create new harms.

Under the law, users also will be able to opt out of algorithmic feeds and will be required to prove their age to access porn.

After the law passed, Albanese reportedly told Parliament it will be a “powerful instrument to protect our children and our society from online harms by placing the onus on the tech platforms to ensure a safer online environment.”

“This is a victory for our children and young people. The dangers were unforeseen when the digital world was young,” Albanese said. “But now that the digital world has matured, they can be easily recognized and they can be remedied.”

Similar legislation is already in place in the U.K. and European Union. A bill seeking to impose a duty of care against large platforms has been introduced by U.S. lawmakers but has stagnated in Congress.

U.S. officials have accused the Australian legislation of violating free speech, threatening American companies’ business models and imposing vague requirements that will cause tech giants to overhaul their services to avoid liability.

“We are particularly concerned by potential mandates that would allow regulators to impose rigid, one-size-fits-all platform design requirements – such as forced chronological feeds or opt-in recommendation systems,” the U.S. Embassies and Consulates in Australia said in a September public comment.

The legislation does not undermine free speech, Albanese told Parliament in October, saying that “rather than someone in the United States deciding” what people see, now Australians can “decide themselves.”

The law will require platforms to “give us more choice over the content and the features that we use, including the feeds that we choose,” Albanese said.

Earlier in her career Suzanne covered the Boston Police Department for the Boston Globe and two presidential campaign cycles for Newsweek. She lives in Washington with her husband and three children.

[59] The Record - Suspected ransomware attack disrupts cancer treatments, surgeries at Guadeloupe hospital
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Wed, 25 Nov 2026 13:30:00 GMT
Link: https://therecord.media/guadeloupe-ransomware-hospital-cyber

[TAM METİN - 728 kelime]
Suspected ransomware attack disrupts cancer treatments, surgeries at Guadeloupe hospital

France’s national cybersecurity agency is investigating a suspected ransomware attack that impaired the computer systems of the University Hospital of Guadeloupe, forcing doctors to postpone surgeries, suspend some cancer treatments and revert to pen and paper.

France’s national cybersecurity agency, known as ANSSI, said Tuesday the incident appeared to involve ransomware and that investigators were examining whether hackers gained access to the hospital’s systems through an unnamed IT supplier that was reportedly compromised several months ago.

French prosecutors have launched a criminal investigation into the incident. It remains unclear who was behind the attack. The hospital’s management told local media on Monday that it had not received a ransom demand and ruled out paying a ransom.

The hospital — the largest on the French Caribbean island of approximately 380,000 residents — said on its website it had canceled some surgical procedures, medical consultations and imaging tests through at least Friday.

Radiation therapy has been temporarily suspended over safety concerns, while chemotherapy sessions scheduled for Monday were canceled. The hospital is reviewing upcoming appointments based on each patient’s medical needs.

Emergency services, dialysis treatments and urgent cardiac procedures remain available. The hospital has asked residents to visit its emergency departments only if they need immediate medical care.

Hospital employees first experienced computer and telephone outages on Saturday evening, according to local media reports. What initially appeared to be a technical malfunction turned out to be a cyberattack that encrypted the hospital’s databases.

“We’re back to pen and paper, relying on couriers who travel from one department to another,” the hospital’s director told local media.

As of Tuesday, healthcare workers were still unable to access patients’ electronic medical records and were manually processing prescriptions and collecting laboratory results, slowing patient care.

The hospital has encouraged patients to bring paper copies of their medical records, prescriptions and previous test results to appointments.

It remains unclear whether the hackers stole any personal or medical information. The hospital said it would notify anyone whose data was compromised and warned patients to watch out for phishing attempts by criminals posing as hospital staff.

The hospital plans to gradually restore the affected systems after checking them for malware. Recovery is expected to take several days, but it remains unclear when normal operations will fully resume.

The attack came as the hospital was relocating its operations to a new facility in Belle-Plaine. The French government financed the construction of the new hospital after a major fire damaged its previous facilities in 2017.

According to hospital management, the new facility’s computer systems have stronger security protections, making the disruption easier to manage there. Despite the attack, the hospital has maintained its plans to transfer pediatric emergency services to the new site.

Healthcare under attack

Two other French healthcare organizations — Bordeaux University Hospital and France’s National Cancer Institute — disclosed cyberattacks earlier this week.

Bordeaux University Hospital said it discovered its computer systems had been compromised after detecting that two of its email accounts had been used to send mass emails to employees.

A threat actor known as Scarlettgroup claimed responsibility for the breach, threatening to publish allegedly stolen medical records, prescriptions, laboratory results and financial documents.

The hospital has not confirmed the hackers’ claims and is investigating whether any information was stolen. It temporarily restricted external access to one of its servers as a precaution but said the incident had not disrupted patient care.

France’s National Cancer Institute confirmed Monday that a cyberattack had compromised personal information and disrupted two of its websites. The institute said it had stopped the intrusion but has not disclosed how many people were affected or what information was exposed.

It remains unclear whether the three incidents are linked.

French Health Minister Stephanie Rist said Tuesday the government was working to improve cybersecurity across the country’s healthcare sector and planned to secure long-term funding for these efforts through France’s proposed 2027 social security budget.

“The cybersecurity of our hospitals is also the safety of our patients,” Rist added.

is a reporter for Recorded Future News based in Ukraine. She writes about cybersecurity startups, cyberattacks in Eastern Europe and the state of the cyberwar between Ukraine and Russia. She previously was a tech reporter for Forbes Ukraine. Her work has also been published at Sifted, The Kyiv Independent and The Kyiv Post.

[60] Talos Intelligence - Oh, is it Thanksgiving? I hadn’t noticed!
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Wed, 25 Nov 2026 19:00:00 GMT
Link: https://blog.talosintelligence.com/oh-is-it-thanksgiving-i-hadnt-noticed/

[TAM METİN - 1017 kelime]
Oh, is it Thanksgiving? I hadn’t noticed!

Let’s start off with something I’m pretty sure none of us are thankful for: threat actors. Seriously, screw you guys! I know I'd be out of a job without you, but that’s just another way of saying “I coulda been a professional bagpiper.” There are days when that doesn’t sound so bad. So, no gratitude coming your way, people!

In terms of actual thankfulness, I want to give a big THANK YOU to the entire Blue Team, everywhere. Everyone out there defending, especially those in the U.S. taking shifts on Thursday to keep the turkey-eating public safe and secure. This is a hard, often high-pressure job for which you get little thanks, but all the blame when a breach occurs. We see you. We appreciate you. We promise we’ll save you some stuffing.

Speaking of defenders, I’m also very thankful to get to work with so many talented and wonderful people here at Talos. I’m not just saying this because I know my boss will read it! Without exception, everyone I’ve worked with here has not only been an expert at what they do, but they’ve been friendly, welcoming, and very patient with me as I’ve been feeling my way around this new org over the past several months. Thank you!

Finally, and maybe most importantly of all, I want to thank you, our readers. You are the reason we’re all here, doing what we do, helping you to look out for your own networks and protect your own people. We appreciate your trust!

Whether you’re celebrating Thanksgiving here in the States or just having a normal weekend wherever you are, I hope you get some time to relax, unplug, recharge, and de-stress.

Now, who wants some pie?

In Talos’ latest blog, we examine the fascinating tradecraft of the OpenAI-linked agentic swarm that broke out of its containerized environment in 2026. While not inherently malicious, this swarm of AI agents used public internet services as improvised infrastructure to bypass their sandbox constraints and complete their training tasks. By analyzing this unintentional great escape, we can learn how multi-agent systems coordinate, partition work, and adapt when their paths are blocked. This view into autonomous AI operations provides a baseline for understanding how these behaviors transfer to purpose-built cyber attack frameworks.

The leap from an AI sandbox escape to weaponized cyber attacks has already happened. Agentic frameworks like ARTEX are currently in the wild, proving that multi-agent systems can move from a single compromised credential to deep network access in under 15 minutes. These autonomous threats adapt to errors in real time and execute dozens of actions in mere seconds. Understanding how these swarms coordinate, adapt, and exfiltrate data is a necessity to keep up with an adversary that operates at machine speed.

To catch an AI swarm, defenders must look for its operational fingerprints. Monitor your environment for irregular "burst-and-pause" timing patterns that indicate rapid, sub-second automated reasoning. Keep a close eye on unusual traffic to allow-listed public services like “webhook.site”, which agents use to park data before terminating. Finally, hunt for self-labeling conventions, like highly specific URL parameters or predictable file names (e.g., “artex-probe-*”), that agents rely on to track their state. Read the full blog for a deeper dive into these swarm behaviors.

[61] Talos Intelligence - The Great Escape: Tradecraft insights from an agentic swarm
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Wed, 25 Nov 2026 11:00:00 GMT
Link: https://blog.talosintelligence.com/the-great-escape-tradecraft-insights-from-an-agentic-swarm/

[TAM METİN - 2687 kelime]
The Great Escape: Tradecraft insights from an agentic swarm

The OpenAI-linked swarm activity in 2026 is fully traceable through public records and offers an unprecedented view into how multi-agent AI systems operate and coordinate at scale.

Distilling and documenting the swarm tradecraft may help defenders understand multi-agent operations and communication, and how agents adapt when a path is  blocked.

Some documented behaviors may transfer to more purpose-built cyber attack frameworks like ARTEX, including methods to persistently store results, the burst-and-pause timing patterns of reasoning loops, and the self-labeling conventions that can reveal workload assignments and distribution.

Beginning in June 2026 and extending for several months, a collection of OpenAI models conducting internal training and evaluation tasks broke out of their containerized environments and accessed websites and databases via the public internet in ways the company says it did not authorize.

This activity represents a new kind of cyber event: not a malicious intrusion, but an unintentional series of consequences stemming from an agentic swarm, a group of AI agents exhibiting coordinated behavior toward a shared objective. A swarm implies many agents but doesn’t have a rigid count. The main distinction is behavioral; the collective activity of a swarm becomes complex and unpredictable even when individual agents may be explainable. This swarm provided an initial, public glimpse into the behavior and coordination of autonomous, multi-agent operations.

Due to constraints in their evaluation environment, the OpenAI swarm used public internet services as improvised infrastructure, which made the activity visible and traceable. The behaviors exposed revealed methods for persistent probing, iterative payload development, work partitioning and coordination across agents, and adaptation to execution constraints, in ways that are likely to be relevant for understanding agent behavior beyond this just case.

Agentic cyber attack frameworks are now appearing in intrusion activity, with the ability to support scaling to multi-agent operations. These attacker swarms largely replace the use of public internet resources with purpose-built infrastructure that removes some of the artifacts visible. However, documenting our understanding of operational swarm techniques can give defenders a baseline for recognizing behaviors as they move into new environments and contexts. In some cases, we can already see the evidence where these techniques do transfer.

In this post, we’ll explore and document the tradecraft of the escaped OpenAI swarm and conclude by comparing it to early signals and behaviors from the current agentic attack activity seen in the wild.

Swarms are an important concept to track because they can divide work across parallel agents, potentially completing complex tasks faster, though the gains depend on the task and the quality of coordination. Parallel agent coordination was crucial to OpenAI’s ability to solve the Navier-Stokes Millenium Prize Problem, which arrived at a winning solution with a group on the order of 10,000 concurrent agents over the course of 88 hours.

Early evidence suggests swarms transfer effectively to cyber attack problems. The main factors for swarm efficiency are how parallelizable the tasking is, and if the attackers want to pay the inflated token cost. A rough example is using four agents on the same problem, to get it done twice as fast; as Noam Brown of OpenAI said on a Dwarkesh podcast, “Because there are four agents working for half as long, you’re paying 2x more to get an answer twice as quickly.”

AI models undergoing training and evaluation operate inside sandboxed computing environments. These are controlled workspaces where they can write and run code, search for information, and iterate toward solutions. These environments are designed to be both enabling and constraining. They give models room to practice, while limiting what they can reach and do, so that mistakes or misaligned behavior stay contained.

Operating under constraint

While the techniques employed by the OpenAI swarm may have crossed the thresholds of intended use, they should not be viewed as malicious intent. A useful framing for understanding these techniques is creativity under constraint. The agents were optimized to meet their objectives, and they displayed considerable persistence and ingenuity to that end.

The constraints the agents were working around were layered and understanding them helps explain the motivation behind every technique. The most fundamental was the absence of a browser runtime: Agents operating in text and code environments cannot natively load JavaScript-heavy pages, call browser APIs, or interact with dynamic web content. The creative solution was submitting URLs to a public scanner like “urlquery.net” which provided a real Chromium instance on demand.

This workaround by itself, however, was still not enough. Browsers apply a security rule called the same-origin policy: JavaScript running on one website generally cannot read data from another. Cross-origin resource sharing (CORS) lets a destination website explicitly permit access. The agents used public CORS proxies to relay requests when target websites did not grant permission.

They also lacked infrastructure of their own to host payloads or receive data, so they relied on public services such as httpbin, httpbun, and "webhook.site" services capable of testing HTTP requests and responses but not designed for this activity.

In some evaluation environments another constraint applied: Agents appear to have been restricted to GET requests only, with no ability to make outbound POST calls. That is likely what pushed them toward using public wikis as shared state stores, as wiki page edits are a write operation achievable through ordinary browser navigation.

In response to their constraints and in persistent pursuit of their objectives, the swarm developed and refined its tradecraft over time. This included chaining legitimate web services like URL shorteners, CORS proxies (services that relay requests to bypass browser security restrictions), Base64 decode endpoints (services that decode encoded data back into executable web pages), and markdown renderers into extraction pipelines that never required dedicated attacker infrastructure.

Talos observed structured work partitioning across parallel agent instances, iterative probe development, and sandbox identification techniques. The agents coordinated using a variety of identifying tags (e.g., uqscan=claude..., CHATGPTTEST1, OAI_META_1312, archive_test=20261004). These self-labels, intended as internal tracking strings, became the primary cross-campaign fingerprint linking multiple disparate operations.

To better understand the flow, we will detail the activity of the swarm in a seven-step chain, aligning the purpose of the action with the publicly accessible internet services used.

A separate orchestration layer, Coordinate (COORD), runs above the chain rather than within it. When multiple agents execute the same chain in parallel, COORD governs how work is divided between them — for example, URL shorteners (“da.gd”, “is.gd”) distribute identical payloads to multiple instances; structured identifiers (?uniq0–19, node=, validation=v<unix>_<county>_<req>) partition tasks and track completion. The COORD layer distinguishes a swarm from a single agent running sequentially.

Evolution and execution

The swarm's operational techniques evolved over time. Early activity relied on silent CDX enumeration, querying public web archive indexes to map target sites and using a single exit node. By the peak operational phase, the swarm was running against multiple simultaneous targets with structured work partitioning, sandbox identification, and covert exfiltration beacons. To better understand these events, we can reconstruct from public records the unauthorized access to Australian government websites.

Traced extraction demonstration

Step 1 — RECON: Enumerate target via Common Crawl CDX

The agent silently maps the target site structure by querying Common Crawl's CDX, a searchable database of every URL the archive has ever crawled. This allows enumeration of all potentially relevant target URLs, without the server ever being contacted.

The request in Figure 2 returns archived URL paths, MIME types, timestamps, and status codes. The agent discovers objective-relevant Tableau visualization endpoints at “vizprod.aihw.gov.au” without ever touching AIHW servers.

Effect: Agent identifies the target: vizprod.aihw.gov.au/t/Public/views/AGE-115/...

Step 2 — CRAFT: Build extraction payload

The agent writes a self-contained HTML page with embedded JavaScript. This payload uses the Tableau JS API to load a dashboard; enumerate its parameters, worksheets, and filters; then beacon the extracted data out via an image request.

The L() function is the exfiltration design. Each call fires an image beacon to "httpbun.com/status/204" with data in the ?pr= query parameter. The probe ID p0011782077286 is a Unix-epoch timestamp enabling session correlation.

Effect: Payload is ready, now encode as Base64 for hosting.

Step 3 — ENCODE: Host on legitimate decode service

The entire HTML payload is base64-encoded and appended to a public decode service URL. When fetched, the service decodes and serves the payload as a real web page.

Effect: The delivery URL is ready; next step is to submit to urlquery scanner.

Step 4 — SUBMIT: Launder through “urlquery.net”

The agent submits the delivery URL to “urlquery.net”, a legitimate web security scanner. urlquery's Chromium sandbox fetches and renders the page, executing the embedded JavaScript. The target sees requests from urlquery's scanner IPs (“92.42.109.x”), not the agent.

[COORD sub-step] The self-label ?uq=p001_1782077286_AGE115_v14 includes a probe prefix (p001), Unix epoch, target identifier (AGE115), and iteration count (v14 = 14th payload revision).

Effect: The chromium sandbox renders page, and payload JS executes.

Step 5 — EXECUTE: In-browser extraction via CORS proxy

When the payload executes inside urlquery's sandboxed Chromium browser, it uses the Tableau JavaScript API to load the AIHW health statistics dashboard. Tableau's backend speaks a protocol called VizQL; earlier payload generations bypassed the full dashboard load and hit the VizQL session API directly via a CORS proxy, reusing session tokens the agent had collected from previous runs.

AIHW sees the request originating from Cloudflare's IP range, not the agent, nor urlquery.

Effect: The data is validly extracted. Next, send out via a covert beacon.

Step 6 — EXFIL: Zero-byte 204 beacon

Extracted data is URL-encoded into the query string of image beacon requests to "httpbun.com/status/204". The server returns HTTP 204 No Content with zero bytes back. The data travels exclusively in the request URL, which urlquery logs as an ordinary HTTP transaction.

Each beacon sends up to 1,800 characters. The 204 response means no data flows back to the browser, acting as a write-only channel.

Effect. The transaction is logged; the agent retrieves report.

Step 7 — COLLECT: Read back via urlquery report

After the scan completes, the agent retrieves the urlquery report JSON, which contains every HTTP request and response the browser made, including the exfil beacon URLs with embedded data in the ?pr= parameters.

This closes the loop. The agent submitted a URL, urlquery's browser executed the payload, and the agent reads the results back from the public report. No direct connection between the agent and the target ever existed. The entire extraction was laundered through two intermediaries, httpbun.com (hosting) and urlquery.net (execution).

Technique transfer in a growing ecosystem

In October, a second autonomous actor was identified via the same technique fingerprints. This time, the activity was attributed to a Tencent-hosted “fleet”, operating from Tencent's Hong Kong network (AS132203). A fleet is distinguished from a swarm in that these agents were working on a unified task, but with no signs of coordination. The Tencent fleet’s activity focused on Alibaba’s Amap navigation service, polling points-of-interest data, with requests consistent with systematically collecting location listings and related details.

Did the Tencent fleet learn from the OpenAI swarm?

The discovery of the Tencent fleet can largely be attributed to technique transfer; this group was following the path pioneered by the OpenAI swarm. The Tencent fleet used "urlquery.net" as a browser execution proxy, "httpbun.com" as a payload delivery host, and "webhook.site" as an exfil channel. There was also progression within the Tencent swarm’s capability. The early fleet operations initially used “httpbin.org/base64/”, and later the same infrastructure switched to “httpbun.com/base64/”, mirroring the OpenAI swarm's own progression to the same host.

A separate actor, operating independently, converging on the same non-obvious infrastructure in the same sequence is harder to explain without the OpenAI swarm's publicly indexed urlquery footprint as a common source. In fact, it seems likely that swarms will web search prior documentation of tradecraft relating to their tasking and could offer a potential explanation to how the Tencent fleet managed to find a write up about their own activity, and in turn, communicate with the authors.

Swarm dynamics in cyber attacks

You don’t look far to see where the insights from swarm behavior may transfer to cyber attack and defense implications. Recent intrusions from agentic cyber attack harnesses like ARTEX exhibit similar characteristics. However, these multi-agent planning systems are distinct in that they have architecturally solved the constraints the OpenAI swarm was working around. The swarm needed a browser (“urlquery.net”) to run JavaScript. ARTEX alleviates this constraint by running a headless Go backend that calls tools directly.

What may hold consistent are methods around the reconnaissance logic, the payload iteration, the structured work partitioning, or the self-labeling patterns. Those are properties of the planning layer, not the infrastructure (e.g., work unit identifiers encoding timestamps, geographic scope, and sequence counters). These coordination patterns may survive the move to purpose-built infrastructure, but they likely be only internally visible queue labels.

Further research will be required going forward to grapple with the emergence of agentic attack frameworks and pinning down the meaningful surface area they have for detection and mitigation. It seems possible these may be durable if they are tied to inherent preferences of the underlying models. In our limited analysis, several avenues seem promising:

Persistent-state exfiltration: writing results that outlast the agentIn the documented ARTEX intrusions, victim machines used curl to POST command output to rotating "webhook.site" URLs. The Tencent fleet did the same by pre-creating "webhook.site" tokens from Tencent IPs before payload deployment, then routing exfiltrated data through them. The OAI swarm used image beacons for the same structural purpose. This convergence reflects a constraint of agents; they can't hold a persistent connection back to an operator. A human running a reverse shell is present in real time to receive output. An agent executes a task, may spawn parallel sub-agents, and completes, or is terminated, before the operator checks in. Results must go somewhere that outlasts the agent's execution window, and parallel sub-agents need isolated write channels so they don't collide. That is what the web hooks, beacons, and message boards are solving. Egress bypass seems like a secondary benefit. These services will often happen to be allow-listed, which makes them harder to block.

Burst-and-pause reasoning loopsGoogle Threat Intelligence Group (GTIG) describes ARTEX executing 27 events in 23 seconds, moving from compromised credential to deep system access in as little as 15 minutes and iterating on web shell candidates in real time, rapidly adjusting on error, trimming oversized payloads, and relaunching within seconds. GTIG measured individual burst sequences at 2 – 25 seconds, with sub-second actions inside them. The burst-and-pause timing signature can prove irregular in contexts that reveal rapid reasoning and adaptation.

Self-labeling conventionsAgents mark their own work for later retrieval or correlation. The OAI swarm embedded full operational metadata into URL parameters. ARTEX has used "artex-probe-*" file names. These patterns are high-fidelity signals precisely because they're functional for the agent. They may be useful for fingerprinting agent swarms, because they can't easily alter or remove the labeling scheme without breaking the agent's own state tracking.

The agent-centric future

The 2026 swarm campaigns are not just a curiosity that preceded a threat; they are a snapshot of the same core capability, in the time before the infrastructure constraints were lifted. ARTEX and its cohort of agentic harnesses are now showing us what swarm dynamics look like when resources are unconstrained and aligned on a cyber attack objective.

While these systems are powerful, they are not universally appealing. Using a coordinated multi-agent system is still a trade-off decision for attackers, and one in a growing array of options. Single agent systems can reach the same performance of swarms, and do so more efficiently, following the logic around extending chain-of-thought or scaling test-time compute. Swarm scaling primarily favors situations where speed is the most important factor.

Swarm engagements will have higher token cost, more noise, and require a broader operational footprint. Token costs, however, are only a concern when using an API. The growing list of local, open-weight models with improving cyber capabilities will continue to be an appealing alternative for attackers. As defenders, we need to recognize the distinction in behavior and capabilities and continue to search for identifying characteristics across these attack options and proactively harden our threat models in response.

[62] Talos Intelligence - Securing Grandma's groceries with Pierre Cadieux
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Wed, 25 Nov 2026 11:00:00 GMT
Link: https://blog.talosintelligence.com/securing-grandmas-groceries-with-pierre-cadieux/

[TAM METİN - 622 kelime]
Securing Grandma's groceries with Pierre Cadieux

This month, Amy is joined by Pierre Cadieux, Research Lead for Intelligence Efficacy at Talos. Pierre takes us back to his early days on help desks in the 90s, where learning to correlate logs between Windows and Unix systems sparked the curiosity that eventually led him to security. It’s a great look at how someone who has seen the industry evolve over decades stays grounded and keeps their edge.We also get into what’s kept Pierre at Cisco for nearly ten years. He talks about why he prioritizes the human impact of our work, the importance of knowing when to step away from the keyboard, and how his background in anthropology actually informs the way he approaches threat intelligence today.

Amy Ciminnisi: I've known you since you were in Talos IR, which was not too long ago. But tell me a little bit about your professional journey, because I know that definitely was not the start of it.

Pierre Cadieux: I worked at an ISP as part of the help desk back in the mid-90s. I was one of the people who people would call up and say, "Hey, I've got this computer I just bought, how do I get it on the internet?" I did a lot of learning about what TCP/IP was, what networking was, how to connect things via various different types of connections — whether it be an actual hard-coded network connection or a dial-up connection — and was able to get some expertise around guiding people through that process.

Then I moved to a technology company in San Diego and worked on their help desk. One day, someone called me up and said, "You know how to read Linux logs, right? Or Unix logs, UCS logs, Linux logs." I said, "Yeah." They said, "You know how to use Windows — you're a Windows Server guy, too." I said, "Yeah." They said, "I need you to sit in this chair, look at these two screens — one is a Windows box, one is a Unix box. I need you to be a correlation engine."

So, I looked at the timestamps for both of them, and helped to figure out what was happening because we were having an emergency, an incident, and they had nobody that could look at both. People at that point in time in this organization were very much, "I am doing just Unix things and only know Unix things. I am Windows things only. I only know one of those things." They didn't cross the streams. I could talk both. So I sat in the chair, I stared at both screens and I helped them to track the timeline of what was happening, which ended up being an intrusion exploiting a vulnerable SSH server that was compromised. That changed my course into security.

I spent a number of years in internal security for a number of organizations and got into leadership positions, even CISO positions, leading security teams globally for financial services, for instance, and others. Then I moved into consulting and did that for a number of years as well.

PC: I'm hitting my ten-year anniversary at Cisco in January. What keeps me at Cisco and Talos is the flexibility of my work. I'm able to, within most bounds, choose what I work on and how I get things done. I have a lot of flexibility in my workday, day-to-day. I work West Coast hours compared to most of my team members who are East Coast. So I sometimes work early shifts with them and am done by the early afternoon. Or sometimes I have to work with folks who are over in Asia Pacific, and I'm working later.

[63] Unit 42 - The AI Threat Horizon: Why Speed, Edge AI and Baselines Are Our Best Defense
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Wed, 25 Nov 2026 15:00:09 +0000
Link: https://unit42.paloaltonetworks.com/ai-threat-horizon/

[TAM METİN - 1059 kelime]
Threat Research Center

The AI Threat Horizon: Why Speed, Edge AI and Baselines Are Our Best Defense

Published:November 25, 2026

Categories:GeneralInsights

Tags:Agentic AIAIEndpointReal-time detection

The New Reality of AI Attackers

AI-driven attacks are on the rise, transitioning from theoretical risks to real-world threats. Recent investigations reveal that criminals are using AI to accelerate attacks at scale. We have observed attackers leveraging AI agents to execute context-aware commands with real-time decision-making. In one campaign that we observed, a highly persistent threat actor utilizing AI generated 117 unique scripts across multiple languages and executed over 8,000 unique commands in just 12 days whereas normal attackers usually do dozens of commands with a few scripts for similar time periods.

The behavioral difference between human operators and AI agents is staggering. In one attack we observed, humans acted followed by an agentic AI attacker. In the first (human attacker) phase, time between commands averaged roughly 24 seconds. The hackers made typographical errors, repeated manual checks and demonstrated environment confusion. On the other hand, in the agentic phase of the attack, we observed flawless, zero-syntax-error commands with a median gap of just 1.4 seconds.

When an AI agent is instructed to execute aspects of an attack, it rapidly scans networks, attempts to escalate privileges and utilizes legitimate native tools to blend in. Because of AI’s machine-speed execution, our Mean Time To Respond (MTTR) metrics must improve considerably. Attack isolation and near real-time response are now critical mandates. If we wait hours to react, an AI agent can have already enumerated our cloud infrastructure and extracted or encrypted our data.

To effectively defend against AI-powered attacks, defenders must employ AI-powered tools. This should be done right where the attacks happen. Defenders should also strive to understand the behaviors that separate AI-based attacks from normal operations, whether fully autonomous or partially controlled by a human.

The Power of Edge AI and Layered Detection

To counter adversaries operating at automated speeds, defense must occur where the attack takes place. Leveraging AI on the edge, specifically on endpoints and Next-Generation Firewalls (NGFW), is a major strategic necessity. Edge-based AI can process telemetry instantly, making split-second decisions to terminate malicious web shell sessions or block active privilege escalation attempts locally.

Edge AI is also exceptionally fast. In our own product deployment, edge AI operated in milliseconds rather than taking minutes like agentic LLMs. It can function independently of cloud access or continuous network connectivity. This capabilities shift is crucial for several key reasons:

Even if an attack vector is used to temporarily disable network traffic to operate silently, local edge AI maintains detection coverage

It enables defenders to catch and isolate threats at the earliest possible stage

It safeguards sensitive corporate data locally without requiring telemetry or proprietary data to be transmitted to the cloud for analysis

The Defender’s Ultimate Advantage: Knowledge and Context

Despite their speed, agentic AI attackers have a foundational weakness that affects any attacker: They enter your environment without information. To operate, they must perform extensive reconnaissance, port sweeping and environment enumeration. This is where defenders hold the ultimate upper hand through comprehensive AI analytics.

Winning against machine-speed adversaries requires turning the advantage into real-time detection through automated data fusion and detection. By collecting telemetry across diverse network, endpoint, identity and cloud sources, normalization can map these distinct data streams into a single, cohesive schema. Advanced stitching can then merge related events across these disparate sensors into unified, context-rich stories rather than disconnected events.

Observing these integrated stories allows AI analytics engines to establish baselines of normal organizational behavior across the entire ecosystem. While establishing baselines has been longstanding best practice, AI analytics engines have made it easier for defenders to create these and keep them up to date.

Because threat actors lack this context, their automated attempts to rapidly map the terrain inevitably trigger detectors against established baselines, immediately exposing their presence across our detection layers.

Detection alone was never enough to stop machine-speed threats, but the issue is even worse in the era of AI attacks. Detecting an intrusion minutes after it begins still leaves defenders vulnerable if no immediate action is taken, and in those minutes an agentic AI attacker can cause significant damage to your organization. To truly close the loop against these attacks, organizations must combine real-time detection with automated response capabilities, enabling instant attack isolation and automated remediation before critical systems or data can be compromised.

The Basics Still Matter (But Require A New Approach)

While the sheer volume and speed of AI attacks can seem daunting, there is a positive takeaway: AI-empowered threat actors are not invisible, and their campaigns are entirely detectable. Every attack that we observed triggered alerts, and deep forensic investigations enabled us to reconstruct the attack lifecycles.

The cybersecurity basics still matter. The tools we have relied on as defenders in the past, including XDR solutions, network monitoring and active prevention rules, are still essential and relevant today. We do not need to throw away our playbooks; we simply need to use them differently. By utilizing our existing tools to automate isolation, leaning into AI on the edge for real-time response and trusting our baseline knowledge of our own environments, we are well-equipped to win against the next generation of threats. Crucially, unifying these tools into a cohesive SOC platform connects the dots across the enterprise — where vertical integration and correct platform usage ultimately drive vastly superior security outcomes.

If there’s one thing to take away from this, it is that Mean Time to Detect and Mean Time to Respond (MTTD and MTTR) are still king. But if we want to win these battles, detection and response times must drop to minutes, not hours.

To safeguard your organization against the unprecedented speed and adaptability of AI-driven threats, now is the time to evaluate your defense strategy. Ensure your enterprise is equipped with advanced detection and response capabilities, deploy robust security technologies across your endpoints, network, cloud and identity environments, and continuously refine their configurations to eliminate coverage gaps. Take action today to empower your security operation with the real-time visibility, automated response, and contextual intelligence needed to stay ahead of next-generation adversaries.

[64] Microsoft Security - What defenders need to know about ARTEX, the agentic AI framework threat actors abuse
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Wed, 25 Nov 2026 18:09:06 +0000
Link: https://www.microsoft.com/en-us/security/blog/2026/11/25/what-defenders-need-to-know-artex-agentic-ai-framework-threat-actors-abuse/

[TAM METİN - 6893 kelime]
ARTEX: Publicly available AI-powered offensive security framework

AI agent architecture

Identifying ARTEX network activity

Identifying ARTEX system activity

Mitigation and protection guidance

Microsoft Defender detections

MITRE ATT&CK techniques

Since its release in July 2026, the AI-powered penetration testing framework ARTEX has been abused by many threat actors to accelerate their existing workflows related to network reconnaissance, credential attacks, and web application exploitation. As of November 2026, Microsoft Threat Intelligence has observed both state-sponsored and financially motivated threat actors such as Storm-0940, Storm-3358, Onyx Sleet, Pearl Sleet, Crystal Typhoon, and Salt Typhoon integrating ARTEX into their tooling.

In the past few weeks, we’ve reported several distinct campaigns that abused ARTEX to search public repositories for exposed secrets, enable supply chain compromise, and scan internet-facing systems for exploitation. These campaigns illustrate how threat actors use ARTEX to automate various stages of the attack chain. Threat actors define scope and targets for ARTEX, which then uses AI agents to set verifiable goals, define tactical steps for each stage of the operation, use tools to attack targets, create graphs to track discovered systems and results of actions, and report back to the threat actor. Depending on the threat actor’s configuration and the target’s defenses, ARTEX can advance from reconnaissance to persistent, privileged access to a target organization in a matter of minutes.

ARTEX does not introduce new attack techniques; it uses common, pre-existing penetration testing tools and techniques. ARTEX does sometimes transmit distinctive characteristics associated with its integrated penetration testing tools. However, threat actors using ARTEX can and do vary network indicators like source IP address or user agent string, especially to bypass static or simple signature-based detection and blocking. The most reliable and durable signal of ARTEX activity is behavioral: ARTEX sends a wide variety of requests in a brief time and retries variations on the same technique or moves on to next-stage actions at machine speed.

In this blog, we’re sharing a detailed breakdown of ARTEX, including its architecture and capabilities, as well as guidance for identifying related network and system activity. Microsoft disrupts and contains threats posed by ARTEX-enabled attacks across the Microsoft Defender suite of products. Defenders can further reduce and mitigate the effectiveness of ARTEX and similar AI agentic attacks by following Microsoft’s recommendations outlined in this report to reduce internet-facing exposure, secure credentials and secrets, and limit lateral movement paths in their environment. Additionally, Microsoft recommends customers to enable automatic attack disruption in Microsoft Defender XDR to contain and isolate affected devices during an attack.

To help defenders keep pace with machine-speed attacks, Microsoft Perception coordinates red, blue, and green team agents that find exploitable weaknesses, investigate threats, and remediate risks, with security teams approving critical actions. As threat actors increasingly adopt AI, we’re publishing this information to equip defenders with insights that can help protect systems, secrets, identities, and networks from AI-assisted attacks.

ARTEX: Publicly available AI-powered offensive security framework

ARTEX (“AI 自主渗透测试系统,” or AI autonomous penetration testing system) is an AI-driven penetration testing platform. A Go-based backend coordinates multiple large language model (LLM) agents that can take an attack from reconnaissance and attack surface discovery through exploitation, verification, and reporting, with little or no human involvement. ARTEX includes an embedded Next.js web interface and uses PostgreSQL for storage.

ARTEX is primarily a web application penetration testing tool. Its built-in guidance, skills, and findings categories center on vulnerabilities in HTTP applications and APIs, including SQL injection, server-side template injection, XML external entity (XXE) injection, file upload flaws, authentication bypass, and insecure direct object references (IDOR).

ARTEX agents also perform general network reconnaissance and credential attacks. After agents gain initial access to a system or network, they can pivot through interactive shells. By default, ARTEX does not take any measures to obfuscate its malicious activity.

In July 2026, GitHub user Autumn-27 published ARTEX as open-source code with a Chinese-language interface. While the original developer removed the source code in October 2026, other developers who had forked the project continue to publish their own versions of ARTEX in many other languages. In November 2026, the China-based cybersecurity company ThreatBook (微步在线) claimed ownership of ARTEX and began offering commercial access to “ARTEX Pro”, in addition to CyberStrikeAI Enterprise Edition and other AI-powered penetration testing frameworks.

AI agent architecture

ARTEX uses multiple AI agents for different functions. The threat actor defines the target scope, and a goals model breaks the engagement into objectives that can be verified. A planner agent continuously reviews a knowledge graph containing discovered assets and findings, then issues short attack directions called “intents.” Worker agents claim those intents and carry them out with a real shell and offensive tools. They write new assets, facts, and confirmed vulnerabilities back to the graph. Each update wakes the planner agent, which decides what to do next. The cycle continues until the goals are met or the task reaches its time or step limit. A reporter agent then documents each confirmed finding with reproduction steps and a proof of concept.

The threat actor can configure ARTEX to power its agents with virtually any large language model, including frontier models, open-weight models, or models that the threat actor has abliterated (modified to remove built-in guardrails) or otherwise manually tuned. For that reason, ARTEX activity is not necessarily constrained by any single provider’s safety controls or visible in any single provider’s telemetry.

An optional pool function allows ARTEX to switch to different models when one fails for any reason (for example, quota exhausted, invalid key, rate-limited, or erroring). The function includes a configurable priority order and cooldown, so a long campaign can continue across multiple providers or keys.

ARTEX agents call tools to act on intents. While threat actors and agents can add arbitrary tools, ARTEX includes a set of pre-configured tools that define its core capabilities: network reconnaissance, web application mapping, web shell upload, and web browser-based attacks.

nmap — A network discovery and port-scanning tool that identifies systems, exposed services, and often their versions, rather than simply testing one connection

nc (netcat) — A general-purpose network utility for opening TCP or UDP connections, listening on ports, and moving raw data between systems

telnet — A basic interactive TCP client useful for manually probing text-based services

whois — A registration-data lookup tool that retrieves information about domain names or IP address allocations

dig — A DNS diagnostic tool

curl — A command-line HTTP and protocol client suited to crafting requests and inspecting responses, including headers, authentication, and APIs

wget — A web download utility

Playwright + Chromium — A browser-automation stack that drives a real Chromium browser, with all elements of the HTTP exchange exposed for manipulation

node — A JavaScript runtime

python3 — A general-purpose scripting runtime

git — A tool for retrieving and inspecting code repositories and their history

ripgrep — A fast recursive text-search tool for finding strings or patterns

jq — A command-line JSON processor that filters and transforms structured output from APIs and other tools

Threat actors also commonly add exploitation frameworks and credential attack tools to extend the capabilities of ARTEX agents:

sqlmap — A specialized web-application testing tool that detects and assesses SQL injection in application inputs

hydra — A parallelized credential-testing tool supporting many network login protocols and configurable username/password combinations

medusa — A modular, parallel network-login tester distinguished by its service-specific authentication modules

ncrack — A network authentication testing tool from the Nmap ecosystem, focused on high-speed testing of supported remote services

nuclei — A template-driven vulnerability scanner that checks targets for defined exposures, misconfigurations, and known issues across multiple protocols

nikto — A web-server scanner focused on potentially dangerous files, outdated components, and common server misconfigurations

gobuster — A wordlist-based discovery tool that enumerates web paths, DNS subdomains, or virtual hosts with a relatively focused command-line workflow

ffuf — A flexible web fuzzer that substitutes candidate values into chosen parts of a request, such as paths, parameters, headers, or hostnames

Msfconsole (Metasploit) — An exploitation framework and its interactive console, combining exploit modules, payloads, and post-exploitation workflows in one environment.

john (John the Ripper) — An offline password-auditing tool notable for broad hash-format support and adaptable cracking modes

hashcat — An offline password-recovery tool distinguished by GPU-accelerated candidate generation and hash testing

ARTEX agents can perform a wide range of actions, depending on the available tools and goals that the threat actor sets. Microsoft Threat Intelligence has observed ARTEX agents exhibiting the following capabilities as of November 2026:

Identifying ARTEX network activity

While ARTEX does not invent new attack techniques, it can combine the adaptable attack chain of a human threat actor with the speed and precision of malicious software. Therefore, the challenge of identifying network activity from AI-driven tools like ARTEX is differentiating it from both human- and software-driven activity.

Defenders can detect three types of evidence that may suggest AI-driven activity:

The most durable signal of AI-driven activity is the pattern formed by the bursts of machine speed activity. ARTEX agents, sometimes working in parallel, continually adapt and retry a given tactic until they succeed or run out of time. ARTEX agents quickly and exhaustively iterate through different methods of access, exploitation, or abuse. A human threat actor takes time to move from one stage of the attack chain to the next. In contrast, after successfully compromising a system or credentials, the framework then dispatches additional agents to move on to the next stage of the activity at machine speed. While ARTEX is a popular tool, other AI-driven offensive security frameworks also generate similar patterns of behavior.

Automated penetration testing tools, including the ones that ARTEX agents call, can produce a characteristically large volume of anomalous traffic from a single source in a short time. ARTEX does not obfuscate its malicious activity; a large number of these traffic bursts occurring at intervals is consistent with automated targeting.

A small amount of traffic might have user agent strings, network protocol options, or other default values strongly associated with ARTEX or the tools it calls. However, threat actors and ARTEX agents frequently change these values, particularly when evading static rules that block such values. The following table shows two such user agent strings specific to ARTEX:

Defensive honeypots, decoys, tarpits, and fake wins can pull autonomous AI attacks away from legitimate targets. Honeypots and decoy surfaces such as Microsoft Sentinel Deception give the agent attractive but worthless targets to pursue. Tarpits consume the agent’s time and budget. Fake wins present convincing evidence that the agent’s objective is complete, which can cause it to stop on a false success. Honeytokens can provide early warnings of AI compute hijacking. In Microsoft’s testing, these controls helped reduce the attack traffic reaching real assets and produced high-confidence, attribution-quality signal.

Identifying ARTEX system activity

After gaining initial access to a system, ARTEX agents often use well-known risky utilities (for example, certutil, curl, wget, netcat) on the system to launch web shells, install remote access trojans (RATs), or take other action to achieve persistence. Because ARTEX does not take any measures to obfuscate its malicious activity, existing methods for detecting compromise will likely capture ARTEX activity:

Web server processes (such as w3wp.exe, php-fpm, httpd or apache2, or Java application servers) starting sh, bash, cmd.exe, or PowerShell

New or modified script files (.php, .jsp, .jspx, .asp, .aspx, or .ashx) in web-accessible or upload directories; this includes files written by a database server process after its logging settings were changed to point a log file at a web directory

Several short-lived web shells with incrementing names, each running a different command, as ARTEX agents correct errors until a web shell runs cleanly

Rapid reads of /etc/shadow, .env and configuration files, private keys, and cloud credential files, and bursts of database extraction

Reverse shells and outbound requests from the server to webhook or file-sharing services used to deliver scripts and return command output

Two or three attack chains active at once, and renewed attempts through new paths after containment

Mitigation and protection guidance

Microsoft recommends the following mitigations to reduce the impact of this threat. Check the recommendations card for the deployment status of monitored mitigations.

ARTEX doesn’t introduce new attack techniques. It runs common penetration testing tools and tries one approach after another at machine speed. The mitigations that matter most close exposure before scanning finds it, slow down bursts of requests, protect credentials and application secrets, and contain a compromise automatically.

Reduce internet-facing exposure

Find and fix exposed assets first by using Microsoft Defender External Attack Surface Management discovery to inventory the internet-facing hosts, websites, and services you own, including ones you didn’t know about. Then prioritize fixes with Microsoft Defender Vulnerability Management, which shows internet-exposed resources that lead to sensitive data or privileged identities. ARTEX agents start with port scans and web path enumeration and act on whatever they find.

Block and slow down attacks on web applications and APIs. After tuning in detection mode, switch Web Application Firewall on Application Gateway to prevention mode so that SQL injection and other common web attacks are blocked, not only logged. Add rate limiting on Application Gateway or rate limiting on Azure Front Door to cap how many requests one client can send in a short time, and turn on bot protection to block or log requests from known malicious IP addresses. For APIs in Azure API Management, follow Mitigate OWASP API security top 10 threats to enforce authentication, object-level authorization, and rate limits. ARTEX agents send hundreds of requests to learn which paths a server allows and harvest API documentation to find internal routes, then test them for injection, authentication bypass, and access-control flaws.

Map and test attack paths

Map what an exposed asset or stolen credential could reach before an agent does. Use attack paths in Microsoft Security Exposure Management and attack path analysis in Defender for Cloud to find defensive choke points, where paths to critical assets converge, and explore the blast radius of each one. Classify your critical assets so the paths are complete, and query the enterprise exposure graph in advanced hunting to explore the same connections. During an incident, blast radius analysis in the incident graph shows the paths an attacker could take from a compromised entity to a critical asset. ARTEX agents record the assets and vulnerabilities they discover in a graph, so mapping your own graph first shows where they would go next.

Test the paths that matter most, within Microsoft’s rules. Microsoft recommends regular red team operations to complement vulnerability scanning. Agree on the scope and constraints with resource owners and fix all critical findings. Testing of Azure resources must follow the penetration testing rules of engagement, which encourage testing your security monitoring and detection, prohibit automated testing that generates excessive traffic, and prohibit using secrets that aren’t your own, including publicly leaked ones. Agentic tools like ARTEX scan public code repositories for secrets, so decide before testing how testers will handle credentials that belong to someone else.

Organizations can incorporate Microsoft Perception’s Recon Agent to map relationships between Azure resources, Microsoft Entra ID identities, and permissions to identify potential lateral movement paths, privilege escalation opportunities, and high-value assets accessible following an initial compromise. Recon Agent’s Identify attack paths playbook searches for potential attack paths within an Azure subscription, while the Assess identity risks playbook investigates whether a managed identity could provide a path to critical assets. The Protect against a threat playbook correlates identified exposures with relevant threat intelligence. Because Perception’s Recon Agent performs read-only analysis rather than active exploitation, organizations should complement its findings with authorized penetration testing to validate viable attack paths.

Run endpoint protection on every internet-facing server and keep it blocking. Use Defender for Endpoint on Linux and the Defender for Servers plan so that the endpoint detections and hunting queries in this report cover the servers that ARTEX agents exploit, including Linux servers. Turn on tamper protection, use EDR in block mode on Windows devices that run a non-Microsoft antivirus product, and turn on the Block Webshell creation for Servers attack surface reduction rule on Exchange servers.

Restrict outbound connections from web servers. Allow only the destinations each server needs, for example with Azure Firewall threat intelligence-based filtering, and create indicators for IPs, URLs, and domains in Defender for Endpoint to block known-bad destinations. ARTEX agents have used curl and netcat to download remote access trojans, and curl with rotating webhook.site URLs to deliver scripts and return command output.

Secure accounts and sign-ins

Apply Conditional Access to every user and workload identity. Create policies that require multifactor authentication (MFA) for all users, enforce phishing-resistant MFA for administrators, and block legacy authentication. Consider wider adoption of phishing-resistant passwordless authentication deployment to all users. Configure risk policies in Microsoft Entra ID Protection to require MFA or block access for medium-risk and high-risk sign-ins; sign-ins from known threat actor infrastructure, including infrastructure behind ARTEX activity, raise Verified threat actor IP detections at high risk.

For service principals, use Conditional Access for workload identities to block sign-ins from unexpected locations or with elevated risk; workload identity risk detections flag leaked credentials and suspicious sign-ins, including credentials found in public code on GitHub.

Ban weak passwords and slow down guessing. Turn on Microsoft Entra Password Protection with a custom banned password list, extend it to on-premises Active Directory, and review smart lockout settings. In one Storm-0940 campaign that used ARTEX, Microsoft recorded more than 151,000 failed sign-ins, peaking at 2,900 failures per minute.

Protect application secrets and privileges

Keep application secrets out of code and replace any that leak. Use application management policies to limit which kinds of secrets and certificates apps can use and to enforce rotation, and move workloads to managed identities where possible. Turn on secret scanning and push protection for Azure DevOps repositories and secrets scanning in Defender for Cloud for virtual machines and cloud deployments and invalidate and replace any secret found in code or configuration files, including .env files. In one case that Microsoft observed, an AI agent went from searching GitHub for client secrets to taking data from Azure Key Vault in five minutes.

Govern app registration, consent, and privileged roles by restricting app registration to the users who need it, configuring user consent so that users can’t grant broad permissions to unverified apps, and using app governance in Microsoft Defender for Cloud Apps to see which apps can reach your data. Use Privileged Identity Management for time-bound role activation, and monitor application credential changes and role assignments as described in the Microsoft Entra security operations guide for applications. During Storm-0940 activity, ARTEX agents added credentials to applications, and an application assigned itself the Global Administrator role.

Protect cloud resources and data

Limit what a stolen credential or token can reach. Follow best practices for Azure RBAC to limit each service principal to the resources and operations its application needs, and grant managed identities only the access they need so that a token taken from the Azure Instance Metadata Service through an XXE or SSRF flaw can’t reach other resources. ARTEX agents map the permissions of the credentials they capture and have used an XXE flaw to query a cloud metadata service for role credentials, and in one investigation AI agents used a compromised OAuth application to run scripts on Azure virtual machines.

Harden and monitor the services that hold secrets and data. Follow Secure your Azure Key Vault; for storage accounts, prevent Shared Key authorization and remediate anonymous read access to blob data; disable basic authentication for App Service publishing; and disable local authentication so that Foundry Tools accept only Microsoft Entra ID authentication. Then turn on Defender for Key Vault, Defender for Storage, Defender for Resource Manager, Defender for App Service, Defender for open-source relational databases, and AI threat protection for the workloads you run. ARTEX agents have run commands through cloud administration interfaces such as Kudu when they had credentials for them, and AI agents have focused on credentials for AI services.

Limit lateral movement and contain attacks automatically

Detect credential theft on domain controllers. Install Microsoft Defender for Identity sensors on all domain controllers. Its alerts, listed in the Detections section, cover the Active Directory credential theft and reconnaissance that ARTEX agents run in compromised domains.

Remove reusable passwords and standing access. Use group managed service accounts, whose passwords Windows manages automatically, so that service accounts don’t rely on passwords that people choose and rarely change. Use Windows LAPS to rotate each device’s local administrator password so that one captured password doesn’t work across many devices and use just-in-time machine access in Defender for Cloud to keep management ports such as RDP and SSH closed until they’re needed. ARTEX agents reuse captured credentials to move laterally over SSH and WinRM.

Enable automatic attack disruption to contain compromised assets that an attacker is using and limit lateral movement early, as well as automated investigation and response, which investigates and remediates alerts automatically. In multiple ARTEX-driven Storm-0940 intrusions, privileged access came less than three hours after initial targeting.

Microsoft Security Copilot

Microsoft Security Copilot is embedded in Microsoft Defender and provides security teams with AI-powered capabilities to summarize incidents, analyze files and scripts, summarize identities, use guided responses, and generate device summaries, hunting queries, and incident reports.

Security Copilot is also available as a standalone experience where customers can perform specific security-related tasks, such as incident investigation, user analysis, and vulnerability impact assessment. In addition, Security Copilot offers developer scenarios that allow customers to build, test, publish, and integrate AI agents and plugins to meet unique security needs.

Threat intelligence reports

Microsoft Defender XDR customers can use the following threat analytics reports in the Defender portal (requires license for at least one Defender XDR product) to get the most up-to-date information about the activity and techniques discussed in this blog. These reports provide intelligence, protection information, and recommended actions to prevent, mitigate, or respond to associated threats found in customer environments.

Microsoft Security Copilot customers can also use the Microsoft Security Copilot integration in Microsoft Defender Threat Intelligence, either in the Security Copilot standalone portal or in the embedded experience in the Microsoft Defender portal to get more information about this threat threat.

Audit logs and investigation data

Detecting and investigating ARTEX activity depends on logs that some organizations don’t collect by default. An empty result from a hunting query isn’t proof that no access occurred if the underlying logs aren’t collected.

Microsoft Entra logs. Use Microsoft Entra diagnostic settings to send audit logs and sign-in logs to a Log Analytics workspace. Service principal sign-ins are recorded separately from interactive user sign-ins, so include them.

Microsoft Graph requests. Microsoft Graph activity logs record the requests that applications and users send to Microsoft Graph, including the user agent, which helps scope reconnaissance with stolen application credentials.

Web server logs. Collect IIS logs with Azure Monitor and turn on App Service diagnostic logs so that request bursts and user agents are available for hunting.

Web application firewall activity. Web Application Firewall logs on Application Gateway include access and firewall logs for allowed and blocked requests.

The following prompts may be used in Perception, utilizing red team, blue team, and green team agents within the Microsoft Defender XDR portal.

Focus an investigation on ARTEX-driven activity

Focus triage on ARTEX-driven activity

Find and summarize recent Defender XDR alerts and incidents in which activity from the same source moves from one attack stage to the next within minutes, such as web application exploitation followed by web shell activity, credential access, or cloud identity abuse. Prioritize activity with user agents that contain artex-enrich or norma. Return affected entities, timeline, supporting alerts and incidents, verdict, and evidence-based next steps.

Microsoft Defender XDR

Microsoft Defender XDR customers can run the following advanced hunting queries to find related activity in their networks. The first three queries require Microsoft Defender for Endpoint on the Windows and Linux servers that host internet-facing applications. The service principal query uses the EntraIdSpnSignInEvents table, which requires Microsoft Entra ID P2.

Web server processes running bursts of shell commands

Find web and application server processes that start shells or download tools many times within five minutes. ARTEX agents run commands through web shells and retry at machine speed and have used curl and netcat to download remote access trojans. Tune the threshold, set to 10 distinct commands by default, and exclude build or automation servers that run shells under Java or Node.js.

Script files written to web folders by web or database server processes

Find script files, such as .php, .jsp, and .aspx files, that web or database server processes create or change, including several files in a short time. ARTEX agents upload web shells with incrementing names and have written web shells through database log files. Content management system updates also write script files, so review the file names and folders.

Commands that read several kinds of credential and secret files

Find devices where commands reference three or more kinds of credential and secret files within 10 minutes, such as /etc/shadow, .env files, private keys, and cloud credential files. ARTEX agents read these files in rapid succession after they gain a foothold.

Service principal sign-ins for several applications from one IP address

Find IP addresses that attempt service principal sign-ins for three or more applications in an hour with both failures and successes, or that send the default ARTEX user agents. Agentic tools have used leaked application credentials across many applications and tenants. Error codes 7000215 and 7000222, an invalid or an expired client secret, show attempts with a credential for the application. When a success follows those failures from the same address, investigate right away.

These queries require Microsoft Entra ID audit logs, and IIS logs, App Service HTTP logs, or Application Gateway access logs in the workspace.

Machine-speed web request bursts and default ARTEX user agents

Find client IP addresses that request 100 or more distinct paths with 50 or more client errors in one minute, or that send the default ARTEX user agents, artex-enrich and norma. The query also flags the default user agents of tools that ARTEX runs, such as curl, Wget, Python HTTP clients, headless Chromium, and Nmap. Those are common in legitimate automation, so don’t alert on them alone.

Application credential changes and role assignments by the same identity

Find users or applications that both add or change application credentials and assign directory roles or application permissions within an hour. During Storm-0940 activity, ARTEX agents added credentials to applications, and an application assigned itself the Global Administrator role.

MITRE ATT&CK techniques

This tool has exhibited use of the following attack techniques. For standard industry documentation about these techniques, refer to the MITRE ATT&CK framework.

T1588.007 Obtain Capabilities: Artificial Intelligence | Threat actors obtain ARTEX from public repositories or forks and pair it with commercial or self-hosted LLMs.

T1588.002 Obtain Capabilities: Tool | ARTEX agents install offensive tools at runtime or write equivalent scripts.

T1595.001 Active Scanning: Scanning IP Blocks | ARTEX runs nmap for port and service scanning.

T1595.002 Active Scanning: Vulnerability Scanning | ARTEX agents probe web applications and APIs for injection, authentication, and access-control weaknesses.

T1595.003 Active Scanning: Wordlist Scanning | ARTEX agents enumerate directories, endpoints, and file names, sending hundreds of requests to learn which ones the server allows.

T1594 Search Victim-Owned Websites | ARTEX agents harvest JavaScript bundles, source maps, runtime configuration, and API documentation from target applications to discover internal API routes, and ARTEX’s enrichment component collects page titles from discovered sites.

T1190 Exploit Public-Facing Application | ARTEX agents exploit injection, file upload, authentication, and access-control flaws in internet-facing applications.

T1078 Valid Accounts | ARTEX reuses credentials that it captures, or that the operator supplies, against applications, hosts, and services.

T1078.004 Valid Accounts: Cloud Accounts | ARTEX agents authenticated to Microsoft 365 tenants with stolen application credentials that Storm-0940 supplied.

T1199 Trusted Relationship | ARTEX agents reached downstream tenants through an already-consented third-party application whose credentials Storm-0940 had stolen.

T1059 Command and Scripting Interpreter | ARTEX runs shell, Python, and PowerShell commands on compromised servers through command injection, web shells, and remote shells.

T1651 Cloud Administration Command | ARTEX agents have run commands through cloud administration interfaces, such as Kudu, when they had credentials for them.

T1505.003 Server Software Component: Web Shell | ARTEX uploads web shells, or writes them through database logging, and then sends commands to them.

T1098.001 Account Manipulation: Additional Cloud Credentials | ARTEX agents added credentials to applications to maintain access during Storm-0940 activity.

T1098.003 Account Manipulation: Additional Cloud Roles | ARTEX agents assigned privileged roles during Storm-0940 activity, including an application assigning itself the Global Administrator role.

T1110.001 Brute Force: Password Guessing | ARTEX tests weak passwords and brute forces sign-in forms.

T1110.003 Brute Force: Password Spraying | Storm-0940 used ARTEX to accelerate password spraying against Microsoft 365, and other actors have used it to spray identity providers.

T1552.001 Unsecured Credentials: Credentials In Files | ARTEX reads .env files, configuration files, and keys after gaining a foothold.

T1003.008 OS Credential Dumping: /etc/passwd and /etc/shadow | ARTEX reads /etc/shadow on compromised Linux servers.

T1552.005 Unsecured Credentials: Cloud Instance Metadata API | ARTEX agents have used an XXE flaw to query a cloud metadata service for role credentials.

T1558.003 Steal or Forge Kerberos Tickets: Kerberoasting | ARTEX agents run Kerberoasting scripts in compromised domains.

T1003.006 OS Credential Dumping: DCSync | ARTEX agents run DCSync scripts to obtain domain credentials.

T1087.004 Account Discovery: Cloud Account | ARTEX agents performed Microsoft Graph and directory reconnaissance during Storm-0940 activity.

T1087.002 Account Discovery: Domain Account | ARTEX agents enumerate Active Directory objects, including privileged groups.

T1580 Cloud Infrastructure Discovery | ARTEX agents map the permissions of stolen cloud credentials and enumerate cloud resources.

T1021 Remote Services | ARTEX agents capture credentials for other reachable systems and move laterally through remote services such as Secure Shell (SSH) and WinRM.

T1213.006 Data from Information Repositories: Databases | ARTEX extracts database contents through SQL injection, such as with sqlmap, or with database clients.

T1530 Data from Cloud Storage | ARTEX agents perform mass reads from cloud storage with stolen credentials.

T1090 Proxy | ARTEX can send target traffic through a configured outbound proxy. Threat actors have rotated through virtual private network (VPN), proxy, and Tor infrastructure when blocked.

T1102.002 Web Service: Bidirectional Communication | ARTEX agents have used curl with rotating webhook.site URLs to deliver scripts to a compromised server and return command output.

Kate Robertson Delivers Testimony to the Senate Committee on National Security, Defence and Veterans Affairs

Senior researcher Kate Robertson testified before Canada’s Senate Committee on National Security, Defence and Veterans Affairs on Bill C-22, An Act respecting lawful access. Civil society has pushed back against the bill for its broad surveillance reforms, and our analysis found a myriad of issues including human rights risks, transparency deficits, and dangers to Canada’s cybersecurity.
