[67] CrowdStrike - CrowdStrike Accelerates the Shift to the Agentic SOC
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Nov 25, 2026 00:00:00-0800
Link: https://www.crowdstrike.com/en-us/blog/crowdstrike-named-leader-2026-gartner-mq-isoc/

[TAM METİN - 1228 kelime]
CrowdStrike has been named a Leader in the inaugural 2026 Gartner® Magic Quadrant™ for Integrated Security Operations Center (ISOC) Systems. We have also been named a Leader in the 2026 Gartner® Magic Quadrant™ for SIEM. We believe this placement reflects one core principle: The unified CrowdStrike Falcon® platform provides the foundation where expert SOC agents gain a complete view of the enterprise, reason over key decisions, and act at machine speed.

One Platform for the Agentic SOC

Security teams are drowning in detections. Analysts spend hours piecing together context across dozens of point tools, while every new data source increases SIEM costs. Now AI agents operate across the enterprise with elevated privileges, adding a new attack surface the SOC has to defend. We believe the inaugural Gartner Magic Quadrant for ISOC Systems reflects a long-expected shift: SIEM can no longer stand alone as a system of record.

We believe Gartner created this new market to evaluate security operations as one integrated system rather than a collection of separate tools integrated with SIEM.

According to Gartner,

We feel this shift points to a new operating model: the agentic SOC, where expert agents investigate and respond to every detection and analysts direct the work. Read on to discover the five essential requirements for the agentic SOC and learn why we believe only CrowdStrike delivers them natively on a unified platform.

The agentic SOC starts with SIEM, and SIEM starts with data. Agents are only as good as the context they act on. When data is fragmented across silos, agents reason on incomplete information and inherit the same blind spots that have long plagued human analysts. CrowdStrike Falcon® Next-Gen SIEM brings first-party and third-party signals together so every agent works from the same complete picture.

Native Falcon platform telemetry across endpoint, cloud, identity, exposure management, and AI agents comes in with no added ingest cost. Certified data pipelines, built on CrowdStrike Falcon® Onum and validated by CrowdStrike Falcon® Complete, bring in detection-ready third-party data and filter out noise at ingestion. Customers can reduce data storage costs by up to 50%,2 and federated search reaches data wherever it lives.

The same data layer also addresses agent activity. CrowdStrike Falcon® Guardian shows what an AI agent did and interacted with on the endpoint, including prompts, tool calls, MCP servers, skills, and subagents. This data is correlated with identity and endpoint data, so a compromised agent surfaces already connected to its owner and host.

Adversary Intelligence

The agentic SOC needs adversary context to understand how disparate alerts may relate to the same attack. Unified data provides visibility across the environment, while adversary intelligence helps explain how threat actors operate and who may be behind an attack.

Intelligence from CrowdStrike Counter Adversary Operations, which tracks more than 290 named adversaries, is built into the Falcon platform and applied natively to detections in Falcon Next-Gen SIEM. Agents correlate related alerts into unified investigations, using adversary intelligence to recognize known tradecraft, understand potential threats, and help prioritize them based on business risk.

Unified Agentic Investigations

The agentic SOC must scale human expertise with round-the-clock coverage for every alert. It redefines the investigation workflow to put agents at the center, with humans in control.

Security teams that simply automate linear, serial workflows end up with agents making fast, but confidently wrong, decisions. AI SOC tools built on general-purpose frontier models lack the precision needed to accurately close false positives and find the true threats hiding in the queue.

CrowdStrike's agents encode the expertise of the elite frontline Falcon Complete team. CrowdStrike trains its agents on real casework from tens of thousands of customers for autonomous triage and investigation, so they know how to act and when to escalate for human oversight. CrowdStrike's agents gain even greater accuracy the longer they learn from and adapt to each organization's unique environment. Falcon Next-Gen SIEM creates one unified data layer where agents pass context to one another as the investigation unfolds, with a central coordinator that reaches a verdict no single agent could on its own. Every investigation shows its work, so analysts stay in control.

The agentic SOC must extend beyond turnkey agents. CrowdStrike’s expert agents handle investigations, but each team has its own data, playbooks, escalation paths, and risk tolerances that no vendor can pre-package. CrowdStrike Charlotte AI™ AgentWorks is a no-code workspace where teams build custom agents using the same unified data and shared context as every out-of-the-box agent.

The most advanced companies have agents in production delivering real outcomes. For example, one customer who participated in the AgentWorks Hackathon used Charlotte AI AgentWorks to build an agent that catches multifactor authentication (MFA) reset abuse in about 70 seconds instead of their usual 20 to 30 minutes. Another customer’s custom agent found 1,710 hosts running unapproved MCP servers in a single day.

Machine-speed Response

Close the gap between detection and response. Without automated response, every verdict waits on a human to act, and adversaries can take advantage of that delay. CrowdStrike™ Charlotte Agentic SOAR connects every verdict to a governed response across the Falcon platform and third-party tools, from containing a host to revoking a session. Humans approve the most critical actions, and every step is traced and auditable, so the platform can act in seconds rather than hours.

The Platform and the Engine

The shift to the agentic SOC marks a turning point in security operations, from fragmented and passive systems of record to a unified platform built to stop breaches. We believe being named a Leader in both the 2026 Gartner® Magic Quadrant™ for ISOC Systems and the 2026 Gartner® Magic Quadrant™ for SIEM reflects this shift. By bringing together unified data, adversary intelligence, agentic investigations, custom agents, and machine-speed response within a single unified platform, CrowdStrike empowers security teams to stay ahead of modern adversaries and operate at unprecedented speed and scale.

Visit the Falcon Next-Gen SIEM product page.

Learn more about agentic SOC transformation.

Learn how CrowdStrike is extending the agentic SOC in this blog.

[68] CrowdStrike - Securing Employees&rsquo; AI Use Requires a Unified Architecture
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Nov 25, 2026 00:00:00-0800
Link: https://www.crowdstrike.com/en-us/blog/securing-employees-ai-use-requires-unified-architecture/

[TAM METİN - 331 kelime]
AI is changing how employees work. From browser-based AI applications and coding assistants to autonomous agents that access tools, data, and systems, AI is becoming an integral part of everyday workflows. Much of this activity happens on the endpoint, where AI interactions can translate into real system actions.

Securing employees’ use of AI requires more than visibility into which applications employees use or what they send to AI models. Security teams must also understand what agents are instructed to do, how they communicate, and what they execute. No single feature can do this alone. It’s only possible with multiple security capabilities working in concert across each organization’s AI estate.

CrowdStrike® Falcon Guardian™ brings together complementary security mechanisms to deliver visibility, protection, and response across AI interactions and agent activity. Here, we detail these capabilities and how they help security teams better understand AI activity and risk.

Layered Security for AI on the Endpoint

AI activity spans multiple layers, from employee interactions with AI applications to agents communicating with models and executing actions on the endpoint. Securing this activity requires complementary visibility and security controls at each layer.

Securing a single layer is not sufficient to gain a full picture of all AI usage. A browser extension, for example, would only be able to look at traffic going to and from web-based agents and AI tools, completely missing activity agents executed on the endpoint. Similarly, agent hooks would miss AI web apps and any prompts transiting through there; it would not be able to discover agents on the endpoint. The CrowdStrike Falcon® sensor is capable of seeing AI traffic, supplies runtime visibility, and allows for agent discovery of even dormant agents; however, it does not directly hook into the agent’s inference framework.

It takes multiple layers to get the full picture and to have sufficient security and visibility to apply defense in depth.

Falcon Guardian extends security beyond individual AI interactions to the communications and underlying system activity that AI agents generate.

[69] Securelist (Kaspersky) - How to protect against preinstalled Android malware
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Wed, 25 Nov 2026 09:00:35 +0000
Link: https://securelist.com/tr/how-to-protect-against-preinstalled-android-malware/122093/

[TAM METİN - 1881 kelime]
Preinstalled malware on mobile devices is a major threat to user security and privacy. When we buy a new smartphone or tablet, we expect it to be ready to use and secure right out of the box. In some cases, however, devices ship with malware already on them. Such malware can do almost anything: display ads, collect personal data and credentials, track user activity, and more. Preinstalled malware can also turn the infected device into a tool for fraud, such as stealing credit card numbers or other sensitive information, causing financial loss and reputational damage.

We’ve previously reported cases where users bought smartphones on marketplaces with malware already preinstalled. Over the past few months, however, several large organizations have encountered malware embedded in the firmware of mobile devices. The specific risks to corporate security depend on how the devices are used and on the company’s line of business. But because the Trojan sits in the device firmware, any app can be compromised, and the potential damage is hard to overstate. This post looks at the current landscape of preinstalled threats on mobile devices, the risks they pose to corporate users, and available countermeasures, including Kaspersky solutions.

Landscape of preinstalled threats

The total number of devices with malicious code found in their firmware or system apps keeps growing. According to Kaspersky Security Network, the number of such devices detected each month has increased more than 2.5-fold over the past year.

Monthly number of Android devices with preinstalled malware detected by Kaspersky solutions, November 2025 — October 2026 (download)

Several malware families are known to spread through firmware and system apps, the most common being Keenadu and Triada. According to our data, one or another of these two families was found on 88% of infected devices in the first ten months of 2026.

The Triada backdoor (Backdoor.AndroidOS.Triada) was discovered ten years ago and has remained popular with attackers ever since. Early versions of the malware actively exploited Android vulnerabilities to gain root access and the privileges they needed. At that time, Triada was often distributed as a regular app. Over time, however, Android introduced tighter restrictions, and attackers now mostly use the preinstalled version of this backdoor. Throughout 2026, users encountered about twenty markedly distinct variants. The most common ones were Backdoor.AndroidOS.Triada.ag and Backdoor.AndroidOS.Triada.z. The latest Triada versions have two key distinguishing features: the ability to inject malicious functionality into any app on the device, system or user-installed, and a modular architecture that lets attackers tailor the malware to their current targets with additional payloads. Known modules can steal credentials for messaging and social media apps, intercept calls and text messages, replace links in browsers, siphon off cryptocurrency, and run a reverse proxy on the infected device. It’s worth stressing that by loading additional modules, attackers get virtually unlimited capabilities.

Keenadu is another preinstalled backdoor we discovered, which appeared relatively recently. The earliest known firmware version that contains this malware dates back to 2023. Even so, the number of devices infected with Keenadu is considerable: according to our data, more than 220,000 devices were infected in the first ten months of 2026.

Keenadu is similar to Triada in many ways, especially in its main architectural feature: once it’s on a device, attackers can download additional modules with any functionality and inject them into target apps. In most known Keenadu attacks, the malware was used for ad fraud, in particular to generate fake clicks. Technically, however, it can perform any action on the infected device. Notably, on first contact with the C2 servers, the backdoor versions we analyzed received a command to activate after about two and a half months. Until then, the malware showed no activity. This is probably meant to give the device owner time to install all the apps they need and settle into daily use, and to make the compromise harder to detect.

Other families of preinstalled malware exist, although they’re less common than Triada and Keenadu. They include Dwphon, Vo1d, and BADBOX. Their functionality is generally similar: mostly ad fraud and traffic redirection. Note that most cases involve phones and tablets, but preinstalled backdoors also threaten other types of devices. For example, Dwphon has turned up in the firmware of children’s smartwatches, and Vo1d samples in TV box firmware.

Corporate environment

We first saw an organization, rather than a home user, encounter preinstalled malware several years ago. That case involved the Dwphon malware. Over the past year, however, we’ve received more and more reports from different customers. In less than a year, several large organizations have come to us with the same request. After installing Kaspersky Endpoint Security for Android on brand-new, out-of-the-box devices, they saw detections of Keenadu or Triada and asked us to confirm that those detections were genuine.

Note that when we talk about corporate threats, we mean the Corporate Owned, Business Only (COBO) and Corporate Owned, Personally Enabled (COPE) usage scenarios, where the employee’s device belongs to the organization. The difference between these models is that COBO doesn’t allow personal use of the device (and usually blocks it at the software level). With COPE, the organization controls global settings but allows free personal use and, in some cases, even installation of third-party apps. By corporate environment, we mean primarily the setup with centralized procurement, where an organization buys all the devices it needs (hundreds, thousands, or even tens of thousands) for uniform use. This post doesn’t cover infections under the Bring Your Own Device (BYOD) model, where employees use infected personal devices they bought themselves to access company resources or for work communications.

Preinstalled malware appears on a new device as a result of a supply chain attack. Devices can be manufactured and flashed in different places. Likewise, different parties in the production process can create the firmware and prepare it for the device. Malicious code typically gets onto a device in one of three ways:

The manufacturer installs the malware deliberately: the attackers control the entire production process and either sell infected devices cheaply under an obscure brand name or, conversely, pose as a well-known manufacturer, damaging that company’s reputation.

A legitimate brand that makes the final device receives malicious firmware from a contractor acting in bad faith.

A legitimate manufacturer builds the firmware in-house, but the build relies on an already infected base or code component. Here, no one directly involved in manufacturing knows the production chain has been compromised.

What risks do corporate customers face when they buy such devices? Based on the technical capabilities of preinstalled malware, we can identify the following main risks:

Degraded performance of infected devices: shorter battery life, overheating, and glitches in various apps. Some devices have even been known to play random sounds on their own.

Ads on devices, both visible to the user and hidden. They consume extra traffic and also connect the device to various websites, which creates a separate group of risks.

Account takeover in messaging apps, social media, and corporate resources: theft of credentials can damage the company’s reputation and lead to financial losses or even new attacks.

Direct financial losses, such as cryptocurrency theft.

Theft of confidential data and secrets: in effect, spying on corporate devices.

Use in a proxy botnet: the organization’s infected devices can relay arbitrary traffic to any internet resources.

Use in a DDoS botnet: infected devices can be enlisted to launch DDoS attacks on any target. Reputational risks come into play here as well.

External access to the corporate infrastructure through a proxy module. Infected devices are known to have been used as residential proxies to infect other devices on the same local network.

Device downtime: attackers who disable corporate devices in active use can disrupt operations.

Note that not all of the attack scenarios involving preinstalled malware listed above have been seen in real-world incidents. However, Triada and BADBOX, for example, have already expanded their functionality: besides displaying ads, they now steal accounts. We can therefore expect preinstalled malware to keep gaining capabilities that pose even higher risks.

Using Kaspersky solutions to mitigate risks

Mobile Device Management (MDM) is a separate category of solutions built to manage mobile devices, and it helps mitigate infection risks in corporate environments. With MDM, IT teams can centrally configure smartphones, tablets and laptops, enforce security policies, and control app installation. This significantly reduces the risk of infection caused by user actions, because it prevents a user from installing an APK from an attacker’s message, a malicious website, or a rogue app store listing.

However, these measures alone are not effective against preinstalled Trojans. Centralized mobile device management alone is not enough to contain threats like Triada and Keenadu. It must be complemented by protection that can detect malware, including any already present on a device before the company starts using it. The best approach is to combine management and protection in a single agent with a unified console, which simplifies rollout and day-to-day operation: IT teams only need to deploy and maintain one application instead of two.

Kaspersky Next product line, starting with Kaspersky Next EDR Optimum, combines Advanced MDM functionality with antivirus protection. The antivirus engine scans files and installed apps and provides real-time protection. Kaspersky Next can detect malware on Android devices, even in preinstalled apps, and alert administrators to any threats it finds. This allows spotting an infection on a device that nominally complies with corporate policies.

Triada detection report in the unified KSC console (screenshot in Russian)

Note that it’s technically impossible for either antivirus tools or the OS itself to remove preinstalled malware from the system partition. However, Kaspersky Next lowers the risk of buying a whole batch of devices and then finding a preinstalled Trojan that is hard or impossible to remove by reflashing. If Kaspersky Next detects malware like Triada or Keenadu, it alerts the IT administrator, who can then contain the threat by blocking the device’s internet access, clearing app data, rotating passwords, or locking the device entirely. Administrators can monitor and manage the infected device from the unified console of the Kaspersky Security Center.

Threat monitoring in the unified KSC console

Companies planning a mobile infrastructure should account for the risk of encountering preinstalled malware. Before ordering a large batch of devices, we recommend inspecting and pilot-testing sample units. We should stress that this measure is no substitute for installing security software on every device used in the organization. Even if the tests come out fine, an unscrupulous supplier may have deliberately sent clean units for inspection. Protection must also be continuous because, for example, the device update system could come under attack.

Manufacturers need to check devices and firmware code for malware just as customers do. Whether the firmware is written in-house or by a contractor, the result can contain malicious code, as we noted earlier. To detect preinstalled threats, you need a file scanning solution that can respond quickly when it finds malware. Kaspersky Scan Engine is one of the Kaspersky products designed for this task. The engine uses the latest detection technologies to identify a wide range of cyberthreats, including preinstalled ones. It integrates easily into a variety of workflows. For example, it can connect to a software build pipeline or any other system that needs to check files for malicious code.

Triada detection in Kaspersky Scan Engine

(XXXXXXX, AÇIK - https://securelist.com/tr/how-to-protect-against-preinstalled-android-malware/122093/, securelist.com, 25.11.2026)

[73] The Cyber Express - New Zealanders Lost $5.9 Million to Cyber Attacks in Just Three Months
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Thu, 26 Nov 2026 06:37:34 +0000
Link: https://thecyberexpress.com/new-zealand-cyber-incidents-hit-two-year-high/

[TAM METİN - 601 kelime]
New Zealanders Lost $5.9 Million to Cyber Attacks in Just Three Months

New Zealand cyber incidents have reached their highest level in two years, and the money lost to them has more than doubled in just three months.

New Zealand’s National Cyber Security Centre (NCSC) received 1,694 incident reports between July and September 2026, according to its latest Cyber Security Insights report.

Reported financial losses climbed to $5.9 million, up 118% from $2.7 million in the previous quarter. The NCSC says it was its busiest quarter since Q3 2024.

How Much Did New Zealand Cyber Incidents Rise?

The jump was steep across every measure:

1,694 incident reports, up 50% from 1,129 in Q2 2026

179 incidents needed specialist technical support because of their potential national significance, up 94% from 92

1,515 reports did not need specialist support, up 46%

339 incidents involved a direct financial loss

The NCSC says reported incidents rose in every category.

Phishing Still Leads the List

Phishing and credential harvesting was again the most common type of incident, with 511 reports, a 56% increase on the previous quarter. Scams and fraud came second.

One category stood out for a different reason. Command and control server hosting returned for the first time since Q3 2025, with 94 incidents. These happen when attackers gain a foothold inside a network and connect it to their own infrastructure so they can send commands, install more malware or steal data.

Why Are New Zealand Cyber Incidents Increasing?

The NCSC points to frontier AI as a key factor.

The emergence of frontier AI systems is accelerating the cyber threat landscape,” said NCSC Deputy Director-General Catriona Robinson. Vulnerabilities can now be identified and exploited at unprecedented speed, enabling malicious actors to target New Zealanders and organisations at greater scale and volume than ever before.

The report notes that more vulnerabilities are being identified than ever before, and that they can be found and exploited faster and at greater scale.

Who Was Behind the Serious Attacks?

For the 179 incidents that needed specialist support, the NCSC tried to link the activity to known actors:

31% were likely linked to state-sponsored actors

26% were likely linked to cybercriminals

43% could not be linked to a known actor

How Serious Were the Incidents?

Most reports were minor, with 1,267 classed as C6. Higher up the scale, there were 73 routine C5 incidents, 53 moderate C4 incidents, 12 significant C3 incidents and one highly significant C2 incident. There were no C1 national cyber emergencies.

The education sector had one of the biggest jumps of the quarter. Reported incidents from education and training rose from 13 to 43, an increase of 230%.

What the NCSC Wants People to Do

Robinson said the rise should prompt action. Individuals, businesses, and organisations should take this as a signal to review their cyber security settings, ensure basic protections are in place, and remain vigilant against increasingly sophisticated online threats,” she said.

The report also includes two feature articles. The first looks at how quickly vulnerabilities are now being exploited, what organisations should do and how the NCSC issues critical vulnerability alerts.

The second examines how operational technology systems are increasingly being targeted by cybercriminals and how organisations can strengthen their defences.

[74] The Cyber Express - Microsoft Confirms Attackers Accessed Customer Data Through Entra Connect Health
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Wed, 25 Nov 2026 11:55:56 +0000
Link: https://thecyberexpress.com/entra-connect-health-data-breach/

[TAM METİN - 541 kelime]
Microsoft Confirms Attackers Accessed Customer Data Through Entra Connect Health

Microsoft has confirmed that attackers exploited a flaw in its Entra Connect Health service to read data belonging to customer organizations worldwide. The Entra Connect Health data breach could touch banking, healthcare, government, manufacturing, and education, since many organizations use the service to link on-premises IT systems to Microsoft’s cloud and manage access to computers and business applications on their own servers.

The cyberattack on Microsoft exposed tenant metadata and administrator email addresses. The company has warned that the stolen details may be used for social engineering, and it has not disclosed how many tenants were affected.

What the Entra Connect Health Data Breach Exposed

The attackers gained read-only access to tenant-level metadata, including:

Tenant IDs and organization names

Initial domain names and geographic details

Microsoft Entra license tiers

Directory synchronization status and Connect Health onboarding information

Email addresses of technical contacts and administrators

The API also returned an expired certificate once used to register Connect Health agents with an internal Microsoft service retired in late 2024.

“This vulnerability did not expose user passwords, password hashes, authentication tokens, valid cryptographic keys, or on-premises server and Active Directory topology details,” Microsoft said, as reported by Cybernews.

Timeline of the Cyberattack on Microsoft

The flaw entered the Entra Connect Health API on July 23, 2026, and went unnoticed for 109 days. Microsoft detected it on November 9, according to an email sent to affected administrators and later shared on Reddit. The company said the bug could let an unauthorized party read tenant and service-monitoring metadata and potentially alter Connect Health monitoring settings, though it found no sign that any configuration was changed.

A server-side fix was deployed on November 19, and customers need no software update or other remediation. Microsoft concluded its investigation on November 20, confirmed unauthorized read-only access for the tenants it notified, and began alerting them through Azure Service Health.

Phishing Risk After the Entra Connect Health Data Breach

The leaked details show how organizations run their IT infrastructure, giving attackers material for convincing phishing campaigns. Emails posing as Microsoft support or internal IT teams, and citing real Entra details, may look legitimate to the administrators they target.

While our investigation did not identify unauthorized configuration changes, we recommend the following precautionary measures to help your organization assess potential risks associated with the exposed tenant metadata and technical contact email addresses,” the company said.

Microsoft advises alerting security teams to targeted phishing, using phishing-resistant multifactor authentication for privileged accounts, reviewing Connect Health configurations, and checking Entra audit and sign-in logs for suspicious activity linked to the cyberattack on Microsoft.

“We recommend confirming that your tenant’s technical contacts and notification recipients are current and appropriate,” Microsoft added.

Administrators can run a PowerShell script, published at aka.ms/ConnectHealth/scripts, to see which types of tenant metadata may have been exposed.

[75] The Cyber Express - 23 VMware Vulnerabilities Disclosed as Broadcom Flags Critical vCenter and ESX Flaws
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Wed, 25 Nov 2026 06:12:42 +0000
Link: https://thecyberexpress.com/cve-2026-123903-esx-cve-2026-123910/

[TAM METİN - 733 kelime]
23 VMware Vulnerabilities Disclosed as Broadcom Flags Critical vCenter and ESX Flaws

Broadcom published VMSA-2026-0011 on November 24, 2026, a Critical-rated advisory covering 23 vulnerabilities across vCenter, ESX, Workstation, Fusion, VMware Tools, and Avi Load Balancer. The most serious are CVE-2026-123903, an authentication bypass in vCenter, and CVE-2026-123910, a network-reachable code execution flaw in ESX.

Affected stacks include VMware Cloud Foundation (VCF), vSphere Foundation (VVF), Telco Cloud Platform, and Telco Cloud Infrastructure.

The release arrives shortly after VMSA-2026-0009 and during a major US holiday. Broadcom attributed the timing to coordinated disclosure and release pipeline schedules, admitting it was not ideal. The company said the advisory itself remains the authoritative source for affected and fixed versions.

CVE-2026-123903 Tops Seven vCenter Flaws

CVE-2026-123903 lets an attacker with network access to vCenter bypass authentication in the VMware Directory Service. Broadcom said it is a new issue, unrelated to CVE-2026-123890 from VMSA-2026-0009. Three more directory service bugs (CVE-2026-123905, CVE-2026-123906 and CVE-2026-123907) allow denial of service. These exist whether or not Enhanced Linked Mode is used.

The rest of the vCenter list includes an arbitrary file write while processing archives (CVE-2026-123904), which requires access to the backup repository and can lead to root-level code execution. A vpxd service script privilege escalation (CVE-2026-123908) and a vSphere Distributed Switch policy enforcement failure (CVE-2026-123909) complete it. Broadcom advises restricting write access to backup storage and keeping Promiscuous Mode, MAC Address Changes, and Forged Transmits set to their Reject defaults.

Fixed vCenter builds are 9.1.2, the 9.0.2 express patch, and 8.0 U3m. Customers who already installed 8.0 U3m for VMSA-2026-0009, including on VCF 5.2.x, need not patch vCenter again. The updates are not eligible for vCenter Quick Patch. Running workloads continue during the update, though some encryption operations against a Standard Key Provider pause while vCenter is offline.

CVE-2026-123910 and the ESX Host Risks

CVE-2026-123910, a heap out-of-bounds write, and CVE-2026-123911, a use-after-free, both sit in the ESX I/O filter service. Neither requires access to a virtual machine; network access to the host is enough to execute code on it.

Other ESX flaws include a VMkernel stack-based buffer overflow (CVE-2026-123913), a host configuration store bypass (CVE-2026-123914), and a signature verification bypass (CVE-2026-123915). The list also covers an XHCI USB controller out-of-bounds write (CVE-2026-123912) and a guest RPC denial of service (CVE-2026-123916). CVE-2026-123916 was already fixed in VCF 9.1.1, VCF 5.2.4, ESX 8.0 U3j, and Workstation and Fusion 26H1.

Fixed releases are ESX 9.1.2, the 9.0.2 express patch, and 8.0 U3m (ESXi80U3m). vSAN Witness Appliances also need the update, as does the host running them. ESX Live Patch does not apply, so hosts must reboot, with vMotion moving workloads beforehand. VMX fixes take effect only after a VM powers on or migrates; a guest OS restart is not enough.

Desktop Hypervisors, VMware Tools and Avi

CVE-2026-123912 scores 9.3 on Workstation and Fusion, where a guest administrator may escape to the host machine, and 8.5 on ESX, where the VMX sandbox contains it. Workstation and Fusion 26H1u1 fix it, and CVE-2026-123916.

VMware Tools carries CVE-2026-123918, a denial-of-service vulnerability in the VGAuth service. Fixes are 13.1.10 and 12.5.6, plus 12.4.11 for 32-bit Windows; Linux vendors ship their own open-vm-tools builds. Tools must be updated separately inside each VM.

Avi Load Balancer has eight issues scored 3.4 to 6.7, from insecure deserialization to directory traversal. Version 32.1.4 fixes all of them, while 31.2.3, 30.2.7, 32.1.3-2p1 and 32.1.2-2p3 resolve only some.

Patching Beyond CVE-2026-123903 and vCenter

CVSS 3.1 scores range from 3.4 to 9.8. Four issues are Critical (CVE-2026-123903, CVE-2026-123910, CVE-2026-123911 and CVE-2026-123912), qualifying as ITIL emergency changes. Broadcom lists no workarounds; removing XHCI controllers or limiting I/O filter access only partly helps.

SDDC Manager fixes from VMSA-2026-0009 still apply separately, and VCF 5.x uses KB 88287. VMSA-2026-0010 is not covered. vSphere 7, out of support since October 2, 2025, is affected, while VCF 9.1.2 lifts the earlier “Back-in-Time” upgrade block.

[76] Industrial Cyber - NRWA’s Water Watch Center supports nearly 100 water utilities with free cybersecurity monitoring across 26 US states
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Wed, 25 Nov 2026 10:09:46 +0000
Link: https://industrialcyber.co/utilities-energy-power-water-waste/nrwas-water-watch-center-supports-nearly-100-water-utilities-with-free-cybersecurity-monitoring-across-26-us-states/

[TAM METİN - 551 kelime]
NRWA’s Water Watch Center supports nearly 100 water utilities with free cybersecurity monitoring across 26 US states

The National Rural Water Association (NRWA) reported growing demand for cybersecurity assistance among small and rural water and wastewater utilities in the U.S., with nearly 100 utilities contacting its Water Watch Center since the initiative was announced in August 2026. Three months after its launch, utilities in 26 states, from Maine to California, were seeking support, while the first participating systems had already entered 24/7 cybersecurity monitoring. The joint initiative between NRWA and DEF CON Franklin provides free, around-the-clock cybersecurity monitoring, threat intelligence, basic cyber hygiene guidance and incident response support to under-resourced water and wastewater systems that often lack dedicated cybersecurity personnel or budgets to hire them.

The NRWA said the increased demand comes amid mounting cyber threats against water infrastructure, including suspected Iran-linked attacks that breached water systems in a dozen U.S. states this year. Of approximately 50,000 community drinking water systems in the country, 91% serve small and rural communities, many of which support high-value targets such as military installations, data centers and hospitals.

“These water utilities are under a constant barrage of attacks from hackers across the planet,” Jake Braun, executive director of the University of Chicago’s Cyber Policy Initiative and co-founder of the Water Watch Center, said in a recent media statement. “After two years of research rolling up our sleeves in the trenches with water operators, our team has developed a model for under-resourced utilities that for the first time provides basic cyber best practices and visibility into their network so they have a fighting chance to defend themselves against America’s enemies.”

“Our State Rural Water Associations have decades of experience working directly with our nation’s small and rural systems and understand the challenges systems face when it comes to addressing cybersecurity threats,” said Matthew Holmes, NRWA chief executive officer. “The Water Watch Center gives these systems access to professional, 24/7 monitoring at no cost to secure their vital infrastructure from cyber vulnerabilities. To help defend against cyber threats, I encourage eligible utilities to apply for the program.”

Under the program, an initial set of five managed detection and response providers has joined forces with DEF CON Franklin and NRWA to deliver cyber services directly to small water utilities serving fewer than 10,000 people, which make up 91% of the approximately 50,000 community water systems in the country. These providers, including Defendify, Legato Security, L1 Secure, Rapid7, and Sentinel Technologies, will exchange threat information and patches through a dedicated collaboration mechanism and share it with NRWA, the national hub.

The Water Watch Center connects participating utilities with regional managed security providers that monitor networks, identify potential intrusions, provide cybersecurity recommendations, and assist with incident response at no cost. Craig Newmark Philanthropies, the Hewlett Foundation, and the Defense Advanced Research Projects Agency (DARPA) support the program.

Water and wastewater utilities are among the most targeted and least defended pieces of American infrastructure. This year, suspected Iran-linked actors breached water systems in a dozen states. Of the roughly 50,000 community drinking water systems in the U.S., 91% serve small and rural communities. Similarly, a spate of suspicious and high-profile ‘glitches’ at government and corporate institutions like the FAA, NSA, Microsoft, and United Airlines has water and wastewater operators on edge.

[77] Industrial Cyber - NCSC warns agentic AI frameworks boost cyberattacks by exploiting exposed credentials, API flaws, security gaps
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Wed, 25 Nov 2026 10:03:59 +0000
Link: https://industrialcyber.co/ai/ncsc-warns-agentic-ai-frameworks-boost-cyberattacks-by-exploiting-exposed-credentials-api-flaws-security-gaps/

[TAM METİN - 1665 kelime]
NCSC warns agentic AI frameworks boost cyberattacks by exploiting exposed credentials, API flaws, security gaps

The U.K. National Cyber Security Centre (NCSC) reported a marked increase in artificial intelligence (AI)-assisted cyberattacks since September this year, with organizations across multiple sectors experiencing incidents involving agentic offensive frameworks originally developed for authorized red teaming and penetration testing. Hackers are using these frameworks, combined with capable open-source AI models and low-cost or illegally resold application programming interfaces (APIs), to automate the discovery and exploitation of weaknesses in internet-facing services and assets. These attacks have resulted in service disruptions and system compromises, providing attackers with initial access for further automated or human-driven activity.

The NCSC noted that agentic attacks are exploiting low-hanging fruit such as credentials mistakenly committed to code repositories; misconfigurations in infrastructure as code (IAC); default credentials or lack of authentication; and abuse of weaknesses in APIs. Also, the weaknesses being exploited are not new or previously unknown vulnerabilities, but longstanding security gaps, including exposed credentials in code repositories, infrastructure-as-code misconfigurations, default credentials, inadequate authentication and API weaknesses.

The agency warned that agentic offensive frameworks are accelerating pace, persistence and scale of cyber attacks, enabling attackers to move from discovery to exploitation within minutes, systematically test endpoints and credentials, and adapt subsequent attempts based on errors and failed requests. Once inside an organization, agents can exploit over-privileged credentials and interconnected or flat networks to expand their access.

“The incidents involved automated identification and exploitation attempts across organisations’ internet-facing services and assets in quick succession,” Kris B, incident handler for incident management; Ellen T, lead data scientist at vulnerability management team; and David Chismon, CTO for architecture, wrote in an NCSC blog. “This resulted in downtime to some services or the compromise of systems, providing attackers with a foothold for further agent-driven activity (or in some cases, human-driven activity within their network).”

They further noted that none of the weaknesses being exploited are new or novel, while the tools that the agents have access to are common and provide no new capability the typical threat actor would have access to. “What has changed is how quickly and persistently threat actors can conduct attacks – at low cost – using these frameworks combined with cheap, capable open models or illegal API resellers.”

These agentic offensive frameworks allow AI models to interact with off-the-shelf cybersecurity tools to probe digital systems for weaknesses. Typically, a framework will use an LLM to plan a sequence of attack steps, use the tools against a target, retrieve the results, and decide what to do next.

“At the time of writing (November 2026), this type of activity has become synonymous with the ARTEX framework, a multi-agent penetration testing and red-teaming tool originally developed for authorised security testing,” the executives detailed in the post. “Different tools come and go (others include Cairn, PentAGI, RedAmon, Xalgorix and Darkmoon), so rather than concentrating on a particular framework, here we’ll focus on how the combination of a human attacker plus a capable model plus a framework creates an uplift in capability.”

Compared with a human attacker working without tools, an agent plus a framework works quickly (it moves from discovery to exploitation in minutes); is exhaustive in its search (it will try every endpoint, parameter and credential it finds); instantly learns from failures (it creates memory, reads error messages, responds and adapts its next attempt); and is cheap to run, at scale (the cost of running automated attacks is low).

They added that “This does mean that today’s frameworks are noisy. They trade stealth for speed, generate a lot of traffic and will interact with almost anything they find, which makes them easier to detect. This may not always be the case if future frameworks prioritise stealth.”

Notably, the issues being exploited currently are not novel memory corruption vulnerabilities (or even known but unpatched vulnerabilities), but unmitigated weaknesses that are often the result of years of technical debt that organisations have accumulated. “Once an initial foothold has been gained, the agents explore the environment, exploiting the fact that many credentials in an organisation are over-privileged for their needs, and networks are often flat or interconnected.”

To counter the threat, the NCSC urged organizations to strengthen asset visibility, restrict unnecessary internet-facing services, improve monitoring and logging, accelerate vulnerability patching, secure APIs through account- and session-based rate limits, and enforce stronger authentication and access controls. It also recommended using bot challenges, throttling account creation and deploying deception measures such as honeytokens and decoy systems. They must also adopt operational discipline to avoid being overwhelmed by the volume, intensity and duration of attacks from agentic offensive frameworks. Answering key questions about defensive readiness helps organisations develop a holistic approach to protection.

The agency emphasized that established cybersecurity practices, including zero-trust principles, least-privilege access and basic cyber hygiene, remain central to defending against agentic attacks, rather than relying solely on emerging AI-powered defensive capabilities.

The agency suggested that agents start by mapping every device and service that is reachable, so organisations need to know which systems and services are exposed to the internet and who is responsible for them, drawing on the NCSC’s Asset Management Guidance. Services successfully compromised by agentic offensive frameworks are sometimes those an organisation has forgotten about, such as old websites and legacy equipment. Organisations should conduct regular security reviews of known assets and use either internal skills or a consultancy to perform reconnaissance against their perimeter.

Rather than trying to harden all services individually, organisations should look at making services available only to authenticated users. This can be done by placing services behind an identity-aware proxy with strong phishing-resistant authentication following modern zero trust methods, using firewalls to restrict access unless traffic comes from the corporate VPN or internal ranges, or configuring mutual TLS certificates to require user authentication prior to interacting with a service.

In most organisations, most services currently accessible from the perimeter can be locked down quickly so they are only accessible by staff or partners, allowing specific hardening to focus on the remaining services that cannot be locked down. Assuming everything connected to the internet is vulnerable prompts a fundamental shift in how security is approached.

Agent activity tends to look different from human activity, showing bursts of requests followed by short thinking phases, a wide variety of endpoints and parameters (including garbage or example variants that would never work), and increased error rates. Defenders must correlate across log sources to detect these signals and collect and retain logs needed to detect, investigate, and recover from an incident, supported by the NCSC’s logging guidance. Like all threat actors, agents leave a trail, and appropriate logging helps responders understand which assets an agent interacted with and what actions it took to remediate. Unlike human threat actors, an AI agent reads every error it receives and uses that information to refine its next attempt, so error messages shown to external users should reveal as little as possible to reduce the efficacy of subsequent actions and give nothing away.

Agents can test known vulnerabilities and exploits across targets very quickly, meaning patching internet-facing systems needs to keep pace. The same applies to configuration, particularly identity and access (IAM) settings that quietly drift from original designs. To identify, prioritise and fix vulnerabilities within assets accessible from the internet, organisations can use the NCSC’s external attack surface management guidance alongside vulnerability management guidance. Companies assured under the NCSC’s penetration testing scheme (CHECK) can conduct penetration testing to uncover vulnerabilities and misconfigurations, while smaller organisations can refer to guidance on adversary simulation to assess whether it fits their needs.

Account and identity flows, inventory, checkout, and resource-intensive endpoints, such as search, report generation, or LLM-backed features, are high-priority assets that benefit from deploying bot challenges. These require human interaction to prevent automated tools from abusing services and can be set to low interaction (essentially invisible to human users) unless risk signals become elevated. When deploying bot challenges, organisations should provide an accessible alternative when a challenge is triggered.

Bot challenges are presented in the browser on pages such as login or submission forms, but if an underlying API does not enforce the challenge, an attacker can call the API directly and bypass it. Many APIs set limits to prevent large volumes of requests from specific IP addresses, yet agents routinely rotate through proxies and VPNs, making IP blocking often ineffective. Instead, defenders should apply rate limits to accounts, API tokens and authenticated sessions, evaluate behaviour as well as volume and consult NCSC’s API security guidance. Where an organisation publishes public code repositories, enabling pre-commit automation helps prevent secrets from being pushed, supported by guidance on keeping secrets out of the codebase and using a cloud platform securely.

If anyone can create an account in seconds, account-based controls, including rate limits, are easy to circumvent because agents can register new accounts as fast as defenders block them. Organisations should consider throttling account creation, with heavier throttling for sign-ups using disposable email services. Agents can also exploit default credentials and use tools to brute-force weak passwords, making strong authentication and appropriate access controls essential in line with the NCSC’s authentication guidance.

The traits that make agents effective also make them susceptible to deception. A honeytoken or tripwire placed somewhere no legitimate user should touch produces a high-confidence alert the moment it is accessed. An agent’s perseverance and exhaustive searching mean decoy systems, tarpits and fake successes can consume an agent’s time or attempt budget and keep it away from real assets. Microsoft has reported that deception measures reduced attack traffic reaching real systems during a recent campaign using deception strategies.

Earlier this month, the NCSC warned in its Annual Review 2026 that the U.K.’s critical national infrastructure should prepare for ransomware attacks involving artificial intelligence-powered malware by 2028. It also warned that hostile actors will likely increase their use of AI over the next two years to expand scale and efficiency of cyber operations, including ransomware attacks, social engineering, and vulnerability exploitation.

[78] Industrial Cyber - Cyfirma maps pharmaceutical cyber threats as hackers target drug research, vaccine development and manufacturing
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Wed, 25 Nov 2026 09:56:30 +0000
Link: https://industrialcyber.co/pharma/cyfirma-maps-pharmaceutical-cyber-threats-as-hackers-target-drug-research-vaccine-development-and-manufacturing/

[TAM METİN - 1381 kelime]
Cyfirma maps pharmaceutical cyber threats as hackers target drug research, vaccine development and manufacturing

New Cyfirma data reported that pharmaceutical and biotechnology organizations faced sustained cyber threats from nation-state espionage, intellectual property theft, ransomware and underground data exposure, with attackers targeting sensitive research, vaccine development, clinical information and manufacturing infrastructure. The report highlighted the October 2026 VAPOR PANDA campaign, in which attackers targeted two U.S.-based pharmaceutical organizations by exploiting cloud authentication weaknesses, deploying web shells and accessing production container environments. The campaign also involved ARTEX, an artificial intelligence (AI)-driven intrusion framework, alongside commercial language models to coordinate attack operations

The assessment, covering activity from April 1 to Nov. 17, identified operations involving China-, Russia- and North Korea-linked threat actors, alongside financially motivated cybercriminal groups. The research also recorded 96 ransomware-related victim entries across 31 countries, involving 38 ransomware groups, with North America, Europe and East Asia accounting for approximately 77.1% of the recorded cases. The U.S. had the highest country-level concentration, with 31 entries, followed by Taiwan and China with eight each, Italy and Germany with five each, and India with four. N0n accounted for the largest number of recorded victim entries at 18, followed by TheGentlemen with 13 and Qilin with 10.

Cyfirma also identified underground listings involving alleged theft of pharmaceutical source code, clinical research data, biological information, cloud credentials, distributor records and confidential corporate information, although the authenticity and extent of individual exposures remain subject to validation. The findings highlight risks extending beyond individual pharmaceutical companies to research partners, contract manufacturers, active pharmaceutical ingredient suppliers, technology providers and distribution networks.

The post also assessed dark web exposure, finding underground listings that claimed to contain pharmaceutical source code, research data, clinical records, cloud credentials, and confidential business information. Observed operations relied on multiple attack methods, including phishing, credential theft, vulnerability exploitation, web shells, and unauthorized access to cloud environments. It also warned of supply-chain risks, noting that research partners, contract manufacturers, API suppliers, and distribution networks create exposure well beyond individual organizations. To reduce sector-wide cyber risk, protecting pharmaceutical research, intellectual property, cloud infrastructure, and manufacturing operations remains essential.

Pharmaceutical organizations continue to face persistent threats from state-sponsored espionage, intellectual property theft, underground data exposure, and ransomware. Safeguarding sensitive research while maintaining secure, resilient operations must remain top priorities.

Cyfirma identified the pharmaceutical industry as an attractive target for nation-state cyber espionage because of its strategic scientific, economic and public health importance. Pharmaceutical companies hold valuable intellectual property, proprietary formulations, patents, manufacturing expertise and research data that can provide competing states with technological and economic advantages.

State-sponsored actors may target drug development pipelines, unpublished research, vaccine development, advanced biotechnology and manufacturing know-how to accelerate scientific capabilities and gain access to emerging treatments. They may also seek commercial strategies, licensing information and intelligence on active pharmaceutical ingredient suppliers, manufacturing dependencies and critical medicine distribution networks, making pharmaceutical organizations valuable sources of industrial and strategic intelligence.

The researchers highlighted that pharmaceutical supply chains create cyber exposure beyond individual organizations because of reliance on interconnected research partners, contract manufacturers, active pharmaceutical ingredient (API) suppliers, technology providers and distribution networks. Contract Research Organisations (CROs) and biotechnology partners may hold confidential research and clinical data, while Contract Development and Manufacturing Organisations (CDMOs) and API suppliers maintain sensitive production information and manufacturing processes.

External IT and cloud providers may have access to business applications and research environments, while wholesalers and logistics partners handle commercially sensitive orders, inventory and supplier information. Connected operational technology (OT) also introduces risks to manufacturing availability and process integrity. A compromise of a critical third party could therefore expose multiple organizations or disrupt interconnected pharmaceutical operations.

The Organisation for Economic Co-operation and Development (OECD) highlighted supply-chain dependencies and limited visibility into some upstream pharmaceutical suppliers, while the U.S. Food and Drug Administration (FDA) identified cybersecurity risks associated with connected technologies used in pharmaceutical manufacturing.

Nation-state-linked threat actors have shown sustained interest in pharmaceutical, biotechnology and medical research organisations, mainly seeking intellectual property, scientific developments and commercially sensitive information through espionage, credential compromise, research-data theft and unauthorised access to corporate and production environments.

China-linked groups account for the longest list. APT41, active since at least 2012 and known for mixing state-sponsored espionage with financially motivated operations, has stolen pharmaceutical development information, clinical-trial data, academic research and confidential corporate documents, and one intrusion at a biotechnology company also exposed research funding and acquisition details. APT27 has stolen trade secrets from pharmaceutical and technology companies in Germany and has used access inside compromised organisations to launch spear-phishing against other targets.

APT10, associated with China’s Ministry of State Security, exploits trusted IT service relationships to reach further organisations and was accused in 2021 of targeting India’s biotechnology firms for vaccine-related intellectual property, though those allegations remain unconfirmed. APT9, whose state affiliation is uncertain but may be Chinese, has gone after proprietary research and development projects and is suspected of exploiting trust between biotechnology organisations.

The most recent China-nexus case is VAPOR PANDA, which CrowdStrike reported in October, targeted two U.S.-based pharmaceutical organisations during October through cloud authentication weaknesses, web shells and unauthorised access to production container environments, using the AI-driven ARTEX framework alongside commercial language models to coordinate its operations.

Russian activity has centred on vaccine research and scientific intelligence. APT29, linked to the Foreign Intelligence Service (SVR), targeted COVID-19 vaccine developers in the U.S., U.K., and Canada to obtain research and intellectual property. APT28, linked to the military intelligence agency GRU, was associated with password-spraying attempts against pharmaceutical companies and vaccine researchers in the U.S., Canada, France, India and South Korea, a reminder that research personnel and their accounts are targets in their own right. Turla, attributed to the Federal Security Service (FSB) and active since at least 2004, lists pharmaceutical companies among its documented target sectors, although public intelligence offers little incident-level detail, so its relevance rests chiefly on its established interest in research-intensive organisations and the case for continued monitoring.

North Korean actors have shown interest in pharmaceutical research and development. Andariel, associated with the Reconnaissance General Bureau, has been linked to the exfiltration of pharmaceutical R&D information alongside weapons-related research. Operation DreamJob, a cluster under the broader Lazarus umbrella, was reported by ESET in March 2026, shortly before the assessment period, to have targeted the South Korean pharmaceutical sector with employment-themed lures, weaponised MFC applications and a RAR archive exploiting CVE-2025-8088 and CVE-2025-6218, with BlindingCan as the cluster’s current main remote-access trojan.

Taken together, the identified actors show varying levels of interest, but the main concerns remain consistent, covering proprietary research acquisition, unauthorised access to sensitive corporate environments and exploitation of trusted relationships to gain further access. Pharmaceutical-sector threats should therefore be assessed both by the value of the information targeted and by the potential for sustained access to critical research and operational environments.

Cyfirma called upon pharmaceutical organisations to adopt a risk-based security approach focused on protecting proprietary research, restricting unauthorised access and maintaining the resilience of critical business and manufacturing operations. A first step is to protect research and intellectual property by implementing strict access controls across R&D repositories, clinical-trial databases and proprietary research environments, while monitoring bulk data access, unusual downloads and unauthorised external transfers to detect potential theft. Identity and cloud infrastructure need equal attention: organisations should enforce phishing-resistant multi-factor authentication (MFA), privileged access controls and regular credential rotation, strengthen authentication, protect cloud secrets and watch for suspicious administrative activity, particularly across cloud and container environments.

Exposure to exploitable vulnerabilities should be reduced by maintaining continuous visibility of internet-facing assets and prioritising remediation of actively exploited flaws, with timely patching of vulnerable applications, archive utilities, remote-access services and exposed enterprise infrastructure. Third-party and manufacturing security matters as well. External supplier and contractor access should be restricted to approved systems through monitored, time-limited connections, and manufacturing operational technology (OT) should be separated from corporate networks, with access to production-critical systems monitored.

Preparedness for ransomware means maintaining immutable, offline backups of research, clinical and manufacturing data, regularly testing recovery procedures and establishing incident-response plans that address both data exfiltration and system encryption. Finally, organisations should monitor Dark Web marketplaces, ransomware leak sites and underground forums for exposed credentials, corporate access, research information and pharmaceutical data, validate relevant claims against internal telemetry and open investigations wherever potential exposure is identified.
